Spatial populations with seed-bank: renormalisation on the hierarchical group
Andreas Greven, Frank den Hollander, Margriet Oomen

Abstract
We consider a system of interacting diffusions labeled by a geographic space that is given by the hierarchical group Ω N \Omega_{N} of order N ∈ ℕ N\in\mathbb{N} . Individuals live in colonies and are subject to resampling and migration as long as they are active . Each colony has a seed-bank into which individuals can retreat to become dormant , suspending their resampling and migration until they become active again. The migration kernel has a hierarchical structure : individuals hop between colonies at a rate that depends on the hierarchical distance between the colonies. The seed-bank has a layered structure : when individuals become dormant they acquire a colour that determines the rate at which they become active again. The latter allows us to model seed-banks whose wake-up times have a fat tail. We analyse a system of coupled stochastic differential equations that describes the population in the large-colony-size limit. For fixed N ∈ ℕ N\in\mathbb{N} , the system exhibits a dichotomy between coexistence (= locally multi-type equilibria) and clustering (= locally mono-type equilibria). To identify the clustering regime , i.e., the range of parameters controlling the migration and the seed-bank for which clustering prevails, we apply a necessary and sufficient criterion derived in [ GHO20 ] that is valid for any geographic space given by a countable Abelian group endowed with the discrete topology. The diffusion function controlling the resampling does not play a role in this criterion. We carry out a multi-scale renormalisation analysis in the hierarchical mean-field limit N → ∞ N\to\infty . In particular, we show that block averages on hierarchical space-time scale k ∈ ℕ k\in\mathbb{N} perform a diffusion with a renormalised diffusion function that depends on k k . In the clustering regime, after an appropriate scaling with k k , this diffusion function converges to the Fisher-Wright diffusion function as k → ∞ k\to\infty , irrespective of the diffusion function controlling the resampling. Thus, the system exhibits full universality on large space-time scales in terms of the scaling limit. For several subclasses of parameters we identify the speed at which the scaled renormalised diffusion function converges to the Fisher-Wright diffusion as k → ∞ k\to\infty . This speed in turn determines the speed at which mono-type clusters grow in space and time . We show that the seed-bank reduces the speed compared to the model without seed-bank. This reduction is the result of a delicate interplay between migration and seed-bank, which in the limit as N → ∞ N\to\infty can be worked out explicitly. Keywords: Fisher-Wright diffusion, resampling, migration, seed-bank, multi-scale renormalisation, universality. MSC 2010: Primary 60J70, 60K35; Secondary 92D25. Acknowledgements: AG was supported by the Deutsche Forschungsgemeinschaft (grant DFG-GR 876/6-1,2). FdH and MO were supported by the Netherlands Organisation for Scientific Research (NWO Gravitation Grant NETWORKS-024.002.003). FdH visited Bonn and Erlangen in the Fall of 2019 and 2020, supported by a Research Award of the Alexander von Humboldt Foundation. AG visited Leiden in February 2020, as Kloosterman Chair.

\@glotype@symbols@title

1
Background, goals and outline

1.1
Background

Single colony with seed-bank.

In populations with a
seed-bank
, individuals can temporarily become dormant and refrain from reproduction, until they can become active again. In
[
BGCEK15
]
and
[
BGCKWB16
]
the evolution of a population evolving according to the two-type Fisher-Wright model with seed-bank was studied. Individuals move in and out of the seed-bank at prescribed rates. Outside the seed-bank individuals are subject to
resampling
, while inside the seed-bank their resampling is
suspended
. Both the long-time behaviour and the genealogy of the population were analysed in detail.

Seed-banks are observed in many taxa, including plants, bacteria and other micro-organisms. Typically, they arise as a response to unfavourable environmental conditions. The dormant state of an individual is characterised by low metabolic activity and interruption of phenotypic development (see e.g.
[
LJ11
]
). After a varying and possibly large number of generations, a dormant individual can be resuscitated under more favourable conditions and reprise reproduction after having become active again. This strategy is known to have important implications for population persistence, maintenance of genetic variability and stability of ecosystems. It acts as a
buffer
against evolutionary forces such as genetic drift, selection and environmental variability.

Multiple colonies with seed-bank.

In
[
GHO20
]
we considered a
spatial
version of the two-type Fisher-Wright model with seed-bank in which individuals can
migrate
between colonies, organised into a
geographic space
, each having a seed-bank consisting of
multiple layers
, each with their own rate of moving in (becoming dormant) and moving out (waking up). We found that the presence of the seed-bank
enhances genetic diversity
compared to the spatial model without seed-bank. Interestingly, we found that the seed-bank can affect the longtime behaviour of the system both qualitatively and quantitatively.

In
[
GHO20
]
we settled existence and uniqueness of the spatial model when the geographic space is
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
,
d\in\mathbb{N}
. We proved convergence to equilibrium, showed that there is a dichotomy between
coexistence
(= locally multi-type equilibria) and
clustering
(= locally mono-type equilibria), and identified the parameter regime for both. We found a change of the dichotomy due to the presence of the seed-bank. Without seed-bank, for migration in the domain of attraction of Brownian motion, clustering occurs in
d=1,2
and coexistence in
d\geq 3
, i.e., the
critical dimension
for the dichotomy is
d=2
. With seed-bank, however, clustering becomes more difficult and occurs in
d=2
only when the wake-up time of a typical individual in the seed-bank has finite mean, and in
d=1
only when the wake-up time has a sufficiently thin tail. In other words, the seed-bank has a tendency to
lower
the critical dimension.

In fact, in
[
GHO20
]
we found that our technique of proof works for geographic spaces that are
arbitrary
countable Abelian groups endowed with the discrete topology. The reason is that the dichotomy can be formulated in terms of how the
degree
of the random walk that underlies the migration balances with the exponent of the
tail
of the typical wake-up time. This raises the question how we can better understand the behaviour of spatial models with seed-bank close to criticality.

In
[
GHO21
]
we established the so-called
finite-systems scheme
, i.e., we identified how a finite truncation of the system (both in the geographic space and in the seed-bank) behaves as both the time and the truncation level tend to infinity, properly tuned together. We found that if the wake-up time has finite mean, then the scaling time is proportional to the volume of the system and there is a
single universality class
for the scaling limit, namely, the system moves through a succession of equilibria of the infinite system with a density that evolves according to a Fisher-Wright diffusion. On the other hand, we found that if the wake-up time has infinite mean, then the scaling time grows faster than the volume of the system, and there are
two universality classes
depending on how fast the truncation level of the seed-bank grows compared to the truncation level of the geographic space.

1.2
Goals

In the present paper we take as geographic space the
hierarchical group
\Omega_{N}
of order
N
. The reason for this choice is that
\Omega_{N}
allows for more detailed computations. At the same time, migration on
\Omega_{N}
can be used to
approximate
migration on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
in the
hierarchical mean-field limit

N\to\infty
. In particular, by playing with the migration kernel we can approximate two-dimensional migration in the sense of potential theory. We consider migration kernels that in the limit as
N\to\infty
are
critically recurrent
, i.e., the degree of the class of hierarchical migrations that we consider in the present paper converges to
0
,
either from above or from below.

The present paper has three goals:

(1)

We apply the results obtained in
[
GHO20
]
to
\Omega_{N}
with
N<\infty
fixed. We again find that part of the coexistence regime without seed-bank shifts into the clustering regime with seed-bank when the average wake-up time of a typical individual is infinite.

(2)

We analyse a
space-time renormalised
system in the limit as
N\to\infty
. Namely, we show that the block averages on successive space-time scales each perform a diffusion with a
renormalised diffusion function
. In other words, we establish a
multi-scale version of the finite-systems scheme
. Also, we compare the behaviour of the space-time renormalised system with seed-bank to the one analysed in
[
DG93b
]
and
[
DG93a
]
without seed-bank.

(3)

We exhibit
universal behaviour
in the clustering regime close to criticality. To do so, we analyse the attracting orbits of the renormalisation transformation, acting on the space of diffusion functions, that connects successive hierarchical levels. We show that, in the
clustering regime
and after appropriate scaling, the renormalised diffusion function converges to the Fisher-Wright diffusion function as we move up in the hierarchy, irrespective of the diffusion function controlling the resampling. This convergence shows that the hierarchical system exhibits
universality
on large space-time scales in terms of the scaling limit. For several subclasses of parameters we identify the scaling of the renormalised diffusion function, which reveals a delicate interplay between the parameters controlling the migration and the seed-bank. This rate in turn determines the speed at which
mono-type clusters grow in space and time
.

In the
coexistence regime
, universality does
not
hold and the equilibrium depends on the diffusion function. Since the seed-bank enhances genetic diversity, it may be expected that equilibrium correlations between far away colonies decay faster with seed-bank than without seed-bank, an issue that will not be addressed.

Remark 1.1
.

[More general types]

Throughout the paper we consider the
two-type
Fisher-Wright model with seed-bank, in the
continuum limit
where the number of individuals per colony tends to infinity. The extension to a general type space, called the Fleming-Viot model (see
[
DGV95
]
), requires only standard adaptations and will not be considered here. In what follows, we
only
work with continuum models. However, we motivate these models by viewing them as the
large-colony-size limit
of individual-based models. For earlier work on hierarchically interacting Fisher-Wright diffusions without seed-bank we refer the reader to
[
DG93a
,
DG93b
,
DGV95
,
DG96
]
and
[
BCGdH95
,
BCGdH97
,
dH01
]
.

\blacksquare

1.3
Outline

The present paper consist of two parts:

•

Part I: Model and main results.

Sections
2
–
5
collect the main propositions and theorems. In Section
2
we define the hierarchical model and state some basic properties: the
well-posedness
of the associated martingale problem (Proposition
2.6
), the
duality relation
(Proposition
2.7
), and the
clustering criterion
via duality (Proposition
2.12
). These properties were all derived in
[
GHO20
]
. In Section
3
we state our main results for
N<\infty
. In particular, we compute the scaling of the wake-up time and the migration kernel (Theorem
3.2
) and identify the
clustering regime
in terms of the coefficients controlling the migration and the seed-bank under the assumption that these are asymptotically polynomial or pure exponential (Theorem
3.3
)). In Section
4
we state our main results for
N\to\infty
, the hierarchical mean-field limit. In particular, we introduce
block averages on successive hierarchical space-time scales
, analyse their limiting dynamics (Theorems
4.2
and
4.4
), offer a heuristic explanation how this limiting dynamics arises, introduce a path topology called the Meyer-Zheng topology that is needed for a proper formulation, and introduce an object called the interaction chain, which describes how the diffent hierarchical levels interact with each other. In Section
5
we identify the
orbit of the renormalisation transformation
in the clustering regime (Theorem
5.1
), identify the rate of scaling for the renormalised diffusion function (Theorem
5.3
), and link this scaling to the rate of growth of mono-type clusters.

•

Part II: Preparations and proofs.

Sections
6
–
13
provide the proofs of the theorems stated in Part I. These proofs consist of a long series of propositions and lemmas needed to build up the argument. In Section
6
we prove our main results for
N<\infty
. In Sections
7
–
9
we focus on the
mean-field model
(consisting of a single hierarchy) and, respectively, state and prove a number of results that serve as preparation. In Sections
10
–
12
we consider extensions of the mean-field model (consisting of finitely hierarchies), which serve as further preparation. In Section
12
we use the results in Sections
7
–
12
to deal with the full hierarchical model (consisting of infinitely many hierarchies), and prove our main results for
N\to\infty
. In Section
13
we analyse the orbit of the renormalisation transformation controlling the multi-scaling. Appendix
A
contains a technical computation needed for the identification of the clustering regime. Appendix
B
contains a basic introduction to convergence of paths in the Meyer-Zheng topology, which is needed for the main theorems.

Part I contains all the main results and their interpretations, and can be read without reference to Part II.

Part I
MODEL AND MAIN RESULTS

2
Introduction of model and basic properties

Section
2.1
introduces the model ingredients, Section
2.2
gives the evolution equations, Section
2.3
states the well-posedness, Section
2.4
introduces the dual and states the duality relation, while Section
2.5
formulates the dichotomy between clustering versus coexistence in terms of the dual.

2.1
Model: geographic space
\Omega_{N}
, hierachical group of order
N

Single colony.

Our building block is the single-colony Fisher-Wright model with seed-bank defined in
[
BGCKWB16
]
. In that model, each individual in the population carries one of two types,
\heartsuit
or
\diamondsuit
, and each individual can be either active or dormant. Active individuals
resample
until they become dormant. Dormant individuals suspend resampling until they become actieve again. The repository for the dormant individuals is called the
seed-bank
. When an active individual resamples, it randomly chooses another active individual and
adopts its type
. When an active individual becomes dormant, it randomly chooses a dormant individual and forces it to becomes active, i.e., the active and the dormant population
exchange
individuals (see Fig.
1
). This exchange guarantees that the sizes of the active and the dormant population stay fixed over time. During the swap both the active and the dormant individual
retain their type
.

The types of the active population evolve through resampling and through exchange with the dormant population. The types of the the dormant population evolve only through exchange with the active population. It was shown in
[
BGCKWB16
]
that in the large-colony-size limit, i.e., as the number of individuals per colony tends to infinity and time is speeded up by the size of the colony, the two quantities

•

x(t)
= the fraction of active individuals of type
\heartsuit
at time
t
,

•

y(t)
= the fraction of dormant individuals of type
\heartsuit
at time
t
,

satisfy the following system of coupled SDEs:

(2.1)

\displaystyle{\rm d}x(t)

\displaystyle=Ke\,[y(t)-x(t)]\,{\rm d}t+\sqrt{x(t)(1-x(t))}\,{\rm d}w(t),

\displaystyle{\rm d}y(t)

\displaystyle=e\,[x(t)-y(t)]\,{\rm d}t.

Here,
e
denotes the rate at which an active individual
exchanges
with a dormant individual from the seed-bank,
K
denotes the relative size of the dormant population with respect to the active population, and
(w(t))_{t\geq 0}
is a Brownian motion. The first term in the first equation describes the flow from the dormant population to the active population, the term in the second equation describes the flow from the active population to the dormant population, while the second term in the first equation describes the effect of resampling on the active population (see Fig.
1
). Active individuals resample at rate
1
. Since dormant individuals do not resample, we do not see such a term in the second equation. The formal derivation of the continuum equations can be found in
[
BGCKWB16
]
and in
[
GHO20
, Appendix A]
.

A
D
exchange
resampling
Ke
e
1

Figure 1:
Active individuals resample at rate
1
. Active and dormant individuals exchange at rate
e
. The extra factor
K
arises from the fact that the dormant population is
K
times as large as the active population. Dormant individuals suspend resampling.

Multi-colony.

The present paper focuses on a
multi-colony
setting of the model described above, where the underlying geographic space is the hierarchical lattice of order
N
, given by (
\mathbb{N}_{0}=\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
)

(2.2)

??=\left\{\xi=(\xi_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\colon\,\xi_{k}\in\{0,1,\ldots,N-1\},\ \sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\xi_{k}<\infty\right\},

which with addition modulo
N
becomes the hierarchical group of order
N
(see Fig.
2
). The
hierarchical distance
on
\Omega_{N}
is defined by

(2.3)

??(\xi,\eta)=d_{\Omega_{N}}(0,\xi-\eta)=\min\left\{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\colon\,\xi_{l}=\eta_{l}\ \forall\,l\geq k\right\},\qquad\xi,\eta\in\Omega_{N},

and is an ultra-metric, i.e.,

(2.4)

d_{\Omega_{N}}(\xi,\eta)\leq\max\big\{d_{\Omega_{N}}(\xi,\zeta),d_{\Omega_{N}}(\eta,\zeta)\big\}\qquad\forall\,\xi,\eta,\zeta\in\Omega_{N}.

The choice of
\Omega_{N}
as geographic space plays an important role for population models, and was first exploited in
[
SF83
]
in an attempt to formalise ideas coming from ecology. One interpretation is that the sequence
(\xi_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
encodes the ‘address’ of colony
\xi
:
\xi_{0}
is the ‘house’,
\xi_{1}
is the ‘street’,
\xi_{2}
is the ‘village’,
\xi_{3}
is the ‘province’,
\xi_{4}
is the ‘country’, and so on. To describe the system on the hierarchical group we need three ingredients:

1.

Hierarchical migration (Section
2.1.1
).

2.

Layered seed-bank (Section
2.1.2
).

3.

Resampling rate (Section
2.1.3
).

Figure 2:
Close-ups of a 1-block, a 2-block and a 3-block in the hierarchical group
of order
N=3
. The elements of the group are the leaves of the tree (indicated by
\Box
’s).
The hierarchical distance between two elements in the group is the graph distance to the most
recent common ancestor in the tree:
d_{\Omega_{3}}(\eta,\zeta)=2
for
\eta
and
\zeta

in the picture.

2.1.1
Hierarchical migration

We construct a migration kernel
a^{\Omega_{N}}(\cdot,\cdot)
on the hierarchical group
\Omega_{N}
built from a sequence of migration rates

(2.5)

??=(??)_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\in(0,\infty)^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}

that do not depend on
N
. Individuals migrate as follows:

•

For all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, each individual chooses at rate
c_{k-1}/N^{k-1}
the block of radius
k
around its present location and selects a colony uniformly at random from that block. Subsequently it selects an individual in this colony uniformly at random and adopts its type.

Note that the block of radius
k
contains
N^{k}
colonies, and that the migration kernel ??is therefore given by

(2.6)

a^{\Omega_{N}}(\eta,\xi)=\sum\limits_{k\geq d_{\Omega_{N}}(\eta,\xi)}\frac{c_{k-1}}{N^{k-1}}\frac{1}{N^{k}},\quad\eta,\xi\in\Omega_{N},\eta\neq\xi,\qquad a^{\Omega_{N}}(\eta,\eta)=0,\quad\eta\in\Omega_{N}.

Throughout the paper, we assume that

(2.7)

\limsup_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{k}\,\log c_{k}<\log N.

This guarantees that the total migration rate per individual is finite. Indeed, note that for every
\eta\in\Omega_{N}
,

(2.8)

{}\sum_{\xi\in\Omega_{N}}a^{\Omega_{N}}(\eta,\xi)=\sum_{\xi\in\Omega_{N}}\sum_{k\geq d_{\Omega_{N}}(\eta,\xi)}\frac{c_{k-1}}{N^{2k-1}}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\left[\sum_{\xi\in\Omega_{N}}\textbf{1}_{\{d_{\Omega_{N}}(\eta,\xi)\leq k\}}\right]\frac{c_{k-1}}{N^{2k-1}}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{c_{k-1}}{N^{k-1}},

which is finite because of (
2.7
).

Remark 2.1
.

[Degree of random walk]

For a random walk on an Abelian group with time-
t
transition kernel ??, the
degree
is defined as (see
[
DGW05
]
)

(2.9)

??=\sup\left\{\zeta\in(-1,\infty)\colon\,\int^{\infty}_{0}{\rm d}t\,t^{\zeta}a^{\Omega_{N}}_{t}(0,0)<\infty\right\}.

The degree is said to be
\delta^{+}
, respectively,
\delta^{-}
when the integral is finite, respectively, infinite
at
the degree. If
\delta>0
, then
\delta
is called the
degree of transience
. If
\delta\in(-1,0)
, then
-\delta
is called the
degree of recurrence
. If the degree is
0^{-}
, then the random walk is called
critically recurrent
. (It would be interesting to have a version of (
2.9
) that includes a slowly varying function in front of the power
t^{\zeta}
. However, such an extension appears not to have been explored in the literature.)

\blacksquare

By playing with
\underline{c}
and letting
N\to\infty
, we can approximate migration for which the corresponding random walk is critically recurrent, i.e.,
\delta^{-}=0
. In that case both the potential theory and the Green function for the hierarchical random walk have the same asymptotics as the potential theory and the Green function for a critically recurrent random walk on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
in the domain of attraction of Brownian motion. Therefore, by tuning
\underline{c}
properly, we can mimic migration on the geographic space
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
(for which
\delta^{-}=0
), an idea that was exploited in
[
DGV95
]
,
[
DG96
]
,
[
DG14
]
,
[
GdHKK14
]
,
[
GdHK18
]
.

2.1.2
Layered seed-bank

To create a layered seed-bank, dormant individuals are labeled with a colour
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. An active individual that becomes dormant is assigned a colour
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. When an active individual becomes dormant with colour
m
, it exchanges with a dormant individual of colour
m
. This dormant individual becomes active,
loses
its colour, but
retains
its type. To describe the layered seed-bank we need two sequences

(2.10)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle??=(K_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\in(0,\infty)^{\mathbb{N}_{0}},

\displaystyle??=(??)_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\in(0,\infty)^{\mathbb{N}_{0}},

both not depending on
N
, which we interpret as follows:

•

??is the relative size of the dormant population of colour
m
with respect to the active
population, i.e.,

(2.11)

K_{m}=\frac{\text{size $m$-dormant population}}{\text{size active population}}.

•

At rate
K_{m}\frac{e_{m}}{N^{m}}
an active individual becomes dormant, is assigned colour
m
, and retains its type. At the same time a dormant individual with colour
m
becomes active, loses its colour, and retains its type. By defining the rates in this way, the layered structure of the seed-bank is tuned to the hierarchical structure of the geographic space.

By giving the seed-bank a layered structure, we are able to tune the distribution of the wake-up time, i.e., the time an individual spends in the seed-bank before waking up. In particular, we will see that a layered seed-bank enables us to model wake up times with a
fat tail
, while at the same time preserving the Markov property of the evolution.

Since active and dormant individuals
exchange
,
K_{m}
remains constant over time for all
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Throughout the paper we assume that

(2.12)

\limsup_{m\to\infty}\frac{1}{m}\,\log(K_{m}e_{m})<\log N.

This guarantees that the total rate of exchange per individual, given by

(2.13)

??=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}K_{m}\frac{e_{m}}{N^{m}},

is finite. On the other hand, the relative size of the dormant population with respect to the active population

(2.14)

??=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}K_{m}

can be either finite or infinite. We will see that
\rho<\infty
and
\rho=\infty
represent two
different regimes
.

2.1.3
Resampling rate

To describe the resampling we use a diffusion function ??that is taken from the set

(2.15)

??=\Big\{g(x)\colon\,[0,1]\to[0,\infty)\colon\,g(0)=g(1)=0,\,g(x)>0\,\,\forall\,x\in(0,1),\,g\mbox{ Lipschitz}\Big\},

and think of
h(x)=g(x)/x(1-x)
as the rate of resampling at type frequency
x
. The choice
g=dg_{\mathrm{FW}}
,
d\in(0,\infty)
, with
g_{\mathrm{FW}}(x)=x(1-x)
,
x\in[0,1]
, corresponds to Fisher-Wright resampling at rate
d
. We use a collection of independent Brownian motions

(2.16)

W=\big(??\big)_{\xi\in\Omega_{N}}

to describe the fluctuations of the type frequencies caused by the resampling in each colony.

2.2
Evolution equations

2.2.1
Evolution of single colonies

With the above three ingredients, we can now describe the evolution of the system. For
\xi\in\Omega_{N}
, define

(2.17)

\displaystyle??

\displaystyle=\text{ the fraction of active individuals of type $\heartsuit$ at colony $\xi$ at time $t$},

\displaystyle??(t)

\displaystyle=\text{ the fraction of $m$-dormant individuals of type $\heartsuit$ at colony $\xi$ and time $t$}.

Note that
x_{\xi}(t)\in[0,1]
and
y_{\xi,m}(t)\in[0,1]
for all
\xi\in\Omega_{N}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
t\geq 0
. Therefore the state space of a single colony is
\mathfrak{s}=[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
, and the state space of the system is

(2.18)

??=\mathfrak{s}^{\Omega_{N}}.

Our object of interest is the random process taking values in
S
, written

(2.19)

??=??,\qquad(X^{\Omega_{N}}(t),Y^{\Omega_{N}}(t))=\big(x_{\xi}(t),(y_{\xi,m}(t))_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\big)_{\xi\in\Omega_{N}},

whose components evolve according to the following SSDE (= system of stochastic differential equations):

(2.20)

\displaystyle{\rm d}x_{\xi}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\eta\in\Omega_{N}}a^{\Omega_{N}}(\xi,\eta)[x_{\eta}(t)-x_{\xi}(t)]\,{\rm d}t+\sqrt{g(x_{\xi}(t))}\,{\rm d}w_{\xi}(t)

\displaystyle+\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\frac{K_{m}e_{m}}{N^{m}}\,[y_{\xi,m}(t)-x_{\xi}(t)]\,{\rm d}t,

\displaystyle{\rm d}y_{\xi,m}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{e_{m}}{N^{m}}\,[x_{\xi}(t)-y_{\xi,m}(t)]\,{\rm d}t,\quad m\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\qquad\xi\in\Omega_{N}.

The first term in the first equation describes the evolution of the active population at colony
\xi
due to migration, the second term due to the resampling. The third term in the first equation and the term in the second equation describe the exchange between the active and the dormant population at colony
\xi
(see Fig.
3
). Since dormant individuals are not subject to resampling or migration, the dynamics of the dormant population is completely determined by the exchange with the active population. For the
initial state
we assume that

(2.21)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(X^{\Omega_{N}}(0),Y^{\Omega_{N}}(0))=\mu^{\otimes\Omega_{N}}

\displaystyle\text{with }\mathbb{E}^{\mu}[x_{\xi}(0)]=??,\,\mathbb{E}^{\mu}[y_{\xi,m}(0)]=??\text{ with }\lim_{m\to\infty}\theta_{y_{m}}=\theta\text{ for some }\theta\in[0,1].

The last assumption in (
2.21
), which in
[
GHO20
]
was referred to as
\mu
being
colour regular
, guarantees that for finite
N
the system in (
2.20
) converges to an ergodic equilibrium.

Remark 2.2
.

[Notation]

Throughout the sequel we use lower case letters for
single components
and upper case letters for
systems of single components
. We exhibit the geographic space for the system, but suppress it from the components.

\blacksquare

Active individuals (

) are subject to migration, resampling and exchange
with dormant individuals (

). When active individuals become dormant they are assigned a

,

), which they lose when they become active again. The resampling rate

x
equals
h(x)=g(x)/x(1-x)
with
g\in\mathcal{G}
(e.g. for the
standard Fisher-Wright diffusion the resampling rate is
1
).

2.2.2
Evolution of block averages

The choice of the migration kernel in (
2.6
) implies that, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, at rate
\asymp\frac{1}{N^{k}}
individuals choose a space horizon of distance
k+1
and subsequently choose a random colony from that space horizon. Therefore, in order to see interactions over a distance
k+1
, we need to speed up time by a factor
N^{k}
. A similar observation applies to the interaction with the seed-bank. Dormant individuals with colour
k
become active at rate
\asymp\frac{1}{N^{k}}
. Therefore, in order to see interactions with the
k
-dormant population, we need to speed up time by a factor
N^{k}
. To analyse the effective interaction on time scale
N^{k}
, we introduce
successive block averages
labelled by
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

Definition 2.3
.

[Block averages]

For
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, let
B_{k}(0)=\{\eta\in\Omega_{N}\colon\,d_{\Omega_{N}}(0,\eta\leq k\}
denote the
k
-block around
0
. Define the
k
-block average around
0
at time
N^{k}t
by

(2.22)

\displaystyle??

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{k}}\sum_{\eta\in B_{k}(0)}x_{\eta}(N^{k}t),

\displaystyle??

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{k}}\sum_{\eta\in B_{k}(0)}y_{\eta,m}(N^{k}t),\qquad m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

The
k
-block average represents the dynamics of the system on space-time scale
k
.

\blacksquare

By translation invariance of the SSDE in (
2.20
), each
\xi\in\Omega_{N}
can serve as the origin. In the remainder of the paper we consider without loss of generality the
k
-block average around
\xi=0
, and suppress the center
0
from the notation.

Remark 2.4
.

[Notation]

We use lower case letters for the block averages because they live in the space of components
\mathfrak{s}=[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
. At the same time we exhibit the geographic space
\Omega_{N}
for the block averages because they are functionals of the system of components (recall Remark
2.2
).

\blacksquare

Using Definition
2.3
and inserting the specific choice of the migration kernel defined in (
2.6
), we can rewrite (
2.20
) for
\xi=0
as follows (
0
-blocks are single components):

(2.23)

\displaystyle{\rm d}x_{0}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{c_{l-1}}{N^{l-1}}\big[x_{l}^{\Omega_{N}}(N^{-l}t)-x_{0}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)\big]\,{\rm d}t+\sqrt{g\big(x_{0}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)\big)}\,{\rm d}w(t)

\displaystyle+\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\frac{K_{m}e_{m}}{N^{m}}\big[y_{m,0}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)-x_{0}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)\big]\,{\rm d}t,

\displaystyle{\rm d}y_{m,0}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{e_{m}}{N^{m}}\big[x_{0}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)-y_{m,0}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)\big]\,{\rm d}t,\qquad m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

From (
2.23
) we see that migration between colonies can be expressed as a drift towards block averages at a higher hierarchical level.

The SSDE for the
k
-block average on time scale
N^{k}
reads as follows (recall (
2.22
)):

(2.24)

\displaystyle{\rm d}{x}_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{c_{k+l-1}}{N^{l-1}}\big[{x}_{k+l}^{\Omega_{N}}(N^{-l}t)-{x}_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)\big]\,{\rm d}t+\sqrt{\frac{1}{N^{k}}\sum_{i\in B_{k}(0)}g\big(x_{i}(N^{k}t)\big)}\,\,{\rm d}w_{k}(t)

\displaystyle+\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}N^{k}\frac{K_{m}e_{m}}{N^{m}}\big[{y}_{m,k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)-{x}_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)\big]\,{\rm d}t,

\displaystyle{\rm d}{y}_{m,k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)

\displaystyle=N^{k}\frac{e_{m}}{N^{m}}\big[{x}_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)-{y}_{m,k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)\big]\,{\rm d}t,\qquad m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

To deduce these equations from (
2.20
), we sum over
\xi\in B_{k}
, speed up time by a factor
N^{k}
, insert the specific choice of the migration kernel in (
2.6
), and use the standard scaling properties of Brownian motion:
w(ct)=^{d}\sqrt{c}\,w(t)
and
\sqrt{a}w(t)+\sqrt{b}w^{\prime}(t)=^{d}\sqrt{a+b}\,w^{\prime\prime}(t)
, with
w(t)
and
w^{\prime}(t)
independent Brownian motions, and with
=^{d}
denoting equality in distribution. This computation is spelled out in Section
12
.

Remark 2.5
.

The
block averages
and their evolution equations in (
2.24
) will be key objects in the analysis of the hierarchical mean-field limit
N\to\infty
. We will see that the limit
N\to\infty
brings about considerable simplifications. In Section
4
we discuss these simplifications in detail. In particular, a
complete separation of space-time scales
takes places, in which each block average lives on its own time scale, effectively interacts with only one seed-bank, and effectively feels a drift towards the block average one hierarchical level up.

\blacksquare

2.3
Well-posedness

Let ??denote the space of continuous function form
[0,\infty)
to the state space
S
and let ??be the space of bounded continuous functions form
[0,\infty)
to
S
.
The generator of the system in (
2.20
) is given by

(2.25)

\displaystyle??

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\xi\in\Omega_{N}}\Bigg(\sum_{\eta\in\Omega_{N}}a^{\Omega_{N}}(\xi,\eta)[x_{\eta}(t)-x_{\xi}(t)]\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{\xi}}+\frac{1}{2}g(x_{\xi}(t))\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{\xi}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\left[\frac{K_{m}e_{m}}{N^{m}}[y_{\xi,m}(t)-x_{\xi}(t)]\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{\xi}}+\frac{e_{m}}{N^{m}}[x_{\xi}(t)-y_{\xi,m}(t)]\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{\xi,m}}\right]\Bigg).

Let

(2.26)

\displaystyle??=\Big\{

\displaystyle f\in C_{b}([0,\infty),S)\colon\,f\mbox{ depends on finitely many components}

\displaystyle\mbox{and is twice continuously differentiable in each component}\Big\}.

Proposition 2.6
.

[Well-posedness]

(a)

The SSDE in (
2.20
) has a unique strong solution in ??, whose law is the unique solution of the
(G,\mathbf{F},\delta_{u})
-martingale problem for all
u\in S
.

(b)

The process starting from
u\in S
is Feller and strong Markov. Consequently, the SSDE in (
2.20
) defines a unique Borel Markov process starting from any initial law on
S
.

Proof.

Comparing with what is called model 2 in
[
GHO20
]
, we see that the Abelian group is chosen as in (
2.2
), the transition kernel is chosen as in (
2.6
), and the rates in and out of the seed-bank are
\frac{e_{m}}{N^{m}}
and
\frac{K_{m}e_{m}}{N^{m}}
for colour
m
. Hence the claim follows from
[
SS80
]
, in the same way as shown in the proof of
[
GHO20
, Theorem
2.1]
.
∎

Henceforth we write
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{E}
to denote probability and expectation with respect to the random process in (
2.19
).

2.4
Duality

If
g=d??
, then our model has a tractable dual, which turns out to play a crucial role in the analysis of the long-time behaviour. In this section we introduce the dual process following the same line of argument as in
[
GHO20
, Section 2.4]
. There it was shown that the spatial Fisher-Wright diffusion with seed-bank is dual to a so-called
block-counting process
of a seed-bank coalescent. The latter describes the ancestral lines of
n\in\mathbb{N}
individuals sampled from the current population backwards in time in terms of partition elements. At time zero the ancestral line of each individual is represented by a partition element. Traveling backwards in time, two partition elements merge as soon as their ancestral lines coalesce, i.e., two individuals have the same ancestor from that time onwards. Hence the seed-bank coalescent divides the ancestral lines of the
n\in\mathbb{N}
individuals into subgroups of individuals with the same ancestor (i.e., individuals that are identical by descent). Therefore the seed-bank coalescent generates the ancestral lineages of the individuals evolving according to a Fisher-Wright diffusion with seed-bank, i.e., generates their full genealogy. The corresponding block-counting process counts the number of partition elements that are left when we travel backwards in time.

coalescence
migration
e_{m}K_{m}/N^{m}
e_{m}/N^{m}
m
-dormant
active

Figure 4:
Transition scheme for an ancestral lineage in the dual, which moves according to the transition kernel
b(\cdot,\cdot)
defined in (
2.30
). Two active ancestral lineages that are at the same colony coalesce at rate
d
.

Formally, the spatial seed-bank coalescent is described as follows. Let
\mathbb{S}=\Omega_{N}\times\{A,(D_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\}
be the
effective geographic space
. For
n\in\mathbb{N}
the state space of the
n
-spatial seed-bank coalescent
is the set of partitions of
\{1,\ldots,n\}
, where the partition elements are marked with a position vector giving their locations. A state is written as
\pi
, where

(2.27)

\displaystyle\pi=((\pi_{1},\eta_{1}),\ldots,(\pi_{\bar{n}},\eta_{\bar{n}})),\quad\bar{n}=|\pi|,

\displaystyle\pi_{\ell}\subset\{1,\ldots,n\},\quad\{\pi_{1},\cdots\pi_{\bar{n}}\}\ \mbox{ is a partition of }\{1,\ldots,n\},

\displaystyle\eta_{\ell}\in\mathbb{S},\quad\ell\in\{1,\ldots,\bar{n}\},\quad 1\leq\bar{n}\leq n.

A marked partition element
(\pi_{\ell},\eta_{\ell})
is called active if
\eta_{\ell}=(\xi,A)
and
m
-dormant if
\eta_{\ell}=(\xi,D_{m})
for some
\xi\in\Omega_{N}
. The
n
-spatial seed-bank coalescent is denoted by

(2.28)

??,

and starts from

(2.29)

\mathcal{C}^{(n)}(0)=\pi(0),\qquad\pi(0)=\{(\{1\},\eta_{\ell_{1}}),\ldots,(\{n\},\eta_{\ell_{n}})\},\qquad\eta_{\ell_{1}},\ldots,\eta_{\ell_{n}}\in\mathbb{S}.

t

Figure 5:
Picture of the evolution of lineages in the spatial coalescent. The purple blocks depict the colonies, the black lines the active lineages, and the coloured lines the dormant lineages. Blue lineages can migrate. Two black lineages can coalesce when they are at the same colony. Red dormant lineages first have to become black and active before they can migrate or coalesce with other black and active lineages. Note that the dual runs
backwards in time
.

The
n
-spatial seed-bank coalescent is the Markov process that evolves according to the following two rules (see Figs.
4
–
5
).

1.

Each partition element moves independently of all other partition elements according to the transition kernel ??

(2.30)

\hskip-5.69046ptb((\xi,R_{\xi}),(\eta,R_{\eta}))=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}a^{\Omega_{N}}(\xi,\eta),&\text{ if }R_{\xi}=R_{\eta}=A,\\
K_{m}\frac{e_{m}}{N^{m}},&\text{ if }\xi=\eta,\ R_{\xi}=A,\ R_{\eta}=D_{m},\text{ for some }m\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\\
\frac{e_{m}}{N^{m}},&\text{ if }\xi=\eta,\ R_{\xi}=D_{m},\ R_{\eta}=A,\text{ for some }m\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\\
0,&\mbox{ otherwise},\end{array}\right.

where
a^{\Omega_{N}}(\cdot,\cdot)
is the migration kernel defined in (
2.6
),
K_{m}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
are the relative sizes of the
m
-dormant population and the active population defined in (
2.11
), and
e_{m}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
are the coefficients controlling the exchange between the active and the dormant population defined in (
2.10
). Thus, an active partition element migrates according to the transition kernel
a^{\Omega_{N}}(\cdot,\cdot)
and becomes
m
-dormant at rate
K_{m}\frac{e_{m}}{N^{m}}
, while an
m
-dormant partition element can only become active and does so at rate
\frac{e_{m}}{N^{m}}
.

2.

Independently of all other partition elements, two partition elements that are at the same colony and are both active coalesce with rate
d
, i.e., the two partition elements merge into one partition element.

Fig.
4
gives a schematic overview of the possible transitions of a single lineage, while Fig.
5
gives an example of the evolution in the dual. The
spatial seed-bank coalescent

(\mathcal{C}(t))_{t\geq 0}
is defined as the projective limit of the
n
-spatial seed-bank coalescents
(\mathcal{C}^{(n)}(t))_{t\geq 0}
as
n\to\infty
. This object is well-defined by Kolmogorov’s extension theorem (see
[
BGCKWB16
, Section 3]
).

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
we define the block-counting process ??corresponding to the
n
-spatial seed-bank coalescent as the process that counts at each site
(\xi,R_{\xi})\in\Omega_{N}\times\{A,(D_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\}
the number of partition elements of
\mathcal{C}^{(n)}(t)
, i.e.,

(2.31)

\displaystyle L(t)=\big(L_{(\xi,A)}(t),\left(L_{(\xi,D_{m})}(t)\right)_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\big)_{\xi\in\Omega_{N}},

\displaystyle L_{(\xi,A)}(t)=L_{(\xi,A)}(\mathcal{C}^{(n)}(t))=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\bar{n}}1_{\{\eta_{\ell}(t)=(\xi,A)\}},

\displaystyle L_{(\xi,D_{m})}(t)=L_{(\xi,D_{m})}(\mathcal{C}^{(n)}(t))=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\bar{n}}1_{\{\eta_{\ell}(t)=(\xi,D_{m})\}},\qquad m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

The state space of
(L(t))_{t\geq 0}
is
S^{\prime}=(\mathbb{N}_{0}\times\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{N}_{0}})^{\Omega_{N}}
. We denote the elements of
S^{\prime}
by sequences
(m_{\xi},(n_{\xi,D_{m}})_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}})_{\xi\in\Omega_{N}}
, and define
\delta_{(\eta,R_{\eta})}\in S^{\prime}
to be the element of
S^{\prime}
that is
0
at all sites
(\xi,R_{\xi})\in\Omega_{N}\times\{A,(D_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\}\backslash(\eta,R_{\eta})
, and
1
at the site
(\eta,R_{\eta})
. From the evolution of
\mathcal{C}^{(n)}(t)
described below (
2.28
) we see that the block-counting process has the following transition kernel:

(2.32)

\displaystyle(m_{\xi},(n_{\xi,D_{m}})_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}})_{\xi\in\Omega_{N}}\rightarrow

\displaystyle\begin{cases}({m}_{\xi},(n_{\xi,D_{m}})_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}})_{\xi\in\Omega_{N}}-\delta_{(\eta,A)}+\delta_{(\zeta,A)},\ &\text{at rate }m_{\eta}a(\eta,\zeta)\text{ for }\eta,\zeta\in\Omega_{N},\\
({m}_{\xi},(n_{\xi,D_{m}})_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}})_{\xi\in\Omega_{N}}-\delta_{(\eta,A)},\ &\text{at rate }d{m_{\eta}\choose 2}\text{ for }\eta\in\Omega_{N},\\
({m}_{\xi},(n_{\xi,D_{m}})_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}})_{\xi\in\Omega_{N}}-\delta_{(\eta,A)}+\delta_{(\eta,D_{m})},\ &\text{at rate }m_{\eta}K_{m}\frac{e_{m}}{N^{m}}\text{ for }\eta\in\Omega_{N},\\
({m}_{\xi},(n_{\xi,D_{m}})_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}})_{\xi\in\Omega_{N}}+\delta_{(\eta,A)}-\delta_{(\eta,D_{m})},\ &\text{at rate }n_{\eta,m}\frac{e_{m}}{N^{m}}\text{ for }\eta\in\Omega_{N}.\\
\end{cases}

The process
(Z(t))_{t\geq 0}
defined in (
2.20
) is dual to the block-counting process
(L(t))_{t\geq 0}
with
duality function

??\colon\,S\times S^{\prime}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

(2.33)

H\Big(\big(x_{\xi},(y_{\xi,m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\big)_{\xi\in\Omega_{N}},\big(m_{\xi},(n_{\xi,D_{m}})_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\big)_{\xi\in\Omega_{N}}\Big)=\prod_{\xi\in\Omega_{N}}x_{\xi}^{m_{\xi}}\prod_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}y_{\xi,m}^{n_{\xi,D_{m}}}.

Proposition 2.7
.

[Duality relation]
.

Let
H
be as in (
2.33
). Then, for all
(x_{\xi},(y_{\xi,m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}})_{\xi\in\Omega_{N}}

\in S
and
(m_{\xi},(n_{\xi,D_{m}})_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}})_{\xi\in\Omega_{N}}\in S^{\prime}
,

(2.34)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\big(x_{\xi},(y_{\xi,m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\big)_{\xi\in\Omega_{N}}}\Big[H\Big(\big(x_{\xi}(t),(y_{\xi,m}(t))_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\big)_{\xi\in\Omega_{N}},(m_{\xi},n_{\xi})_{\xi\in\Omega_{N}}\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\big(m_{\xi},(n_{\xi,D_{m}})_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\big)_{\xi\in\Omega_{N}}}\Big[H\Big(\big(x_{\xi},(y_{\xi,m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\big)_{\xi\in\Omega_{N}},\big(L_{(\xi,A)}(t),(L_{(\xi,D_{m})}(t))_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\big)_{\xi\in\Omega_{N}}\Big)\Big]

with
\mathbb{E}
the generic symbol for expectation (on the left over the original process, on the right over the dual process).

Proposition
2.7
was proved in
[
GHO20
, Section 2.4]
. Since the duality function
H
captures all the mixed moments of
(Z(t))_{t\geq 0}
, the duality relation is that of a
moment dual
.

Remark 2.8
.

[Duality relation in terms of the effective geographic space]

Interpreting
(Z(t))_{t\geq 0}
as a process on the effective geographic space
\mathbb{S}=\Omega_{N}\times\{A,(D_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\}
, we can rewrite (
2.20
) as

(2.35)

\displaystyle{\rm d}??

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\xi,R_{\xi})\in\mathbb{S}}b((\xi,R_{\xi}),(\eta,R_{\eta}))[z_{(\eta,R_{\eta})}(t)-z_{(\xi,R_{\xi})}(t)]\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle+1_{\{R_{\xi}=A\}}\sqrt{g(z_{(\xi,R_{\xi})}(t))}\,{\rm d}w_{\xi}(t),\qquad(\xi,R_{\xi})\in\mathbb{S},

where
b(\cdot,\cdot)
is the transition kernel defined in (
2.30
). If
g=dg_{\mathrm{FW}}
, then we can write its dual process as follows. Let
(L(t))_{t\geq 0}=(L(\mathcal{C}(t))_{t\geq 0}
be the block-counting process that at each site
(\xi,\mathbb{R}_{\xi})\in\mathbb{S}
counts the number of partition elements of
\mathcal{C}(t)
, i.e.,

(2.36)

\displaystyle L(t)

\displaystyle=(L_{(\xi,R_{\xi})}(t))_{{(\xi,R_{\xi})}\in\mathbb{S}},

\displaystyle L_{(\xi,R_{\xi})}(t)

\displaystyle=L_{(\xi,R_{\xi})}(\mathcal{C}(t))=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\bar{n}}1_{\{\eta_{\ell}(t)={(\xi,R_{\xi})}\}}.

Rewrite the duality function
H
in (
2.33
) as

(2.37)

H\Big((z_{(\xi,R_{\xi})},l_{(\xi,R_{\xi})})_{{(\xi,R_{\xi})}\in\mathbb{S}}\Big)=\prod_{{(\xi,R_{\xi})}\in\mathbb{S}}z_{(\xi,R_{\xi})}^{l_{(\xi,R_{\xi})}}.

Then, for
z\in S
and
l\in S^{\prime}
, the duality relation in (
2.34
) reads

(2.38)

\mathbb{E}_{z(\xi,R_{\xi})}\big[H(z_{(\xi,R_{\xi})}(t),l_{(\xi,R_{\xi})})\big]=\mathbb{E}_{l(\xi,R_{\xi})}\big[H(z_{(\xi,R_{\xi})},L_{(\xi,R_{\xi})}(t))\big].

Interpreting the duality relation in terms of the effective geographic space
\mathbb{S}
, we see that each ancestral lineage in the dual is a Markov process moving according to the transition kernel
b(\cdot,\cdot)
defined in (
2.30
). Interpreting the duality relation in terms of the geographic space
\Omega_{N}
, we see that an ancestral lineage is a random walk on
\Omega_{N}
, with internal states
A
and
(D_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
. Both interpretations turn out to be useful when we analyse the long-time behaviour of the system.

\blacksquare

Figure 6:
Renewal process induced by a lineage in the dual moving according to the transition kernel
b(\cdot,\cdot)
. For
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\sigma_{k}
denotes the
k
th active period and
\tau_{k}
the
k
th dormant period.

Remark 2.9
.

[The renewal process induced by the dual process]

The partition elements describing the dual process give rise to a renewal process on the active state
A
and the dormant state
D=\bigcup_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}D_{m}
. Since the only transition a dormant lineage can make is to become active, irrespectively of its colour, each dual lineage induces a sequence of active and dormant time lapses. Let
(\sigma_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
denote the successive active time periods and
(\tau_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
the successive dormant time periods (see Fig.
6
). Then
(??)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(??)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
are sequences of i.i.d. random variables with marginal laws (recall (
2.13
))

(2.39)

\mathbb{P}(\sigma_{1}>t)={\rm e}^{-\chi t},\quad\mathbb{P}(\tau_{1}>t)=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\frac{K_{m}\frac{e_{m}}{N^{m}}}{\chi}\,{\rm e}^{-\frac{e_{m}}{N^{m}}t},\qquad t\geq 0.

Remark 2.10
.

[Wake up times]

The renewal process in Fig.
6
is key to understanding the long-time behaviour of the model (as we will see in Section
3
). Note that

(2.40)

??=\tau_{1}

represents
the typical wake-up time of a lineage in the dual
. By choosing specific sequences
(K_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
and
(e_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
we can mimic different wake-up time distributions. In particular, if we allow
\rho=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}K_{m}=\infty
(recall (
2.14
)), then
\tau
may have a fat-tail (examples are given in Section
3
). In other words,
the internal structure of the seed-bank allows us to model fat-tailed wake-up times without loosing the Markov property of the evolution.

\Box

Note that even when there is no dual, i.e.,
g\in\mathcal{G}
with
g\neq dg_{\mathrm{FW}}
, we can still define
\tau
by (
2.40
), since
\tau_{1}
in (
2.39
) is a random variable that depends only on the sequences
(K_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
and
(e_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
, and we can still interpret
\tau
as the typical wake-up time of an individual in the population.

\blacksquare

2.5
Clustering criterion

2.5.1
Clustering criterion for Fisher-Wright diffusion function

In
[
GHO20
]
we showed that the system exhibits a dichotomy between
coexistence
(= locally multi-type equilibria) and
clustering
(= locally mono-type equilibria). The clustering criterion is based on the dual and requires the notion of colour regularity.
We call a law translation invariant when it is
invariant under the group action
.

Definition 2.11
.

[Colour regular initial measures]

We say that a translation invariant initial measure
\mu(0)
is
colour regular
when

(2.41)

\lim_{m\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\mu(0)}[y_{0,m}]\quad\text{ exists}.

This condition is needed because, as time progresses, lineages starting from slower and slower seed-banks become active and bring new types into the active population. Without control on the initial states of the slow seed-banks, there may be no convergence to equilibrium.

\blacksquare

The key clustering criterion is the following.

Proposition 2.12
.

[Clustering criterion]

Suppose that
\mu(0)
is translation invariant. If
\rho=\infty
(recall (
2.14
)), then additionally suppose that
\mu(0)
is colour regular. Let
d\in(0,\infty)
. Then the system with
g=dg_{\mathrm{FW}}
clusters if and only if in the dual two partition elements coalesce with probability
1
.

The idea behind Theorem
2.12
is as follows. If in the dual two partition elements coalesce with probability 1, then a random sample of
n
individuals drawn from the current population has a common ancestor some finite time backwards in time. Since individuals inherit their type from their parent individuals, this means that all
n
individuals have the same type. A formal proof was given in
[
GHO20
, Section 4.3]
. The proof is valid for any geographic space given by a countable Abelian group endowed with the discrete topology, of which
\Omega_{N}
is an example.

2.5.2
Clustering criterion for general diffusion function

For
g\in\mathcal{G}
with
g\neq dg_{\mathrm{FW}}
no dual is available and hence we cannot use the clustering criterion in Proposition
2.12
. However, as shown in
[
GHO20
]
, we can argue by duality comparison arguments (see
[
GHO20
, Lemma 5.5 and Lemma 6.3]
) that the system evolving according to (
2.20
) with
g\in\mathcal{G}
clusters if and only if the system with
g=dg_{\mathrm{FW}}
for some
d\in(0,\infty)
clusters. In particular, for
g=dg_{\mathrm{FW}}
,
d\in(0,\infty)
, whether or not the system clusters does not depend on the resampling rate
d
.

3
Main results:
N<\infty
, identification of clustering regime

In this section we identify the
clustering regime
, i.e., the range of
parameters
for which the clustering criterion in Proposition
2.12
is met. In
[
GHO20
, Section 3.2, Theorem 3.3]
we derived a necessary and sufficient condition for when clustering prevails, for any geometric space given by a countable Abelian group endowed with the discrete topology. Recall
\chi
in (
2.13
),
\rho
in (
2.14
) and
\tau
in (
2.40
),. From (
2.39
) it follows that

(3.1)

\mathbb{E}[\tau]=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\frac{K_{m}}{\chi}=\frac{\rho}{\chi},

and hence the mean wake-up time is finite if
\rho<\infty
and infinite if
\rho=\infty
. In Section
3.1
we look at
\rho<\infty
and in Section
3.2
at
\rho=\infty
. In Section
3.3
we summarise our findings and identify the clustering regime.

3.1
Finite mean wake-up time

Suppose that the system evolving according to (
2.20
) has a translation invariant initial measure
\mu(0)
with density
\theta\in(0,1)
. Then
[
GHO20
, Theorem 3.3]
says that for
\rho<\infty
clustering occurs if and only if

(3.2)

\int_{1}^{\infty}{\rm d}t\,a^{\Omega_{N}}_{t}(0,0)=\infty.

It is known that (
3.2
) holds for the hierarchical migration defined in (
2.6
) if and only if
[
DGW05
, Section 3]

(3.3)

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\frac{1}{c_{k}}=\infty.

Hence, for
\rho<\infty
, the clustering criterion depends on the migration kernel
only
and the seed-bank has no effect.

Figure 7:
Successive periods during which the two random walks are active and dormant (recall (
2.39
) and Fig.
6
). The time lapses between successive pairs of dotted lines represent periods of
joint activity
.

In view of Proposition
2.12
, if
g=dg_{\mathrm{FW}}
, then clustering prevails if and only if two lineages in the dual coalesce with probability 1. Recall that two lineages in the dual can only coalesce when they are at the same site and are both active. Since the rate of coalescence is
d\in(0,\infty)
, each time this happens the two lineages have a positive probability to coalesce before moving or becoming dormant. Therefore, clustering prevails if and only if two lineages meet infinitely often while being active. This happens exactly when (
3.2
) holds. The fact that the seed-bank plays no role can be seen from the dual. Each lineage in the dual moves according to the transition kernel
b(\cdot,\cdot)
(recall (
2.30
)). Looking at the renewal process induced by the dual process (recall Remark
2.9
and Fig.
7
), we see that for
\rho<\infty
the probability that a lineage in the dual is active at time
t
is approximately
\frac{1}{1+\rho}
for large
t
. The total activity time of a lineage up to time
t
is therefore approximately
\frac{1}{1+\rho}t
for large
t
. Hence the total time the two lineages in the dual are at the same site and are both active is approximately

(3.4)

\int_{1}^{\infty}{\rm d}t\left(\frac{1}{1+\rho}\right)^{2}a_{2\frac{1}{1+\rho}t}(0,0),

By Polya’s argument, if the integral in (
3.4
) is infinite, then two lineages in the dual meet infinitely often while being active. After a variable transformation, (
3.4
) becomes the integral in (
3.2
) up to a constant. (For a formal proof of the criterion in (
3.2
), we refer to
[
GHO20
]
.) By the above argument, we can think of the integral in (
3.2
) as the
total hazard of coalescence
of two dual lineages. To get the result for general
g\in\mathcal{G}
we must invoke the duality comparison arguments mentioned in Section
2.5.2
.

In terms of the degree of the random walk (recall Remark
2.1
), (
3.2
) corresponds to hierarchical migration with degree
0^{-}
. The same criterion as in (
3.2
) was found in
[
FG96
]
for interacting Fisher-Wright diffusions on the hierarchical lattice without seed-bank (
\rho=0
). Hence we conclude that for
\rho<\infty
the seed-bank does not affect the dichotomy.

3.2
Infinite mean wake-up time

If
\rho=\infty
, then the seed-bank does affect the dichotomy. To apply the criterion in
[
GHO20
, Theorem 3.3]
, we assume that the system evolving according to (
2.20
) has a translation-invariant initial measure
\mu(0)
with density
\theta\in(0,1)
that is colour regular.

The criterion for clustering that was derived in
[
GHO20
]
for
\rho=\infty
applies to wake-up times
\tau
(recall (
2.40
)) of the form

(3.5)

\frac{\mathbb{P}(\tau\in{\rm d}t)}{{\rm d}t}\sim\varphi(t)\,t^{-(1+\gamma)},\quad t\to\infty,\quad\gamma\in(0,1],

with
??

slowly varying at infinity
. Define

(3.6)

\hat{\varphi}(t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\varphi(t),&\gamma\in(0,1),\\[5.69046pt]
\mathbb{E}\left[\tau\wedge t\right]&\gamma=1.\end{array}\right.

As shown in
[
BGT87
, Section 1.3]
, every slowly varying function
\varphi
may be assumed to be infinitely differentiable and to be represented by the integral

(3.7)

\varphi(t)=\exp\left[\int_{(\cdot)}^{t}\frac{{\rm d}u}{u}\,\psi(u)\right]

for some
\psi\colon[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\lim_{u\to\infty}|\psi(u)|=0
. From (
3.5
) we see that
\hat{\varphi}(t)
is also slowly varying. If we assume that
|\psi(u)|\leq C/\log u
for some
C<\infty
, then the system clusters if and only if (see
[
GHO20
, Section 3.2]
)

(3.8)

\int_{(\cdot)}^{\infty}{\rm d}t\,\hat{\varphi}(t)^{-1/\gamma}\,t^{-(1-\gamma)/\gamma}\,\hat{a}_{t}(0,0)=\infty.

Note that
??
in (
3.5
) is the
tail exponent
of the typical wake-up time
\tau
(recall Remark
2.9
) and depends on the sequences
\underline{e},\underline{K}
in (
2.10
) governing the exchange with the seed-bank. If
g=dg_{\mathrm{FW}}
, then in view of Theorem
2.12
the criterion in (
3.8
) determines whether two lineages in the dual coalesce with probability 1.

In Section
6
we will use the renewal process induced by the dual (recall Remark
2.9
) to show that (
3.8
) indeed gives the
total hazard of coalescence
of two dual lineages. Therefore the integral in (
3.8
) is the counterpart of (
3.2
). The rate of coalescence again does not affect the dichotomy: in
[
GHO20
]
a duality comparison argument was used to show that (
3.8
) gives the clustering criterion also for
g\in\mathcal{G}
with
g\neq dg_{\mathrm{FW}}
. The effect of the seed-bank on the dichotomy is embodied by the term
\hat{\varphi}(t)^{-1/\gamma}\,t^{-(1-\gamma)/\gamma}
in (
3.8
). The criterion in (
3.8
) shows that there is a competition between migration and exchange with the seed-bank.

For the special case where
\hat{\varphi}(t)\asymp 1
, the criterion in (
3.8
) says that (recall Remark
2.1
)

(3.9)

\text{clustering}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\text{ either }\delta^{-}\leq-\frac{1-\gamma}{\gamma}\text{ or }\delta^{+}<-\frac{1-\gamma}{\gamma}.

Condition (
3.8
) implies that for
\gamma\in(0,\tfrac{1}{2})
no clustering is possible: the typical wake-up time has such a heavy tail that with a positive probability two dual lineages do not meet, irrespective of the migration.

Definition 3.1
.

In what follows we will focus on the following two specific parameter regimes:

\bullet

Asymptotically polynomial
, i.e.,

(3.10)

K_{k}\sim Ak^{-\alpha},\quad e_{k}\sim Bk^{-\beta},\quad c_{k}\sim Fk^{-\phi},\quad k\to\infty,\,A,B,F\in(0,\infty),\,\alpha,\beta,\phi\in\mathbb{R}.

\bullet

Pure exponential
, i.e.,

(3.11)

K_{k}=K^{k},\quad e_{k}=e^{k},\quad c_{k}=c^{k},\quad k\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\,K,e,c\in(0,\infty).

Note that both (
2.7
) and (
2.12
) are satisfied for
N\to\infty
. Also note that an infinite seed-bank corresponds to
\alpha\in(-\infty,1]
, respectively,
K\in[1,\infty)
.

\Box

The scaling of the wake-up time and the migration kernel in these parameter regimes are as follows.

Theorem 3.2
.

[Infinite seed-bank: Scaling of wake-up time and migration kernel]

Suppose that
\rho=\infty
. Then

(a)

Subject to (
3.10
),

(3.12)

\displaystyle\gamma=1,\quad\varphi(t)\asymp(\log t)^{-\alpha},\quad\hat{\varphi}(t)\asymp\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(\log t)^{1-\alpha},&\alpha\in(-\infty,1),\\[5.69046pt]
\log\log t,&\alpha=1,\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle a_{t}^{\Omega_{N}}(0,0)\asymp t^{-1}\log^{\phi}t.

(b)

Subject to (
3.11
),

(3.13)

\displaystyle\gamma=\gamma_{N,K,e}=\frac{\log(N/Ke)}{\log(N/e)},\quad\varphi(t)\asymp 1,\quad\hat{\varphi}(t)\asymp\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1,&K\in(1,\infty),\\[5.69046pt]
\log t,&K=1,\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle a_{t}^{\Omega_{N}}(0,0)\asymp t^{-1-\delta_{N,c}},

where

(3.14)

\delta_{N,c}=\frac{\log c}{\log(N/c)}.

Theorem
3.2
will be proved in Section
6
.

Note that, by (
3.13
),
\gamma_{N,K,e}=1
for all
N
when
K=1
, while
\gamma_{N,K,e}<1
for all
N
when
K>1
, but with
\gamma_{N,K,e}\uparrow 1
as
N\to\infty
. Also note that, subject to (
3.10
), (
3.12
) says that the degree of the random walk is
0^{-}
for
\phi\geq-1
and
0^{+}
for
\phi<-1
, while subject to (
3.11
), by (
3.13
), the degree of the random walk is
\delta_{N,c}^{-}
, which is
0
for all
N
when
c=1
, and tends to
0
as
N\to\infty
from above when
c>1
and from below as
c<1
. Thus, both (
3.10
) and (
3.11
) with
N\to\infty
correspond to a
critically recurrent migration
and a
critically infinite seed-bank
.

3.3
Clustering regime

Summarising the above discussion, we can now identify the clustering regime for both finite and infinite seed-banks.

Theorem 3.3
.

[Clustering regime]

(1)

If
\rho<\infty
, then clustering prevails if and only if

(3.15)

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\frac{1}{c_{k}}=\infty.

(2)

If
\rho=\infty
, then clustering prevails for
N
large enough

(a)

Subject to (
3.10
) if and only if

(3.16)

-\phi\leq\alpha\leq 1.

(b)

Subject to (
3.11
) if and only if

(3.17)

Kc\leq 1\leq K.

Also Theorem
3.3
will be proved in Section
6
.

Note that for
\rho<\infty
the clustering regime follows by combining (
3.2
) and (
3.3
), while for
\rho=\infty
the clustering regime follows by substituting into (
3.8
) the scaling of the wake-up times and the migration kernel stated in Theorem
3.2
.

Remark 3.4
.

Note that subject to (
3.10
), respectively, (
3.11
),
\rho<\infty
implies that
\alpha>1
, respectively,
K<1
, and so the clustering regime is
-\phi\leq 1
, respectively,
c\leq 1
(recall (
3.15
)), which are less stringent than (
3.16
), respectively, (
3.17
).

\blacksquare

4
Main results:
N\to\infty
, renormalisation and multi-scale limit

This section contains our multi-scale hierarchical limit theorems. The multi-scale hierarchical limit theorems analyse the evolution of the block averages defined in Definition
2.3
. In Section
4.1
we recall a path topology referred to as the
Meyer-Zheng topology
, which we will need in part of our multi-scale hierarchical limit theorems. In Section
4.2
we present the conceptual ingredients needed for our theorems. In Section
4.3
we state two versions of the hierarchical multi-scale limit (Theorems
4.2
and
4.4
), and comment on how they are related to each other. In Section
4.4
we explain how they arise from a heuristic analysis of the SSDE in (
2.20
).

4.1
Intermezzo: Meyer-Zheng topology

Recall the block averages defined in (
2.3
) and their evolution equations in (
2.24
). In the limit as
N\to\infty
, some of the pre-factors in (
2.24
) diverge as a result of the speeding up of time. This makes the processes increasingly more volatile: paths becomes rougher and rougher during rarer and rarer times. Therefore we cannot work with weak convergence on path space
C([0,\infty),E)
w.r.t. the topology generated by the
sup-norm
on compacts, or on path space
??
w.r.t. the Skorohod metric on compacts. Rather we must follow the methodology used in
[
DG14
, pp. 792–794]
and employ the so-called
Meyer-Zheng topology
on
pseudopaths
, (see
[
MZ84
]
), which is based on the following idea. Consider functions
f\colon\,[0,\infty)\to E
, with
(E,d)
a Polish space, and sequences of functions
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
that are càdlàg paths, i.e., functions in the Skorohod space
D([0,\infty),E)
. Then
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
f
in the Meyer-Zheng topology if and only if

(4.1)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{a}^{b}{\rm d}t\,\big[1\wedge d(f(t),f_{n}(t))\big]=0\qquad\forall\,\,0\leq a<b<\infty.

However, the topology induced by the metric in (
4.1
) does not turn
D([0,\infty),E)
into a
closed
space (while in order to apply the classical theory of weak convergence of probability laws on path space we need the path space to be Polish).

To turn the idea from (
4.1
) into a manageable topology, we proceed by defining a
space of pseudopaths
equipped with the
Meyer-Zheng topology
. If
(E,d)
is a Polish space and
s\mapsto v(s)
is a measurable map from
[0,\infty)
to
E
, then the pseudopath
??
is the
probability measure

\rho
on
[0,\infty)\times E
, defined by

(4.2)

\rho((a,b)\times B)=\int_{a}^{b}{\rm d}s\,{\rm e}^{-s}\,1_{B}(v(s)),\qquad B\in\mathcal{B}(E),

where
\mathcal{B}(E)
is the Borel
\sigma
-algebra over
E
.
Hence
\psi_{v}
is the image measure of
{\rm e}^{-t}{\rm d}t
under the mapping
t\to(t,v(t))
. In other words, we consider the
weighted occupation measure
of the path in
E
in order to describe paths that are
regular representatives
in the space of functions once we take into account (
4.1
). Note that a piece-wise constant càdlàg path is uniquely determined by its occupation measure. So is a continuous path with continuous local times. The space of all pseudopaths is denoted by
??
.

Since pseudopaths are measures on
[0,\infty]\times E
, convergence of pseudopaths is defined as
weak convergence
of
probability measures on
[0,\infty]\times E
. A sequence
(v_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of measurable maps from
[0,\infty)\times E
is said to converge in the Meyer-Zheng topology to a measurable map
v
if
\lim_{n\to\infty}\psi_{v_{n}}=\psi_{v}
, i.e.,
\lim_{n\to\infty}\psi_{v_{n}}f=\psi_{v}f
for all continuous bounded functions
f
on
[0,\infty]\times E
.

Remark 4.1
.

[Pseudopaths]

The space
\Psi
of pseudopaths endowed with the Meyer-Zheng topology is Polish, but the space
D([0,\infty),E)
endowed with the Meyer-Zheng topology is not Polish (see
[
MZ84
, p. 372]
).

\blacksquare

In what follows, each time convergence holds in the Meyer-Zheng topology we will say so explicitly. If no topology is mentioned, then we mean convergence in
\mathcal{C}_{b}([0,\infty),[0,1])
. In Appendix
B
we collect some basic facts about the Meyer-Zheng topology taken from
[
MZ84
]
and
[
Kur91
]
.

4.2
Main ingredients for the hierarchical multi-scale limit

Recall the definition of
\theta_{x}
and
\theta_{y_{m}}
in (
2.21
). Define

(4.3)

??=\frac{\theta_{x}+\sum_{m=0}^{k}K_{m}\theta_{y_{m}}}{1+\sum_{m=0}^{k}K_{m}},\qquad k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

For
\rho<\infty
, and for
\rho=\infty
under the additional assumption of colour regularity (recall Proposition
2.12
), we have

(4.4)

\lim_{k\to\infty}\vartheta_{k}=\theta\quad\text{ for some }\theta\in[0,1].

Define the
slowing-down constants
(
E_{0}=1
)

(4.5)

??=\frac{1}{1+\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}K_{m}},\qquad k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

For
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, let

(4.6)

\big(\theta,(y_{m,l})_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\big)

be a sequence of random variables taking values in
[0,1]
, and let

(4.7)

??=\Big(x_{l}(t),(y_{m,l}(t))_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\Big)_{t\geq 0}

be the
full process
on level
l
evolving according to

(4.8)

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle d}x_{l}(t)=E_{l}\Bigg[c_{l}[\theta-x_{l}(t)]\,{\rm d}t+\sqrt{(\mathcal{F}^{(l)}g)(x_{l}(t))}\,{\rm d}w(t)+K_{l}e_{l}\,[y_{l,l}(t)-x_{l}(t)]\,{\rm d}t\Bigg],

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lll}&y_{m,l}(t)=x_{l}(t),&0\leq m<l,\\
&{\rm d}y_{l,l}(t)=e_{l}\,[x_{l}(t)-y_{l,l}(t)]\,{\rm d}t,&m=l,\\
&y_{m,l}(t)=y_{m,l},&m>l.\end{array}

where
\mathcal{F}^{(l)}g
is an element of
\mathcal{G}
, (recall (
2.15
)), that will be defined in (
4.17
) below. By
[
YW71
]
the above SSDE has a unique solution for every initial measure. For
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, let

(4.9)

??=\left(x^{\mathrm{eff}}_{l}(t),y^{\mathrm{eff}}_{l,l}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}

be the
effective process
evolving according to

(4.10)

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle d}x^{\mathrm{eff}}_{l}(t)=E_{l}\left[c_{l}\,[\theta-x^{\mathrm{eff}}_{l}(t)]\,{\rm d}t+\sqrt{(\mathcal{F}^{(l)}g)(x^{\mathrm{eff}}_{l}(t))}\,{\rm d}w(t)+K_{l}e_{l}\,[y^{\mathrm{eff}}_{l,l}(t)-x^{\mathrm{eff}}_{l}(t)]\,{\rm d}t\right],

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle d}y^{\mathrm{eff}}_{l,l}(t)=e_{l}\,[x^{\mathrm{eff}}_{l}(t)-y^{\mathrm{eff}}_{l,l}(t)]\,{\rm d}t.

Comparing (
4.8
) with (
4.10
), we see that the effective process looks at the non-trivial components of the full process.

Apart from (
4.7
) and (
4.9
), we need the following list of
ingredients
to formally state the multi-scale limit:

1.

For
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
t>0
, define the
estimators
for the finite system by

(4.11)

\displaystyle??

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{l}}\sum_{\xi\in B_{l}}\frac{x^{\Omega_{N}}_{\xi}(t)+\sum_{m=0}^{l-1}K_{m}y^{\Omega_{N}}_{\xi,m}(t)}{1+\sum_{m=0}^{l-1}K_{m}},

\displaystyle??

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{l}}\sum_{\xi\in B_{l}}x^{\Omega_{N}}_{\xi}(t),

\displaystyle??

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{l}}\sum_{\xi\in B_{l}}y^{\Omega_{N}}_{\xi,m}(t),\qquad m\in\mathbb{N}_{0},

and put

(4.12)

\displaystyle??

\displaystyle=\big(\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{x}(t),\big(\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{y_{m}}(t)\big)_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\big),

\displaystyle??

\displaystyle=\big(\bar{\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}}(t),\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{y_{l}}(t)\big).

We call
(\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}}(t))_{t\geq 0}
the
full estimator process
and
(\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{\mathrm{eff},(l),\Omega_{N}}(t))_{t\geq 0}
the
effective process
. Note that
\Theta_{x}^{(l),\Omega_{N}}(t)
is the empirical average of the active components in the
l
-block, while
\Theta_{y_{m}}^{(l),\Omega_{N}}(t)
, is the empirical average of the
m
-dormant components in the
l
-block, both without scaling of time. Note that
\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{x}(N^{l}t)=x^{\Omega_{N}}_{l}(t)
. The
level-
l
estimator

\bar{\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}}(t)
will play an important role in our analysis. Using (
2.24
), we can derive the evolution equations of
\bar{\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}}(N^{l}t)
. We see that in the evolution of
\bar{\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}}(N^{l}t)
no rates appear that tend to infinity as
N\to\infty
. However, in the evolution of
\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{x}(N^{l}t)
and
\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{y_{m}}(N^{l}t)
for
m<l
the rates describing the interaction between the active and the dormant population tend to infinity as
N\to\infty
.

2.

For
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, consider
time scales

N^{l}t_{l}
such that

(4.13)

\mathcal{L}\big[\bar{\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}}(N^{l}t_{l}-L(N)N^{l-1})-\bar{\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}}(N^{l}t_{l})\big]=\delta_{0}

for all sequences
??
satisfying
\lim_{N\to\infty}L(N)=\infty
and
\lim_{N\to\infty}L(N)/N=0
, but not for
L(N)=N
. In words,
N^{l}t_{l}
is the time scale on which
(\bar{\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}}(N^{l}t_{l}))_{t_{l}>0}
is no longer a fixed process.

3.

For
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
the
invariant measure
for the limiting evolution of the full process on level
l
in (
4.8
) is denoted by

(4.14)

??,\qquad y_{l}=(y_{m,l})_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}.

(The existence of and convergence to this equilibrium will be proved in Section
12.2
.) Note that
\Gamma_{(\theta,y_{l})}^{(l)}
depends on choice of the rates
E_{l},c_{l},K_{l},e_{l}
in (
4.8
). The
invariant measure
of the limiting evolution for the effective
l
-block process in (
4.10
) is denoted by

(4.15)

\displaystyle??.

Also
\Gamma_{\theta}^{\mathrm{eff},(l)}
depends on the choice of the rates
E_{l},c_{l},K_{l},e_{l}
.

4.

For
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, let
??
denote the
renormalisation transformation
acting on
\mathcal{G}
defined by

(4.16)

(\mathcal{F}^{E_{l},c_{l},K_{l},e_{l}}g)(\theta)=\int_{[0,1]^{2}}g(x)\,\Gamma_{\theta}^{\mathrm{eff},(l)}({\rm d}x),\qquad\theta\in[0,1].

(In Section
9
we show that
\mathcal{F}g\in\mathcal{G}
.) For
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, define the
iterate
of the renormalisation transformation as the composition

(4.17)

??=\mathcal{F}^{E_{k-1},c_{k-1},K_{k-1},e_{k-1}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathcal{F}^{E_{0},c_{0},K_{0},e_{0}}.

5.

For
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, define the
interaction chain

[
DGV95
]

(4.18)

??

as the
time-inhomogeneous
Markov chain on
[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
with initial state

(4.19)

M^{k}_{-(k+1)}=(\vartheta_{k},\overbrace{\vartheta_{k},\cdots,\vartheta_{k}}^{k+1\text{ times }},\theta_{y_{k+1}},\theta_{y_{k+2}},\cdots)

that evolves from time
-(l+1)
to time
-l
according to the transition kernel
??
on
[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
given by

(4.20)

Q^{[l]}(u,{\rm d}v)=\Gamma_{u}^{(l)}({\rm d}v).

(See Fig.
9
.) For
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, define the
effective interaction chain

(4.21)

??

as the
time-inhomogeneous
Markov chain on
[0,1]\times[0,1]
with initial state

(4.22)

M^{\mathrm{eff},k}_{-(k+1)}=(\vartheta_{k},\theta_{y_{k+1}})

that evolves from time
-(l+1)
to time
-l
according to the transition kernel
??
on
[0,1]\times[0,1]
given by

(4.23)

Q^{\mathrm{eff},[l]}(u,{\rm d}v)=\Gamma^{\mathrm{eff},(l)}_{u_{x}}({\rm d}v),

where
u_{x}
denotes the first component of
u=(u_{x},u_{y})
.(See Fig.
8
.) We denote the components of
\left(M^{\mathrm{eff},k}_{-l}\right)
by

(4.24)

M^{\mathrm{eff},k}_{-l}=\big(M^{\mathrm{eff},k}_{-l,x},M^{\mathrm{eff},k}_{-l,y}\big).

4.3
Hierarchical multi-scale limit theorems

First we present and discuss the scaling of the effective process. Afterwards we do the same for the full process.

Figure 8:
Effective interaction chain.

4.3.1
Effective process

We present one of our main theorems, the hierarchical mean-field limit for the effective process. We will use the process and notation introduced in Section
4.2
.

Theorem 4.2
.

[Hierarchical mean-field: the effective process]

Suppose that the initial state of the hierarchical system is given by (
2.21
). Let
L(N)
be such that
\lim_{N\to\infty}L(N)=\infty
and
\lim_{N\to\infty}L(N)/N=0
. For
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
t_{k},\ldots,t_{0}\in(0,\infty)
, set
\bar{t}=N^{k}L(N)+\sum_{n=0}^{k}N^{n}t_{n}
.

(a)

For
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

(4.25)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathcal{L}\left[\big(\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{\mathrm{eff},(l),\Omega_{N}}(\bar{t}\,)\big)_{l=k+1,k,\ldots,0}\right]=\mathcal{L}\left[(M^{\mathrm{eff},k}_{-l})_{-l=-(k+1),-k,\ldots,0}\right].

(b)

For
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

(4.26)

\displaystyle l>k\colon\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}\mathcal{L}\left[\left(\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{\mathrm{eff},(l),\Omega_{N}}(\bar{t}+N^{k}t)\right)_{t>0}\right]=\delta_{(\vartheta_{l},\theta_{y_{l}})},

\displaystyle l=k\colon\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}\mathcal{L}\left[\left(\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{\mathrm{eff},(l),\Omega_{N}}(\bar{t}+N^{l}t)\right)_{t>0}\right]=\mathcal{L}\left[\left(z^{\mathrm{eff}}_{k,M^{\mathrm{eff},k}_{-(k+1),x}}(t)\right)_{t>0}\right],

\displaystyle l<k\colon\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}\mathcal{L}\left[\left(\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{\mathrm{eff},(l),\Omega_{N}}(\bar{t}+N^{l}t)\right)_{t>0}\right]=\mathcal{L}\left[\left(z^{\mathrm{eff}}_{l,M^{\mathrm{eff},k}_{-(l+1),x}}(t)\right)_{t>0}\right],

where the initial laws of the limiting processes are given by (see Fig.
8
)

(4.27)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\left[z^{\mathrm{eff}}_{k,M^{k}_{-(k+1),x}}(0)\right]=\Gamma^{\mathrm{eff},(k)}_{M^{k}_{-(k+1),x}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\left[z^{\mathrm{eff}}_{k,M^{k}_{-(k+1),x}}(0)\right]=\Gamma^{\mathrm{eff},(k)}_{M^{k}_{-(l+1),x}},

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\mathrm{eff},(l)}_{M^{k}_{-(l+1)}}=\int_{[0,1]^{2}}\cdots\int_{[0,1]^{2}}\Gamma^{\mathrm{eff},(k)}_{M^{k}_{-(k+1)}}({\rm d}u_{k})\cdots\Gamma^{\mathrm{eff},(l+1)}_{u_{l+2}}({\rm d}u_{l+1})\Gamma^{\mathrm{eff},(l)}_{u_{l+1}}

Theorem
4.2
can be interpreted as follows. The statement in (a) shows that if we look at the effective process on multiple space-time scales simultaneously, then the joint distribution of the different block averages is the law of the two-dimensional interaction chain defined in (
4.21
) and depicted in Fig.
8
. Note that the process
(\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{\mathrm{eff},(l),\Omega_{N}}(\bar{t}+N^{l}t))_{t>0}
has at each level a different colour seed-bank average as second component, which is called
the effective seed-bank
. The statement in (b) describes the law of the path on different time scales.

•

On time scale
\bar{t}+N^{k}t
the
l
-block averages with
l>k
are not moving, i.e.,
(\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{\mathrm{eff},(l),\Omega_{N}}(\bar{t}+N^{k}t))_{t>0}
converges to the constant process taking the value
(\vartheta_{l},\theta_{y_{l}})=\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{\mathrm{eff},(l),\Omega_{N}}(0)
.

•

On time scale
\bar{t}+N^{k}t
the
k
-block averages have reached equilibrium. The full
k
-block average feels a drift towards the full
(k+1)
-block average, which is still in its initial state
\vartheta_{k}
. Therefore migration between the
k
-blocks in the hierarchical mean-field limit is replaced by a drift towards
\vartheta_{k}
, and the
k
-blocks become independent. This phenomenon is called
decoupling
(or ‘propagation of chaos’). The resampling function for the full estimator converges to
\mathcal{F}^{(k)}g
(see (
4.16
)), the average diffusion function of the
k
-blocks. Finally, the full
k
-block exchanges individuals with the
k
-dormant population. Hence the
k
-dormant population is the effective seed-bank on space-timescale
k
Both the migration and the renormalisation are qualitatively similar to that of the hierarchical system without seed-bank
[
DG93b
]
. However, the seed-bank still quantitatively affects the migration and the resampling through the slowing-down factor
E_{k}
. (In Section
4.4
we will see how the latter arises.)

•

On time scale
\bar{t}+N^{l}t
the
l
-block averages with
l<k
are in a
quasi-equilibrium
. The full
l
-block averages feel a drift towards the instantaneous value of the
(l+1)
-block average at time
\bar{t}
. Therefore also the
l
-block averages decouple. The
(l+1)
-block average is not moving on time scale
\bar{t}+N^{l}t
, and so for
t=L(N)
we see that the
l
-block averages equilibrate faster than the
(l+1)
-block averages moves. The resampling function is given by
\mathcal{F}^{(l)}g
, which is to be interpreted as the average diffusion function of the
l
-blocks. The full average interacts with the
l
-blocks of the
l
-dormant population, which is the effective seed-bank on level
l
. Again the full
l
-block average feels a slowing-down factor
E_{l}
.

Note that Theorem
4.2
only describes the limiting process of the combined block average
\bar{\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}}
and the effective seed-bank
\Theta_{y_{l}}^{(l),\Omega_{N}}
. It does not provide a full description of the system, which is in Theorem
4.4
below. We will see later that Theorem
4.2
does describe all the
non-trivial
components of the system.

Remark 4.3
.

[Quasi equilibria]

Note that Theorem
4.2
does not depend on the choice of
t_{k},\ldots,t_{0}\in(0,\infty)
. Since at each level
0\leq l\leq k
we start from time
\bar{t}
, the
l
-block averages have already reached a quasi-equilibrium.

\blacksquare

Figure 9:
Full interaction chain.

4.3.2
Full process

To state our second main theorem, we will again use the process and the notation as defined in Section
4.2
.

Theorem 4.4
.

[Hierarchical mean-field: full process]

Suppose that the initial state is given by (
2.21
). Let
L(N)
be such that
\lim_{N\to\infty}L(N)=\infty
and
\lim_{N\to\infty}L(N)/N=0
. For
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
t_{k},\ldots,t_{0}\in(0,\infty)
, set
\bar{t}=N^{k}L(N)+\sum_{n=0}^{k}N^{n}t_{n}
.

(a)

For
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

(4.28)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathcal{L}\left[\left(\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}}\left(\bar{t}\,\right)\right)_{l=k+1,k,\ldots,0}\right]=\mathcal{L}\left[(M^{k}_{-l})_{-l=-(k+1),-k,\ldots,0}\right].

(b)

For
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

(4.29)

\displaystyle l>k\colon\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}\mathcal{L}\left[\left(\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}}(\bar{t}+N^{k}t)\right)_{t>0}\right]=\delta_{(M^{k}_{-(k+1)})},

\displaystyle l=k\colon\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}\mathcal{L}\left[\left(\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}}(\bar{t}+N^{l}t)\right)_{t>0}\right]=\mathcal{L}\left[\left(z_{k,M^{k}_{-(k+1)}}(t)\right)_{t>0}\right],

\displaystyle l<k\colon\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}\mathcal{L}\left[\left(\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}}(\bar{t}+N^{l}t)\right)_{t>0}\right]=\mathcal{L}\left[\left(z_{l,M^{k}_{-(l+1)}}(t)\right)_{t>0}\right],

\displaystyle\text{in the Meyer-Zheng topology},

where the initial laws of the limiting processes are given by (see Fig.
9
)

(4.30)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\left[z_{k,M^{k}_{-(k+1),x}}(0)\right]=\Gamma^{(k)}_{M^{k}_{-(k+1)}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\left[z_{k,M^{k}_{-(k+1),x}}(0)\right]=\Gamma^{(l)}_{M^{k}_{-(l+1)}},

\displaystyle\Gamma^{(l)}_{M^{k}_{-(l+1)}}=\int_{\mathfrak{s}}\cdots\int_{\mathfrak{s}}\int_{\mathfrak{s}}\Gamma^{(k)}_{M^{k}_{-(k+1)}}({\rm d}u_{k})\Gamma^{(k-1)}_{u_{k}}({\rm d}u_{k-1})\cdots\Gamma^{(l+1)}_{l+2}({\rm d}u_{1})\Gamma^{(l)}_{u_{l+1}}

Remark 4.5
.

[convergence in the Meyer-Zheng topology]

Note that Theorem
4.4
(b) is stated in the Meyer-Zheng topology. This topology is needed because at time-scales
N^{l}t
rates occur in(
2.24
) that tend to infinity as
N\to\infty
. In Section
4.4
we define the Meyer-Zheng topology and explain why it is neeeded.

\blacksquare

The statement in (a) shows that if we look at multiple space-time scales simultaneously, then the joint distribution of the different block averages behaves like the infinite-dimensional interaction chain defined in (
4.18
). The statement in (b) describes the law of the path on different times scales.

•

On time scale
N^{k}t
, the
l
-block averages with
l>k
are not moving, i.e.,
(\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}}(\bar{t}+N^{k}t))_{t>0}
is a constant process. However, there is a difference between seed-banks with colour
m>k
and seed-banks with colour
0\leq m\leq k
in the way they interact with the active population. For
m>k
, even the
m
-dormant single colonies have not yet moved at time
\bar{t}+N^{k}t
, and hence are still in their initial states, with expectations
(\theta_{y_{m}})_{m=l+1}^{\infty}
. Therefore, also the
l
-block averages of
m
-dormant populations are still in their initial states, with expectations
(\theta_{y_{m}})_{m=l+1}^{\infty}
. For
l\leq k
the
m
-dormant single colonies with
m\leq k
