at time
\bar{t}+N^{k}t
have already interacted with the active population. Due to this interaction, for
l>k
the
l
-block averages of
m
-dormant populations with
m\leq k
are in state
\vartheta_{k}
instead of their initial state
\theta_{y_{m}}
. However, on time scale
\bar{t}+N^{k}t

l
-block averages of
m
-dormant populations are not moving. (In Section
4.4
we explain how the shift from
\theta_{y_{m}}
to
\vartheta_{k}
occurs.) Also the
l
-block averages of the active population are in state
\vartheta_{k}
.

•

On time scale
\bar{t}+N^{k}t
, the
k
-block averages have reached equilibrium. We see that the active
k
-block average and the
k
-dormant
k
-block average evolve together like the effective
k
-block process in Theorem
4.2
. Therefore the evolution of the active
k
-block average is slowed down by a factor
E_{k}
, the active
k
-block feels a drift towards
\vartheta_{k}
(the value of the active
(k+1)
-block average at time
\bar{t}
), resamples with diffusion function
\mathcal{F}^{(k)}g
, and exchanges individuals with the
k
-dormant
k
-block. The
k
-dormant
k
-block average evolves only via interaction with the active
k
-block. The
m
-dormant
k
-block averages with colour
0\leq m<k
equal the active
k
-block average and hence follow their evolution. The
m
-dormant
k
-blocks with colour
m>k
are still in their initial states, since on time scale
\bar{t}+N^{k}t
even single colony seed-banks with colour
m>k
have not yet started to interact with the active population.

•

On time scale
\bar{t}+N^{l}t
, for
0\leq l<k
, the
l
-block averages are in a quasi-equilibrium. Again, the active
l
-block and the
l
-dormant
l
-block average, which is the effective seed-bank, behave as the effective process in
4.2
. Hence, the active
l
-block average feels a drift towards the instantaneous value of the active
(l+1)
-block average, which is given by the first component of the interaction chain
M^{k}_{-(l+1)}
, resamples according to the renormalised diffusion function
\mathcal{F}^{(l)}g
, and exchanges with the
l
-block of the
l
-dormant population. The evolution of the active
l
-block average is slowed down by a factor
E_{l}
. The
l
-block of the
l
-dormant population exchanges individuals with the active population. The
l
-blocks of the
m
-dormant population with colours
0\leq m<l
follow the active population. The states of the
m
-dormant population with colour
m>l
are given by the corresponding components in the interaction chain
M^{k}_{-(l+1)}
. Hence the
l
-block averages with colours
m>k
are still in their initial states
\theta_{y_{m}}
, because on time scale
\bar{t}+N^{l}t
even the single dormant colonies with colour
m>k
have not yet interacted with the active population. However, something interesting is happening with the colours
l<m\leq k
: they are in a state obtained on the time scale in which they where effective, i.e., for
l<m\leq k
the
m
-dormant
l
-block average is in state
y_{m,m}(\bar{t}\,)
. This happens because at time
\bar{t}
the single colonies have already interacted with the active population, but on time scale
N^{l}t
they do not interact anymore with the active population. (Also this effect will be further explained in Section
4.4
.)

Remark 4.6
.

[Comparison to the hierachical multi-scale limit without seed-bank]

Comparing Theorem
4.4
with the multi-scale limit theorems derived for the hierarchical system without seed-bank
[
DG93b
]
,
[
DG93a
]
,
[
DGV95
]
, we see that the seed-bank affects the system both quantitatively and qualitatively. First, the active population is
slowed down
by the total size of the seed-banks it has interacted with, represented by the slowing-down factors
(E_{l})_{l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
. Second, the interaction with the
effective seed-bank
on each space-time scale is special to the system with seed-bank. Still, the
decoupling
of the active component and the
renormalisation transformation
for the diffusion function are similar as in the system without seed-bank.

Remark 4.7
.

[
k\to\infty
limit of the interaction chain]

The result in (
4.26
) raises the question how the hierarchical multi-scale limit behaves for large
k
. We find the following dichotomy:

(4.31)

\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathcal{L}\left[(M^{k}_{-(k+1),-k,\cdots,0})\right]=\mathcal{L}\left[(M^{\infty}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{-}}\right],

where in the
clustering regime

(4.32)

\mathcal{L}\left[(M^{\infty}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{-}}\right]=\theta\delta_{(1,1^{\mathbb{N}_{0}})^{\mathbb{Z}^{-}}}+(1-\theta)\delta_{(0,0^{\mathbb{N}_{0}})^{\mathbb{Z}^{-}}}

and in the
coexistence regime

M^{\infty}=(M_{k}^{\infty})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{-}}
is a realisation of the unique entrance law of the interaction chain at time
-\infty
with

(4.33)

\lim_{l\to\infty}M_{-l}^{\infty}=(\theta,\theta).

In the latter case,
M^{\infty}
corresponds to the equilibrium vector of block averages around site
0
, whose law agrees with that of the equilibrium block averages for the mean-field model after we take the limit
N\to\infty
(see
[
DGV95
, Proposition 6.2 and 6.3]
).

\blacksquare

Remark 4.8
.

[Interaction field]

Theorem
4.4
looks at the tower of block averages over a fixed site, namely,
0
. In order to study the cluster formation in the clustering regime or the equilibria in the coexistence regime, we must analyse the dependence structure between the towers of block averages over
different
sites. We can show that, in the limit as
N\to\infty
, an interacting random field emerges, indexed by a tree with countably many edges coming out of every site at every level. This random field has the property that the averages over any two points
\eta,\eta^{\prime}
with
d(\eta,\eta^{\prime})=l
, follow a single interaction chain in equilibrium from
k+1
until
l
(or from
-\infty
until
l
in the entrance law) and, conditional on the state in
l
, evolve independently as the interaction chain beyond
l
. This corresponds to what is called
propagation of chaos
of the
(l-1)
-block averages given the
l
-block average. For the model without seed-bank such results are described in
[
DGV95
, Section 0(e)]
. Using our results for the model with seed-bank above, we can in principle follow an analogous line of argument. We refrain from spelling out the details.

\blacksquare

4.4
Heuristics behind the multi-scale limit

The proofs of Theorems
4.2
and
4.4
written out in Sections
7
–
12
, are long and technical. In order to help the reader appreciate these proofs, we provide the heuristics in Sections
4.4.1
–
4.4.3
.

4.4.1
Evolution of the block-averages

Recall the block averages introduced in Definition
2.3
and their evolution defined in (
2.24
). In the limit as
N\to\infty
, we heuristically obtain from the SSDE in (
2.24
) the following results for the
k
-block process

(4.34)

({x}_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t),({y}_{m,k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t))_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}})_{t\geq 0}.

•

Migration.
Recall that the migration is captured by the first term of the first equation in (
2.24
), i.e., the first term of the evolution of the active part of the population. Letting
N\to\infty
, we see that in the sum over
l
only the term
l=1
contributes. Hence we expect that the effective migration felt by the active
k
-block average is towards
{x}_{k+1}^{\Omega_{N}}(0)
, the initial state of the active
(k+1)
-block average. Note that the migration term in (
2.24
) can be written as

(4.35)

\sum_{l\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{c_{k+l-1}}{N^{l-1}}\big[{x}_{k+l}^{\Omega_{N}}(N^{-l}t)-{x}_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)\big]\,{\rm d}t=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{c_{k+l-1}}{N^{l-1}}\left[\frac{1}{N^{l}}\sum_{k=0}^{N^{l}-1}{x}_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)-{x}_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)\right]\,{\rm d}t.

The drift towards the
(k+1)
-block average is therefore also a drift towards the current average of the
k
-blocks in the
(k+1)
-block. In the limit as
N\to\infty
, the latter can be approximated by
\mathbb{E}[x_{k}(t)]
. Effectively, as
N\to\infty
, the
k
-blocks become independent given the the value of
{x}_{k+1}^{\Omega_{N}}(0)
, i.e., there is
decoupling
.

•

Resampling.
Recall that the diffusion term in the evolution equation of the active population represents the resampling. Therefore we see that the active
k
-block resamples at a rate that is the average resampling rate over the
k
-block. For
k=1
, the resampling rate of the
1
-block is the average of the resampling ratse of the single colonies. Therefore, in the limit
N\to\infty
, due to the decoupling described above, we expect that the resampling rate for the
1
block is given by
\mathbb{E}[g]
, where the expectation is w.r.t. the quasi-equilibrium of the single colonies. This expectation is exaclty the renormalised diffusion function
\mathcal{F}g
(see (
4.16
)). For the
k
-block, we may interpret the diffusion function to be the average of the diffusion function for the
(k-1)
-blocks. By “induction” we assume that the
(k-1)
blocks resample at rate
\mathcal{F}^{(k-1)}g
. Hence, due to the decoupling of the
(k-1)
-blocks as
N\to\infty
, we expect the resampling rate for the
k
blocks to equal
\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{F}^{(k-1)}g]
, where the expectation is w.r.t. the quasi-equlibrium of the
(k-1)
-blocks. This yields another iteration of the renormalisation transformation (see (
4.17
)). Hence, we expect the diffusion function for the
k
-blocks to converge to
\mathcal{F}^{(k)}g
.

•

Exchange with the seed-bank.
Recall that the last term of the first equation in (
2.24
) and the second equation in (
2.24
) together describe the exchange of the active
k
-block with the
m
-dormant
k
-block. To describe the limiting behaviour as
N\to\infty
, we distinguish three cases:
0\leq m<k
,
m=k
,
m>k
.

–

If
0\leq m<k
, then we see that the rate of exchange between the active
k
-block and the
m
-dormant
k
-block tends to infinity as
N\to\infty
. We therefore expect them to equalise, i.e.,

(4.36)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathcal{L}\left[\left({x}_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)-{y}_{m,k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)\right)_{t>0}\right]=\delta_{0},

where
0
should be interpreted as the process equal to
0
,
(0)_{t>0}
.
Hence we see that
m
-dormant
k
-block follows the active
k
-block immediately. (To formalise this fact, we need the
Meyer-Zheng topology

[
MZ84
]
.)

–

If
m=k
, then there is a non-trivial exchange between the active
k
-block and the
k
-dormant
k
-block.

–

If
m>k
, then the exchange rate between the active
k
-block and the
m
-dormant
k
-block tends to zero as
N\to\infty
.

Thus, only the
k
-dormant
k
-block has a non-trivial interaction with the active
k
-block. We express this by saying that on space-time scale
k
the
k
-dormant population plays the role of the
effective seed-bank
.

4.4.2
Limiting evolution of the block-averages

To determine the limiting evolution of the full block-averages process, we first have a look at the limiting evolution of the effective process.

The effective process.

To determine the limit as
N\to\infty
of (
2.24
), we need to get rid of the diverging rates. Instead of only looking at the
k
-block process
({x}_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t),({y}_{m,k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t))_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}})_{t\geq 0}
, which evolves according to (
2.24
), we look at the
effective
k
-block process
defined as

(4.37)

\left(\bar{x}_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t),y_{k,k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0},

where we abbreviate

(4.38)

??=\frac{x_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)+\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}K_{m}y_{m,k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)}{1+\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}K_{m}}.

By (
4.36
) and the heuristic discussion given above, the process in (
4.37
) equals
(x_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t),y_{k,k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t))_{t\geq 0}
in the limit as
N\to\infty
, i.e., it describes the joint distribution of the active
k
-block and the effective dormant
k
-block, which is the
k
-dormant
k
-block. Using (
2.24
), we see that the process in (
4.37
) evolves according to the SSDE

(4.39)

\displaystyle{\rm d}\bar{x}_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)

\displaystyle=E_{k}\sum_{l\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{c_{k+l-1}}{N^{l-1}}\big[{x}_{k+l}^{\Omega_{N}}(N^{-l}t)-{x}_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)\big]\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle+E_{k}\sqrt{\frac{1}{N^{k}}\sum_{\xi\in B_{k}(0)}g(x_{\xi}(N^{k}t))}\,\,{\rm d}w_{k}(t)

\displaystyle+E_{k}\sum_{m=k}^{\infty}N^{k}\frac{K_{m}e_{m}}{N^{m}}\big[{y}_{m,k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)-{x}_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)\big]\,{\rm d}t,

\displaystyle{\rm d}{y}_{k,k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)

\displaystyle=e_{k}\big[{x}_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)-{y}_{k,k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)\big]\,{\rm d}t.

In (
4.39
) no infinite rates appear anymore. In the limit as
N\to\infty
, by (
4.36
) we can approximate

(4.40)

x_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)\approx y_{m,k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t),\qquad 0\leq m<k,

such that

(4.41)

x_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)\approx\bar{x}_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t).

We can therefore approximate (
4.39
) by

(4.42)

\displaystyle{\rm d}\bar{x}_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)

\displaystyle=E_{k}\sum_{l\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{c_{k+l-1}}{N^{l-1}}\big[\bar{x}_{k+l}^{\Omega_{N}}(N^{-l}t)-\bar{x}_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)\big]\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle+E_{k}\sqrt{\frac{1}{N^{k}}\sum_{\xi\in B_{k}(0)}g\big(\bar{x}_{\xi}(N^{k}t)\big)}\,\,{\rm d}w_{k}(t)

\displaystyle+E_{k}\sum_{m=k}^{\infty}N^{k}\frac{K_{m}e_{m}}{N^{m}}\big[{y}_{m,k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)-\bar{x}_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)\big]\,{\rm d}t,

\displaystyle{\rm d}{y}_{k,k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)

\displaystyle=e_{k}\big[\bar{x}_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)-{y}_{k,k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)\big]\,{\rm d}t.

Hence, in the limit as
N\to\infty
, the process in (
4.37
) becomes autonomous. Moreover, assuming that
\lim_{N\to\infty}\bar{x}_{l+1}^{\Omega_{N}}(0)=\vartheta_{l}
, we see that (
4.42
) approaches the effective process defined in (
4.10
), with

(4.43)

\theta=\vartheta_{l},\quad E=E_{k},\quad c=c_{k},\quad e=e_{k},\quad K=K_{k},\quad g=\mathcal{F}^{(k)}g.

In particular, we see that the
slowing-down constant

E_{k}
arises because the active population is the only part of the first component of (
4.37
). Note that therefore only a part, the active part, from the first component migrates, resamples and exchanges with the seed-bank. Due to the infinite rates, the active population “drags along all the fast seed-banks with total size
\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}K_{m}
”. This causes the slowing down factors
E_{k}
.

Since there are no infinite rates in the evolution of the effective process, we can use the classical path space topology. This allows us in the proof in Sections
7
–
12
to build on techniques developed for the hierarchical mean-field model without seed-bank in
[
DG93a
]
,
[
DGV95
]
. It turns out that the
effective process
is very useful in our analysis.

From the effective process to the full process.

For large
N
, by (
4.40
) and (
4.41
), the evolution of our original process
({x}_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t),({y}_{m,k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t))_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}})_{t\geq 0}
can be approximated by

(4.44)

\displaystyle{\rm d}\bar{x}_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)

\displaystyle\approx E_{k}\sum_{l\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{c_{k+l-1}}{N^{l-1}}\big[\bar{x}_{k+l}^{\Omega_{N}}(N^{-l}t)-\bar{x}_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)\big]\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle+E_{k}\sqrt{\frac{1}{N^{k}}\sum_{\xi\in B_{k}(0)}g(\bar{x}_{\xi}(N^{k}t))}\,\,{\rm d}w_{k}(t)

\displaystyle+E_{k}\sum_{m=k}^{\infty}N^{k}\frac{K_{m}e_{m}}{N^{m}}\big[{y}_{m,k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)-\bar{x}_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)\big]\,{\rm d}t,

\displaystyle{y}_{k,k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)

\displaystyle=\bar{x}_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t),

\displaystyle{\rm d}{y}_{k,k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)

\displaystyle=e_{k}\big[\bar{x}_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)-{y}_{k,k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)\big]\,{\rm d}t,

\displaystyle{y}_{k,k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)

\displaystyle={y}_{m,k}^{\Omega_{N}}(0).

By the ergodic theorem for exchangeable measures, we can assume that
\lim_{N\to\infty}\bar{x}_{k+1}^{\Omega_{N}}(0)=\vartheta_{k}\,a.s.
. We expect that (
4.44
) approaches (
4.8
) with

(4.45)

\theta=\vartheta_{k},\quad E=E_{k},\quad c=c_{k},\quad e=e_{k},\quad K=K_{k},\quad g=\mathcal{F}^{(k)}g.

To prove that

(4.46)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathcal{L}[{y}_{m,k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)]=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathcal{L}[\bar{x}_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)],\qquad 0\leq m<k-1,

we need
the Meyer-Zheng topology
explained in Section
4.1
. In the proof in Sections
8
–
12
we show how the above approximations can be made rigorous.

Conserved quantities.

Note that, by (
2.24
) and (
4.35
), for each
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

(4.47)

\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{x^{\Omega_{N}}_{k}(t)+\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}K_{m}y^{\Omega_{N}}_{k}(t)}{1+\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}K_{m}}\right]=\theta,\qquad t\geq 0,

is a conserved quantity. For each
k\in\mathbb{N}
we obtain that for
l\geq k

(4.48)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{x^{\Omega_{N}}_{k}(t)+\sum_{m=0}^{l}K_{m}y^{\Omega_{N}}_{k}(t)}{1+\sum_{m=0}^{l}K_{m}}\right]=\vartheta_{l},

is a conserved quantity. For the effective process, (
4.42
) implies that

(4.49)

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\mathbb{E}[\bar{x}_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)]

\displaystyle=E_{k}K_{k}e_{k}\,\Big(\mathbb{E}[y_{k,k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)]-\mathbb{E}[\bar{x}_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)]\Big),

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\mathbb{E}[y_{k,k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)]

\displaystyle=e_{k}\,\Big(\mathbb{E}[\bar{x}_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)]-\mathbb{E}[y_{k,k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)]\Big).

Recall that

(4.50)

\mathbb{E}[\bar{x}_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(0)]=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{x_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(0)+\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}K_{m}y_{m,k}^{\Omega_{N}}(0)}{1+\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}K_{m}}\right]=\vartheta_{k-1},\qquad\mathbb{E}[y_{k,k}^{\Omega_{N}}(0)]=\theta_{y_{k}}.

Therefore we can solve (
4.49
) explicitly as

(4.51)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\bar{x}_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)]

\displaystyle=\vartheta_{k}+\frac{E_{k}K_{k}}{1+E_{k}K_{k}}(\vartheta_{k-1}-\theta_{y_{k}})\,{\rm e}^{-(E_{k}K_{k}+1)e_{k}t},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[y_{k,k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)]

\displaystyle=\vartheta_{k}-\frac{1}{1+E_{k}K_{k}}(\vartheta_{k-1}-\theta_{y_{k}})\,{\rm e}^{-(E_{k}K_{k}+1)e_{k}t}.

The above computation shows what happens to
\mathbb{E}[\bar{x}_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)]
if we move one space-time scale up in the hierarchy, namely, a new seed-bank starts interacting with the active population. This causes that
\vartheta_{k-1}
is pulled a bit towards
\theta_{y_{k}}
, so that also
\mathbb{E}[\bar{x}_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)]
changes a bit. Each new seed-bank that opens up changes the expectation of the active population, which results in the sequence
(\theta_{x},\vartheta_{0},\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2},\ldots)
for the expectation of the active population on space-time scales
\{0,1,2,3,\cdots\}
. From (
4.35
) we see that the drift of
\bar{x}_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)
is towards

(4.52)

\bar{x}_{k+1}^{\Omega_{N}}(N^{-1}t)=\frac{1}{N^{k}}\sum_{k=0}^{N^{k}-1}{x}_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)\approx\mathbb{E}[\bar{x}_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)],

where the last approximation can be made because the
k
-blocks decouple. Hence, in the limit as
N\to\infty
, once the
k
-blocks are in a quasi-equilibrium we can replace the drift towards
\bar{x}_{k+1}^{\Omega_{N}}(N^{-1}t)
by a drift towards
\mathbb{E}[\bar{x}_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}(t)]=\vartheta_{k}
.

Shifting averages.

Recall the full estimator process
(\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}}(t))_{t>0}
defined in (
4.12
). Equation
2.23
implies that the evolution of the estimator process is given by

(4.53)

\displaystyle{\rm d}\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{x}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=l+1}^{\infty}\frac{c_{n-1}}{N^{n-1}}[\Theta^{(n),\Omega_{N}}_{x}(t)-\Theta^{(l)}_{x}(t)]\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle+\sqrt{\frac{1}{N^{2l}}\sum_{\xi\in B_{l}}g\big(x_{\xi}(t)\big)}\,{\rm d}w(t)

\displaystyle+\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\frac{K_{m}e_{m}}{N^{m}}[\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{y_{m}}(t)-\Theta^{(1),\Omega_{N}}_{x}(t)]\,{\rm d}t,

\displaystyle{\rm d}\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{y_{m}}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{e_{m}}{N^{m}}[\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{x}(t)-\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{y_{m}}(t)]\,{\rm d}t,\,m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

Looking at the estimator process
(\mathbf{\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}}(t))_{t>0}
on time scale
N^{k}t
, we see that

(4.54)

\displaystyle{\rm d}\Theta_{x}^{(l),\Omega_{N}}(N^{k}t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=l+1}^{\infty}\frac{c_{n-1}}{N^{n-1-k}}\left[\Theta_{x}^{(n),\Omega_{N}}(N^{k}t)-\Theta_{x}^{(l),\Omega_{N}}(N^{k}t)\right]\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle+\sqrt{\frac{N^{k}}{N^{2l}}\sum_{\xi\in B_{l}}g\big(x_{\xi}(N^{k}t)\big)}\,{\rm d}w(t)

\displaystyle+\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\frac{K_{m}e_{m}}{N^{m-k}}\left[\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{y_{m}}(N^{k}t)-\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{x}(N^{k}t)\right]\,{\rm d}t,

\displaystyle{\rm d}\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{y_{m}}(N^{k}t)

\displaystyle=\frac{e_{m}}{N^{m-k}}\left[\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{x}(N^{k}t)-\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{y_{m}}(N^{k}t)\right]\,{\rm d}t,\qquad m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

From (
4.54
) we get that, on time scale
N^{k}t
, for all
l\geq k+1
,

(4.55)

\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}(N^{k}t)=\frac{\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{x}(N^{k}t)+\sum_{m=0}^{l-1}K_{m}\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{y_{m}}(N^{k}t)}{1+\sum_{m=0}^{l-1}K_{m}},\qquad t\geq 0,

is a conserved quantity in the limit as
N\to\infty
, and for
t\geq 0
,

(4.56)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}(N^{k}t)=\frac{\theta_{x}+\sum_{m=0}^{l-1}K_{m}\theta_{y_{m}}}{1+\sum_{m=0}^{l-1}K_{m}}=\vartheta_{l},\text{ in probability}.

For
m>k
, also
\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{y_{m}}(N^{k}t)
is a conserved quantity, and

(4.57)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{y_{m}}(N^{k}t)=\theta_{y_{m}},\qquad t\geq 0.

However, for
l\geq k+1
,
\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{x}(N^{k}t)
and
(\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{y_{m}}(N^{k}t))_{m=0}^{k}
are not conserved quantities in the limit as
N\to\infty
. Note that from
2.24
we heuristically see that the full
l
-block estimator process
({\bf\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}}(N^{k}t))_{t\geq 0}
with
l>k
converges to the process

(4.58)

\left(\Theta^{(l)}_{x}(t),\left(\Theta^{(l)}_{y_{m}}(t)\right)_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\right)_{t>0},

which evolves according to

(4.59)

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle d}\Theta^{(l)}_{x}(t)=E_{k-1}K_{k}e_{k}[\Theta^{(l)}_{y_{k}}(t)-\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{x}(t)]\,{\rm d}t,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{llll}\Theta^{(l)}_{y_{m}}(t)&=&\Theta^{(l)}_{x}(t),&m<k,\\
{\rm d}\Theta^{(l)}_{y_{k}}(t)&=&e_{k}[\Theta^{(l)}_{x}(t)-\Theta^{(l)}_{y_{k}}(t)]\,{\rm d}t,&m=k,\\
\Theta^{(l)}_{y_{m}}(t)&=&\theta_{y_{m}},&m>k.\end{array}

This system can be explicitly solved as

(4.60)

\displaystyle\Theta^{(l)}_{x}(t)=\frac{\Theta^{(l)}_{x}(0)+E_{k-1}K_{k}\Theta^{(l)}_{y_{k}}(0)}{1+E_{k-1}K_{k}}

\displaystyle+\frac{E_{k-1}K_{k}}{1+E_{k-1}K_{k}}[\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{x}(0)-\Theta^{(l)}_{y_{k}}(0)]\,{\rm e}^{-(E_{k-1}K_{k}e_{k}+e_{k})t},

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{llll}\Theta^{(l)}_{y_{m}}(t)&=&\Theta^{(l)}_{x}(t),&m<k,\\[5.69046pt]
\Theta^{(l)}_{y_{k}}(t)&=&\frac{\Theta^{(l)}_{x}(0)+E_{k-1}K_{k}\Theta^{(l)}_{y_{k}}(0)}{1+E_{k-1}K_{k}}\\[5.69046pt]
&&\qquad-\frac{1}{1+E_{k-1}K_{k}}[\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{x}(0)-\Theta^{(l)}_{y_{k}}(0)]\,{\rm e}^{-(E_{k-1}K_{k}e_{k}+e_{k})t},&m=k,\\
\Theta^{(l)}_{y_{m}}(t)&=&\theta_{y_{m}},&m>k.\end{array}

The latter shows that, each time we enter a new space-time scale, all the large active blocks interact with the large effective dormant blocks until they equalise. Thus, on each space-time scale, all the active
l
-blocks and the dormant
l
-blocks of colour
m\leq l
move for a short period of time. As a consequence, the value of
\Theta_{x}^{(l)}(0)
depends on the scaling we choose. To illustrate this, we note that

(4.61)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\Theta_{x}^{(l),\Omega_{N}}(0)

\displaystyle=\theta_{x},

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\Theta_{x}^{(l),\Omega_{N}}(L(N)+t)

\displaystyle=\vartheta_{0},

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\Theta_{x}^{(l),\Omega_{N}}((L(N)N^{n}+N^{n}t))

\displaystyle=\vartheta_{n},\quad 0\leq n\leq k.

However, the scaling
N^{k}t
would imply that

(4.62)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\Theta_{x}^{(l),\Omega_{N}}(0)

\displaystyle=\theta_{x},

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\Theta_{x}^{(l),\Omega_{N}}\left(N^{k}\frac{L(N)+t}{N^{k}}\right)

\displaystyle=\vartheta_{0},

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\Theta_{x}^{(l),\Omega_{N}}\left(N^{k}\frac{L(N)N^{n}+N^{n}t}{N^{k}}\right)

\displaystyle=\vartheta_{n},\quad 0\leq n\leq k.

Hence, if
(t(N))_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence such that
\lim_{N\to\infty}N^{k}t(N)=0
, then the value of the limit

(4.63)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{x}(N^{k}t(N))

depends on
(t(N))_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
. Moreover, to obtain a limiting process for
({\bf\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}}(N^{k}t))_{t\geq 0}

we need convergence also at time
0
, while it is not clear what
N^{k}t\downarrow 0
means. To circumvent these subtleties, we look at the process at times
t>0
and use as starting time
\bar{t}
defined in Theorem
4.2
.

From (
4.60
) it follows that, for
l\geq k
,

(4.64)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{x}(\bar{t}\,)

\displaystyle=\vartheta_{k},\text{ in probability},

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{y_{m}}(\bar{t}\,)

\displaystyle=\vartheta_{k},\quad m\leq k,\text{ in probability},

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{y_{m}}(\bar{t}\,)

\displaystyle=\theta_{y_{m}},\quad m>k\text{ in probability}.

Note that the shifting of averages mentioned earlier is closely related to the conserved quantities discussed in Section
4.4.2
because, for large
N
,

(4.65)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{x}(\bar{t}\,)\approx\mathbb{E}[x_{k}^{\Omega_{N}}],

where the expectation is taken in the quasi-equilibrium the
k
-blocks have attained after scaling with time
\bar{t}
.

4.4.3
Formation of the interaction chain

In Section
4.4.1
we saw how subsequent space-time scales are connected via the migration term. In this section we show how the interaction chain arises from this connection. We first show how the effective interaction chain arises for the effective process. Then we show how the full interaction chain is formed, by studying the slow seed-banks.

Connections between different space-time scales

Let
\bar{t}
be as in Theorem
4.2
. From (
4.53
) it follows that the process
({\bf\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}}(\bar{t}+N^{l}t))_{t>0}
evolves according to

(4.66)

\displaystyle{\rm d}\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{x}(\bar{t}+N^{l}t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=l+1}^{\infty}\frac{c_{n-1}}{N^{n-1-l}}[\Theta^{(n),\Omega_{N}}_{x}(\bar{t}+N^{l}t)-\Theta^{(l)}_{x}(\bar{t}+N^{l}t)]\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle+\sqrt{\frac{1}{N^{l}}\sum_{\xi\in B_{l}}g\big(x_{\xi}(\bar{t}+N^{l}t)\big)}\,{\rm d}w(t)

\displaystyle+\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\frac{K_{m}e_{m}}{N^{m-l}}[\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{y_{m}}(\bar{t}+N^{l}t)-\Theta^{(1),\Omega_{N}}_{x}(\bar{t}+N^{l}t)]\,{\rm d}t,

\displaystyle{\rm d}\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{y_{m}}(\bar{t}+N^{l}t)

\displaystyle=\frac{e_{m}}{N^{m-l}}[\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{x}(\bar{t}+N^{l}t)-\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{y_{m}}(\bar{t}+N^{l}t)]\,{\rm d}t,\qquad m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

Therefore, in the limit as
N\to\infty
, the active population
\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{x}(\bar{t}+N^{l}t)
feels a drift towards the
(l+1)
-block average
\Theta^{(l+1),\Omega_{N}}_{x}(\bar{t}+N^{l+1}t)
. If
l=k
, then

(4.67)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathcal{L}[{\bf\Theta}^{(k+1),\Omega_{N}}(\bar{t}+N^{k}t)]=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathcal{L}[{\bf\Theta}^{(k+1),\Omega_{N}}(\bar{t})],

since the
(k+1)
-block has not yet started to move at time
\bar{t}+N^{k}t
. From (
4.64
) it follows that

(4.68)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\Theta^{(k+1),\Omega_{N}}_{x}(\bar{t}+N^{k}t)=\vartheta_{k}\,in\,probability.

Therefore the drift of the active population
\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{x}(\bar{t}+N^{l}t)
is towards
\vartheta_{k}
. Since
\bar{t}>L(N)N^{k}
, the process
{\bf\Theta}^{(k),\Omega_{N}}(\bar{t}+N^{k}t)
has, in the limit
N\to\infty
, already reached its equilibrium, which is denoted by
\Gamma_{\bar{\vartheta_{k}}}
, where

(4.69)

\bar{\vartheta_{k}}=(\vartheta_{k},\overbrace{\vartheta_{k},\cdots,\vartheta_{k}}^{k+1\text{ times }},\theta_{y_{k+1}},\theta_{y_{k+2}},\cdots),

so that we recognise
(\bar{\vartheta}_{k})=M^{k}_{-(k+1)}
. From (
4.64
) with
l=k+1
we see that
(\bar{\vartheta}_{k})=M^{k}_{-(k+1)}
represents the state of
{\bf\Theta}^{(k+1),\Omega_{N}}(\bar{t})
.

If we look on time scale
\bar{t}+N^{k-1}t
, then we see that the active
(k-1)
-block averages feels a drift towards the active
k
-block average. The active
k
-block does not move on time scale
N^{k-1}
, but it has already moved at time
\bar{t}
. At time
\bar{t}
the active
k
-block has even reached its quasi-equilibrium, given by
\Gamma^{(k)}_{\vartheta_{k}}
. Thus, the drift of the active
(k-1)
-block average is towards the instantaneous state of the active
k
-block average, which has distribution
\Gamma^{(k)}_{\bar{\vartheta_{k}}}
. This explains the first step in the interaction chain.

For
0\leq l<k
, the active
l
-block average feels a drift towards the
(l+1)
-block average. The latter does not evolve on time scale
N^{l}t
, but it has already moved at time
\bar{t}
. Therefore it is no longer in its initial state, but in a quasi-equilibrium
\Gamma^{(l+2)}_{u}
, where
u
is the value of the active
(l+1)
-block averages determined via the interaction chain, recall Figure
9
. This explains how the different space-time scales are connected via the active block averages. The states of the different seed-bank averages is a little bit more complicated. Below we give a very short heuristic explanation of the different seed-banks in the interaction chain.

For the effective process
(\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{\mathrm{eff},(l),\Omega_{N}}(t))_{t>0}
instead of the full process
(\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}}(t))_{t>0}
, we can consider in (
4.53
) only the active block average and the effective seed-bank average with
m=l
(recall that the full block average equals the active block average). According to the above explanation, we have to replace
\Gamma^{(k)}_{\bar{\vartheta}_{k}}
by
\Gamma^{\mathrm{eff},(k)}_{\vartheta_{k}}
and
\Gamma^{(l)}_{u}
by
\Gamma^{\mathrm{eff},(l)}_{u}
. Hence we find the effective interaction chain defined in (
4.21
) and depicted in Figure
8
.

Slow seed-banks.

From (
4.64
) we see that if
l\geq k
and we use the scaling
\bar{t}
, then all
l
-blocks of seed-banks with colour
0\leq m\leq k
equal
\vartheta_{k}
, and all
l
-blocks of seed-banks with colour
m>k
equal their initial values
\theta_{y_{m}}
. Something interesting happens when we choose
0\leq l<k
and use the scaling
\bar{t}+N^{l}t
. The single colonies of seed-banks with colour
0\leq m<l
on time scale
\bar{t}+N^{l}t
follow the active population, and hence their
l
-block averages equal the
l
-block average of the active population. The
l
-block average of the seed-bank with colour
l
has a non-trivial interaction with the active
l
-block. The
l
-blocks of seed-banks with colour
m>k
have not yet moved and hence are still in their initial states
(\theta_{y_{m}})_{m=k+1}^{\infty}
. However, (
4.66
) implies that the single colonies of the seed-banks with colour
k\geq m>l
are not moving on time scale
\bar{t}+N^{l}t
, even though they had already moved at time
\bar{t}
. Therefore the
l
-blocks averages of seed-banks with colour
l<m\leq k
are no longer in their initial state at time
\bar{t}
. Note that they are also not in the state
\vartheta_{k}
, since this is the state of their
k
-block averages and not of their
l
-block averages. The single colony seed-banks with colour
l<m\leq k
are in the state given by

(4.70)

y_{m,0}(\bar{t}\,)=\int_{0}^{\bar{t}}{\rm d}s\,\frac{e_{m}}{N^{m}}[x_{0}(s)-y_{m,0}(s)].

Hence, for large
N
,

(4.71)

y_{m,0}(\bar{t})\approx\int_{0}^{L(N)N^{k}+N^{k}t_{k}+\cdots+N^{m}t_{m}}{\rm d}s\,\frac{e_{m}}{N^{m}}[x_{0}(s)-y_{m,0}(s)].

Similarly, for the
l
-block average with colour
m
we have

(4.72)

\displaystyle\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{y_{m}}(\bar{t}\,)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\bar{t}}{\rm d}s\,\frac{e_{m}}{N^{m}}[\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{x}(s)-\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{y_{m}}(s)]

\displaystyle\approx\int_{0}^{L(N)N^{k}+N^{k}t_{k}+\cdots+N^{m}t_{m}}{\rm d}s\,\frac{e_{m}}{N^{m}}[\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{x}(s)-\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{y_{m}}(s)].

Thus, we see that the state of
\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{y_{m}}(\bar{t})
is completely determined at time
L(N)N^{k}+N^{k}t_{k}+\cdots+N^{m}t_{m}
, i.e., the last time before
\bar{t}+N^{l}t
that the single colony seed-banks of colour
m
had an opportunity to move. Up to time
L(N)N^{k}+N^{k}t_{k}+\cdots+N^{m}t_{m}
, the single colony seed-banks with colour
m
interact at a very slow rate with the active single colonies, and similarly for the
l
-blocks. Therefore effectively the colour-
m
seed-bank interacts with a “time-average on scale
N^{m}t_{m}
” of the active population. On time scale
N^{m}t_{m}
, a single active colony migrates very fast in its
(m-1)
-block. As a consequence at time
\bar{t}+N^{m}t_{m}
individuals that start from a particular colony, e.g. site
0
, are spread uniformly over the
m
-block containing this site. Hence the interaction of a single
m
-dormant colony with the active population can be intuitively interpreted as an interaction with the active
m
-block, and similarly for an
m
-dormant
l
-block. Once we move to lower time scales, the
m
-dormant single colonies do not interact with the active colony anymore. In the detailed proofs we show that one consequence of this is that, for
l<m
,

(4.73)

\displaystyle\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{y_{m}}(\bar{t}+N^{l}t)

\displaystyle\approx\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{y_{m}}(L(N)N^{k}+N^{k}t_{k}+\cdots+N^{m}t_{m})

\displaystyle\approx\Theta^{(m),\Omega_{N}}_{y_{m}}(L(N)N^{k}+N^{k}t_{k}+\cdots+N^{m}t_{m}).

Thus, the
l
-block averages of colours
l\leq m\leq k
equal the state of the corresponding
m
-block. This is the
(m+2)
-th component of the interaction chain at level
l
.

Conclusion.

Combining the intuitive descriptions in Sections
4.4.1
-
4.4.3
, we see how Theorems
4.2
and Theorems
4.4
come about. Their proofs will rely on coupling techniques and a detailed analysis of the SSDEs. This analysis will be done in several steps. In Sections
7
–
9
we first deal with a simplified system, the mean-field model, for which we derive the McKean-Vlasov limit and the mean-field finite-systems scheme. In Sections
10
–
12
we extend our analysis to finitely many hierarchical levels. In particular, we go through the following list of systems of increasing complexity, each being a simplified version of the system defined by (
2.19
) and each capturing a key feature:

1.

Two-colour mean-field finite-systems scheme (Section
10.1
).

2.

Two-level hierarchical mean-field system (Section
11.1
).

3.

Finite-level mean-field system (Section
12.1
).

In Section
12
we put the pieces together to prove the multi-scaling for the infinite-level system given in Theorems
4.2
and
4.4
.

5
Main results
N\to\infty
: Orbit and cluster formation

In the hierarchical mean-field limit we say that the system clusters when the colonies gradually form larger and larger mono-type blocks. In Section
5.1
we determine whether, in the hierarchical mean-field limit, the system clusters along successive space-time scales. How this happens is captured by the interaction chain. We introduce a sequence of scaling factors
(A_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
, where
A_{k}
is defined in terms of the rates
(c_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
,
(e_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
,
(K_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
and the factors
(E_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
. Using these scaling factors, we analyse the orbit of the renormalisation transformation and establish
universality
:
A_{k}\mathcal{F}^{(k)}g
converges as
k\to\infty
to the Fisher-Wright diffusion function, irrespective of the choice of
g
. In Section
5.2
we show how the scaling factors
A_{k}
are connected to the
growth of mono-type clusters
. In Section
5.3
we identify the asymptotics of
A_{k}
as
k\to\infty
in terms of the model parameters.

5.1
Orbit of renormalisation transformations

n+1

Q^{(n)}\left(z_{n+1},dz_{0}\right)
Q^{[n]}\left(z_{n+1},dz_{n}\right)
Q^{[n-1]}\left(z_{n},dz_{n-1}\right)
Q^{[0]}\left(z_{1},dz_{0}\right)

D_{k-1}

\mathcal{F}^{(k-1)}g
\mathcal{F}^{(k)}g

Figure 10:
Left:
The interaction chain that connects successive hierarchical levels downwards. The arrows on the right correspond to (
4.20
), the arrow on the left corresponds to (
5.1
).
Right:
The renormalisation transformation that connects successive hierarchical levels upwards. The vertical arrows correspond to (
4.17
). The horizontal arrows represent the interaction with the effective seed-bank. The arrows on the left represent the resampling driven by the renormalised diffusion function.

To determine whether clustering occurs, we start from larger and larger time scales and use the interaction chain to see whether mono-type clusters are formed in the single colonies. Recall the kernels introduced in (
4.20
) that describe the connection between subsequent hierarchical levels in the interaction chain. Define the following composition of kernels (see Fig.
10
):

(5.1)

??=Q^{[n]}\circ\cdots\circ Q^{[0]},\qquad n\in\mathbb{N}.

In words,
Q^{(n)}(z_{n},{\rm d}z_{0})
is the probability density to see the population of a single colony in state
z_{0}
given that the
(n+1)
-block average equals
z_{n}
.

In Section
3
we identified the clustering regime for fixed
N<\infty
. In this section we identify the clustering regime in the hierarchical mean-field limit. In the clustering regime, in the hierarchical mean-field limit, an interesting question is to determine how
\mathcal{F}^{(n)}g
(recall (
4.17
)) scales with
n
. We identify the scaling and show that it does
not
depend on
g
(see Fig.
11
).

To state the clustering result, abbreviate

(5.2)

??=(\vartheta_{n},\overbrace{\vartheta_{n},\cdots,\vartheta_{n}}^{n+1\text{ times }},\theta_{y_{n+1}},\theta_{y_{n+2}},\cdots).

Theorem 5.1
.

[Renormalised scaling]

Let
c_{k}
be as in (
2.5
),
e_{k}
and
K_{k}
as in (
2.10
) and
E_{k}
as in (
4.5
). Define

(5.3)

??=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{E_{k}}{c_{k}}\frac{(E_{k}c_{k}+e_{k})}{(E_{k}c_{k}+e_{k})+E_{k}K_{k}e_{k}},\qquad n\in\mathbb{N}.

Then

(5.4)

\lim_{n\to\infty}Q^{(n)}\bigl(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},\,\cdot\,\bigr)=(1-\theta)\,\delta_{(0,0^{\mathbb{N}_{0}})}+\theta\,\delta_{(1,1^{\mathbb{N}_{0}})}

if and only if

(5.5)

\lim_{n\to\infty}A_{n}=\infty.

Moreover, if (
5.5
) holds, then for all
g\in\mathcal{G}
,

(5.6)

\lim_{n\to\infty}A_{n}\mathcal{F}^{(n)}g=g_{\mathrm{FW}}\quad\text{pointwise},

with
g_{\mathrm{FW}}(x)=x(1-x)
,
x\in[0,1]
.

The proof of Theorem
5.1
is given in Section
13
. The scaling factors
A_{n}
can be interpreted as clustering coefficients: in Section
5.2
we will show that the faster the
A_{n}
grow to infinity, the faster we expect to see clusters grow. The property in (
5.4
) corresponds to the
clustering regime
. According to (
5.6
), even though
A_{n}
depends on the choice of the sequences
\underline{K},\underline{e},\underline{c}
in (
2.5
) and (
2.10
), the limit
A_{n}\mathcal{F}^{(n)}g
as
n\to\infty
is
universal
: irrespective of the choice of
g\in\mathcal{G}
, the limit is the standard Fisher-Wright diffusion function
g_{\text{FW}}
. Thus,
g_{\text{FW}}
is the
global attractor of the renormalisation transformation
(see Fig.
11
).

Figure 11:
Flow of the iterates
\mathcal{F}^{(n)}g
,
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, of the renormalisation transformation acting
on the class
\mathcal{G}
. After multiplication by
A_{n}
, the flow is globally attracted by
g_{\text{FW}}
.

5.2
Growth of mono-type clusters

In the
clustering regime
we are interested in how fast mono-type clusters grow in space over time. For the system on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
\Omega_{N}
without seed-bank the growth rate has been studied in detail. Different growth rates were found for
strongly recurrent
and
critically recurrent
migration. Typical examples on
\Omega_{N}
are migrations with coefficients
c_{k}=c^{k}
with
c\in(0,1)
, respectively,
c_{k}=C
with
C\in(0,\infty)
. Typical examples on
\mathbb{Z}
, respectively,
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
are migrations with zero mean and finite variance. For these models the following behaviour occurs.

•

In the strongly recurrent case, mono-type clusters grow fast and cover a volume that increases at time
t
at a rate that is given by the Green function up to time
t
of the underlying random walk, times a certain random constant that can be determined explicitly and that is independent of the diffusion function
g\in\mathcal{G}

[
EF96
]
,
[
Kle96
]
. The cluster growth is monitored by considering families of balls growing in time at such a speed that, starting from a translation invariant and ergodic initial state, the mean of the configuration in the ball is still close to the starting mean but begins to move.
Fast clustering
means that the cluster covers multiples of a scale that eventually lies in every finite family of balls with the above property.

•

In the critically recurrent case, the volume grows only moderately fast, like
N^{(1-U)t}
as
t\to\infty
, with
U\in[0,1]
a random variable. In other words, the cluster sizes have random orders of magnitude, an effect known as
diffusive clustering
. For
c_{k}=C\in(0,\infty)
,
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, the distribution of
U
can be identified by studying the fraction of active individuals of type
1
in a ball of size
N^{(1-u)t}
, which can be shown to converge to
V(\log\tfrac{1}{1-u})
as
t\to\infty
with
(V(s))_{s\geq 0}
the standard Fisher-Wright diffusion, irrespective of the choice of
g\in\mathcal{G}

[
FG94
]
,
[
FG96
]
.

•

For more general migration it is possible that mono-type clusters grow slower than any positive power of
t
as
t\to\infty
. This occurs for recurrent migration in which the Green function up to time
t
grows like
o(\log t)
. For this regime only few results are available
[
DGV95
]
.

From the perspective of explaining
universality
in
g\in\mathcal{G}
in the hierarchical mean-field limit
N\to\infty
, the above type of behaviour has been studied in detail in
[
DGV95
]
and
[
GdHKK14
]
for the Fleming-Viot model, respectively, the Cannings model without seed-bank. The renormalisation analysis for the model with seed-bank allows us to study how the seed-bank affects the cluster growth. In what follows we give a sketch of
three regimes of cluster growth
.

Types of clustering.

If a ball in
\Omega_{N}
lies in a mono-type cluster, then the block average of the active and the dormant components in this ball are all close to either
0
or
1
. We can therefore analyse the growth rate of mono-type clusters by analysing at which hierarchical level block averages hit
0
or
1
in the limit as
N\to\infty
. To that end, we look at the interaction chain
M^{k}_{-l(k)}
for
k\to\infty
, where the
level scaling function

l\colon\,\mathbb{N}_{0}\to\mathbb{N}_{0}
is non-decreasing with
\lim_{k\to\infty}l(k)=\infty
and is suitably chosen such that we obtain a
non-trivial clustering limiting law
, i.e.,

(5.7)

\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathcal{L}\bigl[M^{k}_{-l(k)}\bigr]=\mathcal{L}\bigl[\hat{\theta}\,\bigr],

where the limiting sequence of random frequencies
\hat{\theta}
satisfies

(5.8)

0<\mathbb{P}\bigl(\hat{\theta}\in\{0^{\mathbb{N}_{0}},1^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}\}\bigr)<1.

In line with
[
DGV95
]
and
[
DG96
]
, in order to analyse the growth of mono-type clusters on multiple space-time scales in the hierarchical mean-field limit, it is natural to consider a family of non-decreasing functions
l_{\chi}\colon\,\mathbb{N}_{0}\to\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
\chi\in I\subseteq[0,\infty)
, called the
cluster scales
, satisfying (
5.7
)–(
5.8
):

(1)

Fast clustering:

\lim_{k\to\infty}l_{\chi}(k)/k=1
for all
\chi\in I
.

(2)

Diffusive clustering:

\lim_{k\to\infty}l_{\chi}(k)/k=\kappa(\chi)
for all
\chi\in[0,1]
, where
\chi\mapsto\kappa(\chi)
is
continuous and non-increasing with
\kappa(0)=1
and
\kappa(1)=0
.

(3)

Slow clustering:

\lim_{k\to\infty}l_{\chi}(k)/k=0
for all
\chi\in I
. (This regime borders with the regime of
coexistence.)

We write
(M^{\infty}_{\chi})_{\chi\in I}
with
M^{\infty}_{\chi}=\lim_{k\to\infty}M^{k}_{-l_{\chi}(k)}
to denote the
cluster process
.

Remark 5.2
.

Examples are:

(1)

I=\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
l_{\chi}(k)=k-\chi
.

(2)

I=[0,1]
,
l_{\chi}(k)=\lfloor(1-\chi)k\rfloor
.

(3)

I=[0,1]
,
l_{\chi}(k)=\lfloor L(k^{1-\chi})\rfloor
with
L(0)=0
,
L
non-decreasing and sublinear.

In words, the clusters cover blocks of level: (1)
k-\chi
; (2)
\lfloor(1-\chi)k\rfloor
; (3)
L(k^{1-\chi})
. For the model without seed-bank and with migration coefficients
c_{k}=c^{k}
with
c\in(0,1)
, case (1) is realised with a Markov chain
(M^{\infty}_{l})_{l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
as scaling limit, while for
c_{k}=C
, case (2) is realised with a time-transformed Fisher-Wright diffusion in
\chi
as scaling limit. (For finite
N
, this corresponds to the first and the second example given in the first paragraph of this section.) For the model without seed-bank, these scales have been shown to satisfy the required conditions. Case (3) also appears for the model without seed-bank, but detailed information on scales and scaling limits is lacking. As we will see below, seed-banks can slow down cluster growth, so case (3) is worthwhile to be studied in more detail.

\blacksquare

Recall (
5.3
). Fast clustering corresponds to
A_{k}\gg k
, diffusive clustering to
A_{k}\asymp k
, and slow clustering to
A_{k}\ll k
for large
k
. Theorem
5.3
below shows that, subject to (
3.10
) and (
3.11
), all three regimes are possible for the model with seed-bank. The regimes are the same as for the model without seed-bank when
\rho<\infty
, but different when
\rho=\infty
.

For systems without seed-bank, examples of the three types of clustering can be found in the literature: diffusive clustering in
[
Arr79
]
,
[
CG86
]
(voter model on
\mathbb{Z}
, respectively,
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
) and in
[
DG93a
]
,
[
FG94
]
,
[
DGV95
]
,
[
Kle96
]
(interacting Fleming-Viot processes on
\Omega_{N}
with
N<\infty
, respectively,
N\to\infty
), all types of clustering in
[
DG96
]
,
[
Kle97
]
,
[
Win02
]
(interacting Feller diffusions on
\Omega_{N}
with
N\to\infty
) and in
[
GdHKK14
]
,
[
GdHK18
]
(interacting Cannings processes on
\Omega_{N}
in non-random and random environment with
N\to\infty
).

For the model with seed-bank we have to use the asymptotics of
(A_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
to identify the set
I
and the family
(l_{\chi}(\cdot))_{\chi\in I}
, and show that
(M^{k}_{-l_{\chi}(k)})_{\chi\in I}
converges as
k\to\infty
to a Markov process, which we want to identify.

Computations.

In the following we demonstrate how we can carry out the above task. The key idea is to study first and second moments of the interaction chain, as well as sums of variances in order to get a handle on the quadratic variation process. To that end we calculate

(5.9)

V^{k}_{-l}=\text{ the conditional variance of the active part of }M^{k}_{-l}\text{ given }M^{k}_{-(l+1)}

and consider the sum of random variables
A_{k,n}=\sum_{-(k+1)\leq-l\leq-n}V^{k}_{-l}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. In order for the system to cluster, we must have
\lim_{k\to\infty}A_{k,n}=0
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. The
volatility profile
is given by

(5.10)

(p_{\chi}(k))_{\chi\in[0,1]},\qquad p_{\chi}(k)=A_{k,l_{\chi}(k)}/A_{k,0}.

This profile is a
random variable
that depends on the interaction chain up to
M^{k}_{-l_{\chi}(k)}
. Since
A_{k,l_{\chi}(k)}=A_{k,0}-A_{l_{\chi}(k)-1,0}
, we have
p_{\chi}(k)=(A_{k,0}-A_{l_{\chi}(k)-1,0})/A_{k,0}
. For diffusive clustering, for instance, we want to show that

(5.11)

\lim_{k\to\infty}p_{\chi}(k)=1-\kappa(\chi),\qquad\chi\in[0,1],

while for fast clustering the limit is
0
and for slow clustering the limit is
1
. From (
5.6
) we know that the scaled renormalised diffusion function
A_{n}\mathcal{F}^{(n)}(g)
tends to the standard Fisher-Wright diffusion function as
n\to\infty
. Since the latter hits the boundary
\{0,1\}
after some finite time, the coefficients
A_{n}
describe the speed at which the interaction chain hits this boundary. We next make this idea precise and show how it can be used to obtain information about the growth of mono-type clusters.

The kernels defined in Section
5.1
allow us to compute the first and second moments of all the block averages, which will be done in Section
13.1
(Propositions
13.1
–
13.4
). In particular, using the interaction chain starting between at
-n
and running until
-m
with
-n<-m\leq 0
, and considering the
m
-block averages on time scale
N^{m}t
in the limit
N\to\infty
, we find that the variance of the active component
x^{n}_{m}
of
M^{n}_{-m}
equals

(5.12)

\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}(x^{n}_{m})=\mathbb{E}\left[(x^{n}_{m}-\vartheta_{n})^{2}\right]=A_{m}^{n}(\mathcal{F}^{(n+1)}g)(\vartheta_{n}),

where

(5.13)

??=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=m}^{n}\frac{E_{k}}{c_{k}}\frac{(E_{k}c_{k}+e_{k})}{(E_{k}c_{k}+e_{k})+E_{k}K_{k}e_{k}}.

(Note that
A_{n}=A^{n-1}_{0}
.) On the other hand, since
x^{n}_{m}\in(0,1)
we have
\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}(x^{n}_{m})\in(0,1)
and
A^{n}_{m}(\mathcal{F}^{(n+1)})(\vartheta_{n})\in(0,1)
. Taking
m=0
, we get
(\mathcal{F}^{(n+1)}g)(\vartheta_{n})\in(0,\frac{1}{A^{n}_{0}})
. This implies that

(5.14)

(\mathcal{F}^{(n+1)}g)(\vartheta_{n})=\int_{[0,1]^{2}}(\mathcal{F}^{m}g)(x_{m})\,Q^{(n)}_{m}((\vartheta_{n},\theta_{y,n}),{\rm d}z_{m})\in\left(0,\tfrac{1}{A^{n}_{0}}\right).

Since
\lim_{n\to\infty}A_{n}(\mathcal{F}^{(n+1)}g)=g_{\text{FW}}
, for large enough
m,n
we can approximate
\mathcal{F}^{(m)}g\approx g_{\text{FW}}/A^{m}_{0}
. Therefore

(5.15)

\int\frac{g_{\text{FW}}}{A^{m}_{0}}(x_{m})\,Q^{(n)}_{m}((\vartheta_{n},\theta_{y,n}),{\rm d}z_{m})\in\left(0,\tfrac{1}{A^{n}_{0}}\right),

or, equivalently,

(5.16)

\int_{[0,1]^{2}}x_{m}(1-x_{m})\,Q^{(n)}_{m}((\vartheta_{n},\theta_{y,n}),{\rm d}z_{m})\in\left(0,\tfrac{A^{m}_{0}}{A^{n}_{0}}\right).

Hence, if
A^{m}_{0}/A^{n}_{0}<\epsilon
with
\epsilon>0
small, then we know that with high probability the system on time scale
n
has clusters with a radius of size
m
. (Note that for the interaction chain this means that the variance is almost entirely centred between
n
and
m
.) Therefore the speed at which
A^{m}_{0}/A^{n}_{0}
converges to zero as
m,n\to\infty
says something about the speed at which monotype clusters form.

To capture the cluster growth, we must decide how we let
m,n\to\infty
. For this we look for clusters of radius
l_{\chi}(n)
with
\chi\in I
. Put

(5.17)

f^{n}(l_{\chi}(n))=\frac{A^{l_{\chi}(n)}_{0}}{A^{n}_{0}},

and define, for
\epsilon>0
,

(5.18)

\mathcal{X}^{n}_{\epsilon}=\inf\{\chi\in I\colon\,f^{n}(l_{\chi}(n))<\epsilon\}.

Then the three types of clustering correspond to:

(1)

Fast clustering:

\lim_{n\to\infty}l_{\mathcal{X}^{n}_{\epsilon}}(n)/n=1
.

(2)

Diffusive clustering:

\lim_{n\to\infty}l_{\mathcal{X}^{n}_{\epsilon}}(n)/n=R
for some random variable
R
taking values in
(0,1)
.

(3)

Slow clustering:

\lim_{n\to\infty}l_{\mathcal{X}^{n}_{\epsilon}}(n)/n=0
.

In terms of the interaction chain starting from
-k
with
k\to\infty
, in view of (
5.12
) this corresponds to the variance in the interaction chain being concentrated near the beginning, being spread out or being concentrated near the end.

5.3
Rates of scaling for renormalised diffusion function

For the system without seed-bank, we have
K_{k}=e_{k}=0
and
E_{k}=1
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Hence

(5.19)

A_{n}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{1}{c_{k}}

and (
5.4
) holds if and only if

(5.20)

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\frac{1}{c_{k}}=\infty.

Various subcases were analysed in
[
BCGdH95
]
. For the system with seed-bank because
E_{0}=1
and
E_{k}<1
(see (
4.5
)), it follows from (
5.3
) that

(5.21)

A_{n}<\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{1}{c_{k}}.

Thus we see that the seed-bank
weakens clustering
, i.e., enhances genetic diversity, even in the hierarchical mean-field limit.

We identify the clustering regime in the setting where the coefficients are
asymptotically polynomial
, as in (
3.10
), or are
pure exponential
, as in (
3.11
). It turns out that there is a delicate interplay between the migration and the seed-bank, resulting in 4 different scalings for asymptotically polynomial coefficients and 8 different scalings for pure exponential coefficients.

Theorem 5.3
.

[Rates of scaling for diffusion function]
Let
\rho
be as defined in (
2.14
).

(I)

If
\rho<\infty
, then (
5.5
) holds if and only (
5.20
) hold, and

(5.22)

A_{n}\sim\frac{1}{2(1+\rho)}\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\frac{1}{c_{k}}.

(II)

If
\rho=\infty
, then (
5.5
) holds in the following cases:

\bullet

Subject to (
3.10
) if and only if
-\phi\leq\alpha\leq 1
, with

(5.23)

\displaystyle-\phi<\alpha<1\colon

\displaystyle A_{n}\sim C_{1}\,n^{\alpha+\phi},

\displaystyle-\phi=\alpha<1\colon

\displaystyle A_{n}\sim C_{2}\,\log n,

\displaystyle-\phi<\alpha=1\colon

\displaystyle A_{n}\sim C_{3}\,\frac{n^{1+\phi}}{\log n},

\displaystyle-\phi=\alpha=1\colon

\displaystyle A_{n}\sim C_{4}\,\log\log n,

where

(5.24)

\begin{array}[]{ll}&C_{1}=\frac{1}{2AF}\frac{1-\alpha}{\alpha+\phi},\quad C_{2}=\frac{1}{2AF}(1-\alpha),\quad C_{3}=\frac{1}{2AF}\frac{1}{1+\phi},\quad C_{4}=\frac{1}{2AF}.\\
&\end{array}

The values of
B,\beta
play no role for the clustering, nor for the asymptotics.

\bullet

Subject to (
3.11
) if and only if
Kc\leq 1\leq K
, with

(5.25)

\displaystyle c<Ke,\,Kc<1\colon

\displaystyle A_{n}\sim\hat{C}_{1}\,(Kc)^{-(n-1)},

\displaystyle c<Ke,\,Kc=1\colon

\displaystyle A_{n}\sim\bar{C}_{1}\,n,

\displaystyle c=Ke,\,Kc<1\colon

\displaystyle A_{n}\sim\hat{C}_{2}\,(Kc)^{-(n-1)},

\displaystyle c=Ke,\,Kc=1\colon

\displaystyle A_{n}\sim\bar{C}_{2}\,n,

\displaystyle c>Ke,\,Kc<1\colon

\displaystyle A_{n}\sim\hat{C}_{3}\,(Kc)^{-(n-1)},

\displaystyle c>Ke,\,Kc=1\colon

\displaystyle A_{n}\sim\bar{C}_{3}\,n,

\displaystyle c<1=K\colon

\displaystyle A_{n}\sim\tilde{C}_{1}\,n^{-1}\,c^{-(n-1)},

\displaystyle c=1=K\colon

\displaystyle A_{n}\sim\tilde{C}_{2}\log n,

where

(5.26)

\begin{array}[]{llll}&\hat{C}_{1}=\frac{K-1}{2K(1-Kc)},&\hat{C}_{2}=\frac{(K-1)^{2}}{2(2K-1)(1-Kc)},&\hat{C}_{3}=\frac{K-1}{2(1-Kc)},\\[8.5359pt]
&\bar{C}_{1}=\frac{K-1}{2K},&\bar{C}_{2}=\frac{(K-1)^{2}}{2(2K-1)},&\bar{C}_{3}=\frac{K-1}{2},\\[8.5359pt]
&\tilde{C}_{1}=\frac{1}{2(1-c)},&\tilde{C_{2}}=\frac{1}{2}.&\end{array}

The value of
e
plays no role for the clustering, but does for the asymptotics.

The proof of Theorem
5.3
is given in Section
13
. Part (I) shows that for
\rho<\infty
the clustering regime is the same as for the system without seed-bank. The scaling of
A_{n}
is controlled by the migration and is reduced by a factor
1/(1+\rho)
with respect to the seed-bank. Part (II) shows that for
\rho=\infty
the clustering regime is different from that for the system without seed-bank. Clustering is harder to achieve: since
\lim_{k\to\infty}E_{k}=0
the growth rate of
A_{n}
is
strictly smaller
than without seed-bank.

Furthermore, subject to (
3.10
), if
-\phi<\alpha<1
, then the growth rate of
A_{n}
drops down from
\asymp n^{1+\phi}
without seed-bank to
\asymp n^{\alpha+\phi}
with seed-bank, while if
-\phi=\alpha=1
, then it drops down from
\asymp\log n
to
\asymp\log\log n
. Similarly, subject to (
3.11
), if
Kc<1<K
, then the growth rate of
A_{n}
drops down from
\asymp c^{-n}
to
\asymp(Kc)^{-n}
, while if
c=K=1
, then it drops down from
\asymp n
to
\asymp\log n
.

Returning to the observations made in Section
5.2
, we see that the three clustering regimes also appear in the model with seed-bank, both for
\rho<\infty
and
\rho=\infty
, and in the latter case are accompanied by different migration coefficients. The scaling results mentioned in Section
5.2
can in principle be deduced from the asymptotics of
A_{n}
as
n\to\infty
in Theorem
5.3
. It would be interesting to work out the details and to identify the limiting processes that control the cluster growth.

Part II
PREPARATIONS AND PROOFS

6
Proofs:
N<\infty
, identification of clustering regime

In this section we prove Theorems
3.2
–
3.3
. The integral criterion for
\rho=\infty
in (
3.8
) is explained in Section
6.1
. Theorem
3.2
is proved in Section
6.2
and Theorem
3.3
in Section
6.3
.

6.1
Explanation of clustering criterion for infinite seed-bank

Recall Fig.
6
. Suppose that
g=dg_{\mathrm{FW}}
, so that we have a dual. We will show that the integral criterion in (
3.8
) determines whether or not two dual lineages coalesce with probability 1. Since two lineages in the dual can only coalesce when they are active at the same site, we need to keep track of the probabilities that the lineages are active at a given time. Because the lineages can only migrate when they are active, we also need to keep track of the total time they are active up to a given time.

Recall the renewal interpretation of the dual process (see Remark
2.9
). We argue heuristically as follows. If
\rho=\infty
, then the activity times
\sigma_{k}
are much smaller than the sleeping times
\tau_{k}
, and we may assume that
\tau_{k}+\sigma_{k}\asymp\tau_{k}
,
k\to\infty
. Discretising time, we can use the results from
[
AB16
]
for the intersection of two independent renewal processes. Then the integral criterion in (
3.8
) can be interpreted as follows:

•

If
\gamma\in(0,1)
, then the probability for each of the lineages to be active at time
s
decays like
\asymp\varphi(s)^{-1}s^{-(1-\gamma)}

[
AB16
]
. Hence the total time they are active up to time
s
is
\asymp\varphi(s)^{-1}s^{\gamma}
. Because the lineages only move when they are active, the probability that the two lineages meet at time
s
is
\asymp a^{(N)}_{\varphi(s)^{-1}s^{\gamma}}(0,0)
. Hence the total hazard is
\asymp\int_{1}^{\infty}{\rm d}s\,[\varphi(s)^{-1}s^{-(1-\gamma)}]^{2}\,a^{(N)}_{\varphi(s)^{-1}s^{\gamma}}(0,0)
. After the transformation
t=t(s)=\varphi(s)^{-1}s^{\gamma}
, the latter turns into the integral in (
3.8
), modulo a constant. When carrying out this transformation, we need that
s\varphi^{\prime}(s)/\varphi(s)\to 0
, which follows from (
3.7
), and
\varphi(t(s))/\varphi(s)\asymp 1
, which follows from the bound we imposed on
\psi
in (
3.7
) together with the fact that
\log\varphi(s)/\log s\to 0
. This computation is spelled out in Appendix
A
.

•

If
\gamma=1
, then the probability for each of the lineages to be active at time
s
decays like
\hat{\varphi}(s)^{-1}

[
AB16
]
, and so the total time they are active up to time
s
is
\asymp s\hat{\varphi}(s)^{-1}
. Recall from (
3.6
) that
\hat{\varphi}(t)=\mathbb{E}[\tau\wedge t]
is also slowly varying.) Hence the total hazard is
\asymp\int_{1}^{\infty}{\rm d}s\,[\hat{\varphi}(s)^{-1}]^{2}\,a^{(N)}_{\hat{\varphi}(s)^{-1}s}(0,0)
. After the transformation
t=t(s)=\hat{\varphi}(s)^{-1}s
(for which we can use the same type of computation as in Appendix
A
), the latter turns into the integral in (
3.8
), modulo a constant.

6.2
Scaling of wake-up time and migration kernel for infinite seed-bank

We can prove Theorem
3.2
by direct computation via assumptions (
3.10
)–(
3.11
). We start by computing
\gamma
. Afterwards we compute
\hat{\varphi}(t)
and
a_{t}^{\Omega_{N}}(0,0)
.

Computation of
\gamma
.

Recall (
2.39
), which reads

(6.1)

\mathbb{P}(\tau>t)=\frac{1}{\chi}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}K_{m}\frac{e_{m}}{N^{m}}\,{\rm e}^{-(e_{m}/N^{m})t}.

Since we are interested in the asymptotic behaviour of
\mathbb{P}(\tau>t)
as
t\to\infty
, we need to consider only large values of
t
. For large values of
t
, only large values of
m
(for which
\frac{e_{m}}{N^{m}}
is small) contribute to the sum in (
6.1
). Hence we can estimate the latter by an integral and insert the assumptions made in (
3.10
)–(
3.11
). Subsequently, using the change of variable
s=\frac{e_{m}}{N^{m}}
and taking the logarithm to express
m
in terms of
s
, we obtain the following values of
\gamma
after extracting the
t
-dependence:

(6.2)

\displaystyle\eqref{regvar}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\gamma=1,\quad\varphi(t)\asymp(\log t)^{-\alpha},

\displaystyle\eqref{pureexp}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\gamma=\gamma_{N,K,e}=\frac{\log(N/Ke)}{\log(N/e)},\quad\varphi(t)\asymp 1.

In order to guarantee that
\rho=\infty
, we must require that
\alpha\in(-\infty,1]
, respectively,
K\in[1,\infty)
(while
\beta
, respectively,
e
play no role). Subject to (
3.10
),

(6.3)

\hat{\varphi}(t)\asymp\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(\log t)^{1-\alpha},&\alpha\in(-\infty,1),\\[5.69046pt]
\log\log t,&\alpha=1,\end{array}\right.

while subject to (
3.11
),

(6.4)

\hat{\varphi}(t)\asymp\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1,&K\in(1,\infty),\\[5.69046pt]
\log t,&K=1.\end{array}\right.

Computation of
a^{\Omega_{N}}_{t}(0,0)
.

To compute
a^{\Omega_{N}}_{t}(0,0)
, we first rewrite the migration kernel
a^{\Omega_{N}}(\cdot,\cdot)
in (
2.6
) as

(6.5)

a^{\Omega_{N}}(0,\eta)=\frac{r_{\|\eta\|}}{N^{\|\eta\|-1}(N-1)}

with

(6.6)

r_{\|\eta\|}=\frac{1}{D(N)}\frac{N-1}{N}\sum_{l\geq\|\eta\|}\frac{c_{l-1}}{N^{l-1}}\frac{1}{N^{l-\|\eta\|}},

where
D(N)
is a renormalisation constant such that
\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}r_{j}=1
. For transition kernels of the form (
6.5
), the time-
t
transition kernel
a^{\Omega_{N}}_{t}(\cdot,\cdot)
was computed in
[
FG94
]
with the help of Fourier analysis, see also
[
DGW05
]
. Namely,

(6.7)

a^{\Omega_{N}}_{t}(0,\eta)=\sum_{j\geq k}K_{jk}(N)\,\frac{\exp[-h_{j}(N)t]}{N^{j}},\qquad t\geq 0,\quad\eta\in\Omega_{N}\colon\,d_{\Omega_{N}}(0,\eta)=k\in\mathbb{N}_{0},

where

(6.8)

K_{jk}(N)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0,&j=k=0,\\
-1,&j=k>0,\\
N-1,&\mbox{otherwise},\end{array}\right.\qquad j,k\in\mathbb{N}_{0},

and

(6.9)

h_{j}(N)=\frac{N}{N-1}\,r_{j}(N)+\sum_{i>j}r_{i}(N),\qquad j\in\mathbb{N}.

The expressions in (
6.6
)–(
6.9
) simplify considerably in the limit as
N\to\infty
, namely, the term with
i=j
dominates and

(6.10)

h_{j}(N)\sim r_{j}(N)\sim\frac{c_{j-1}}{D(N)N^{j-1}},\quad j\in\mathbb{N},\qquad D(N)\sim c_{0}.

We show why this is true for
h_{j}(N)
(the argument for
r_{j}(N)
and
D(N)
is similar). Write

(6.11)

\displaystyle h_{j}(N)

\displaystyle=\frac{N}{N-1}\,r_{j}(N)+\sum_{i>j}r_{i}(N)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{D(N)}\left(\sum_{l\geq j}\frac{c_{l-1}}{N^{l-1}}\frac{1}{N^{l-j}}+\frac{N-1}{N}\sum_{l>j}\frac{c_{l-1}}{N^{l-1}}\sum_{i<j\leq l}\frac{1}{N^{l-i}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{D(N)}\frac{c_{j-1}}{N^{j-1}}\left(1+\left[1+O\left(\frac{1}{N}\right)\right]\,\left(\frac{c_{j-1}}{N^{j-1}}\right)^{-1}\sum_{l>j}\frac{c_{l-1}}{N^{l-1}}\right).

Hence it suffices to show that

(6.12)

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left(\frac{c_{j-1}}{N^{j-1}}\right)^{-1}\sum_{l>j}\frac{c_{l-1}}{N^{l-1}}=0,\qquad j\in\mathbb{N}.

To do so, note that, since
\limsup_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{k}\log c_{k}<\log N
by (
2.7
), for
N
large enough we have

(6.13)

\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}c_{k}^{1/k}<N.

Let
\bar{N}=\inf\{N\in\mathbb{N}\colon\,\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}c_{k}^{1/k}<N\}
. Then

(6.14)

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left(\frac{c_{j-1}}{N^{j-1}}\right)^{-1}\sum_{l>j}\frac{c_{l-1}}{N^{l-1}}\leq\limsup_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{c_{j-1}}\sum_{l>j}\frac{\bar{N}^{l-1}}{N^{l-1}}N^{j-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\bar{N}^{j-1}}{c_{j-1}}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\frac{\frac{\bar{N}}{N}}{1-\frac{\bar{N}}{N}}=0,\qquad j\in\mathbb{N},

which settles (
6.12
).

To understand what (
6.9
) gives for finite
N
, note that for asymptotically polynomial coefficients (recall (
3.10
))

(6.15)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{c_{j-1}}{N^{j-1}}\right)^{-1}\sum_{l>j}\frac{c_{l-1}}{N^{l-1}}=[1+o(1)]\,\frac{N^{j-1}}{F(j-1)^{-\phi}}\sum_{l>j}\frac{{F(l-1)^{-\phi}}}{N^{l-1}}

\displaystyle=[1+o(1)]\,\sum_{l>j}\frac{{(l-1)^{-\phi}}}{(j-1)^{-\phi}}N^{-(l-j)}=[1+o(1)]\,\sum_{k\geq 1}\left(1+\frac{{k}}{j-1}\right)^{-\phi}N^{-k},\qquad j\in\mathbb{N}.

For
\phi\geq 0
the right-hand side is bounded from above by
\sum_{k\geq 1}N^{-k}=\frac{1}{N-1}
and for
\phi<0
by
N^{-1}\sum_{k\geq 1}(1+k)^{-\phi}N^{-(k-1)}\leq N^{-1}C_{\phi}
. On the other hand, for pure exponential coefficients (recall (
3.11
)),

(6.16)

\left(\frac{c_{j-1}}{N^{j-1}}\right)^{-1}\sum_{l>j}\frac{c_{l-1}}{N^{l-1}}=\sum_{k\geq 1}\left(\frac{c}{N}\right)^{-k}=\frac{c}{N-c}.

Hence, for both choices of coefficients we have the following:

(6.17)

For
N\to\infty
the quantities
h_{j}(N),r_{j}(N)
are bounded from above

and below by positive finite constants times the right-hand side of

(
6.10
) uniformly in
j\in\mathbb{N}
.

Picking
\eta=0
(
k=0
) in (
6.7
), we obtain

(6.18)

a_{t}^{\Omega_{N}}(0,0)=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}(N-1)\frac{\exp[-h_{j}(N)t]}{N^{j}}.

Since we are interested in the asymptotic behaviour of
a_{t}^{\Omega_{N}}(0,0)
, only large values of
j
are relevant and we can estimate the sum in (
6.18
) by an integral. To do so, we change variables by putting
s=h_{j}(N)
and exploit (
6.17
). Take the logarithm to express
j
in terms of
s
, compute
{\rm d}s/{\rm d}j
, and extract the
t
-dependence. This gives

(6.19)

\displaystyle\eqref{regvar}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad a_{t}^{\Omega_{N}}(0,0)\asymp t^{-1}\log^{\phi}t,

\displaystyle\eqref{pureexp}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad a_{t}^{\Omega_{N}}(0,0)\asymp t^{-1-\delta_{N,c}},

where

(6.20)

\delta_{N,c}=\frac{\log c}{\log(N/c)}.

6.3
Hierarchical clustering

In this section we prove Theorem
3.3
by substituting the results of Theorem
3.2
into the clustering criterion in (
3.8
).

Combining (
3.9
), (
6.2
)–(
6.4
) and (
6.19
)–(
6.20
), we find the following clustering criterion for
fixed

N
and infinite seed-bank:

•

Subject to (
3.10
), clustering prevails if and only if

(6.21)

-\phi\leq\alpha\leq 1.

•

Subject to (
3.11
), clustering prevails if and only

(6.22)

\delta_{N,c}\leq-\frac{1-\gamma_{N,K,e}}{\gamma_{N,K,e}}.

In view of (
6.2
) and (
3.14
), the condition in (
6.22
) amounts to

(6.23)

\log N\times\log(Kc)\leq\log c\times\log(K^{2}e),

where we use that
c<N
and
Ke<N
(recall (
2.7
) and (
2.12
)). The condition in (
6.23
) holds for all
N
when

(6.24)

Kc=1\text{ with }\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}c=1,&K^{2}e\in(0,\infty),\\
c>1,&K^{2}e\geq 1,\\
c<1,&K^{2}e\leq 1.\end{array}\right.

It also holds for
N
large enough when
Kc<1
and fails for
N
large enough when
Kc>1
. Thus, for infinite seed-bank, clustering prevails for
N
large enough if and only if

(6.25)

Kc\leq 1\leq K,

which is the analogue of (
6.21
).

7
Preparation:
N\to\infty
, McKean-Vlasov process and mean-field system

To analyse the scaling of our hierarchical system in the hierarchichal mean-field limit
N\to\infty
, we first need to understand simpler systems. In this section we consider the mean-field system consisting of a
single hierarchy
, and introduce the following:

1.

McKean-Vlasov process (Section
7.1
).

2.

Mean-field system and McKean-Vlasov limit (Section
7.2
).

For each we derive a key proposition that will play a crucial role in our analysis of the truncated system with
finitely many hierarchies
in Sections
10
–
12
and the full system with
infinitely many hierarchies
in Section
12
. The proofs of the propositions stated in this section will be given in Sections
7.3
and
7.4
.

7.1
McKean-Vlasov process

In this section we introduce the McKean-Vlasov process, which will play an important role in our analysis of the mean-field system to be introduced in Sections
7.2
–
8.1
. (In the full system the effective process introduced in (
4.9
) will be seen to be an example of a McKean-Vlasov process.)

For
g\in\mathcal{G}
and
c,K,e\in(0,\infty)
, consider the single-colony process

(7.1)

z(t)=(x(t),y(t))_{t\geq 0},

taking values in
[0,1]^{2}
, with initial law
\mathcal{L}[(x(0),y(0))]=\mu
and with components evolving according to

(7.2)

\displaystyle{\rm d}x(t)=c\,[\mathbb{E}[x(t)]-x(t)]\,{\rm d}t+\sqrt{g(x(t))}\,{\rm d}w(t)+Ke\,[y(t)-x(t)]\,{\rm d}t,

\displaystyle{\rm d}y(t)=e\,[x(t)-y(t)]\,{\rm d}t,

where
\mathbb{E}
denotes expectation with respect to
\mu
. With the help of Itô-calculus we can compute the expectation
\mathbb{E}[x(t)]
. Indeed, from (
7.2
) we get

(7.3)

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\mathbb{E}[x(t)]

\displaystyle=Ke\,\big[\mathbb{E}[y(t)]-\mathbb{E}[x(t)]\big],

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\mathbb{E}[y(t)]

\displaystyle=e\,\big[\mathbb{E}[x(t)]-\mathbb{E}[y(t)]\big].

Define

(7.4)

\theta_{x}=\mathbb{E}^{\mu}[x(0)],\qquad\theta_{y}=\mathbb{E}^{\mu}[y(0)],\qquad\theta=\mathbb{E}^{\mu}\left[\frac{x(0)+Ky(0)}{1+K}\right].

Note that (
7.3
) implies that
\theta
is a preserved quantity, i.e.,

(7.5)

\mathbb{E}^{\mu}\left[\frac{x(0)+Ky(0)}{1+K}\right]=\mathbb{E}^{\mu}\left[\frac{x(t)+Ky(t)}{1+K}\right]=\theta,\qquad t\geq 0.

Solving (
7.3
), we find

(7.6)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[x(t)]

\displaystyle=\theta+\frac{K}{1+K}(\theta_{x}-\theta_{y})\,{\rm e}^{-(K+1)et},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[y(t)]

\displaystyle=\theta-\frac{1}{1+K}(\theta_{x}-\theta_{y})\,{\rm e}^{-(K+1)et}.

In particular, from (
4.51
) we see that

(7.7)

\lim_{t\to\infty}(\mathbb{E}[x(t)],\mathbb{E}[y(t)])=(\theta,\theta).

Hence, in equilibrium we can replace
\mathbb{E}[x(t)]
in (
7.2
) by
\theta
. After inserting (
7.6
) into (
7.2
), we can use
[
YW71
, Theorem 1, Remark on p.156]
to show that for every deterministic initial state
(x(0),y(0))\in[0,1]^{2}
the SSDE in (
7.2
) has a unique strong solution. We will refer to this solution as the
McKean-Vlasov process
.

Remark 7.1
.

[Self-consistency]

To prove uniqueness of the solution to (
7.2
) we can also use
[
Gar88
]
, where self-consistent mean-field dynamics are treated in detail. The solution has the Feller property.

\blacksquare

Proposition 7.2
.

[Single-colony McKean-Vlasov process: equilibrium]

For every initial law
\mu\in\mathcal{P}([0,1]^{2})
satisfying

(7.8)

\mathbb{E}^{\mu}\left[\frac{x(0)+Ky(0)}{1+K}\right]=\theta,\qquad\theta\in[0,1],

the process in (
7.1
) converges to a unique equilibrium,

(7.9)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathcal{L}[(x(t),y(t))]=\Gamma_{\theta},

and

(7.10)

\Gamma_{\theta}\in\mathcal{P}([0,1]^{2}),

satisfies

(7.11)

\theta=\int_{[0,1]^{2}}x\,\Gamma_{\theta}({\rm d}x,{\rm d}y)=\int_{[0,1]^{2}}y\,\Gamma_{\theta}({\rm d}x,{\rm d}y).

The proof of Proposition
7.2
is given in Section
7.3
. Note that
\Gamma_{\theta}=\Gamma_{\theta}^{g,c,K,e}
depends on all the parameters appearing in (
7.2
). In Section
8
we will see that
\Gamma_{\theta}
is continuous as a function of
\theta
.

Remark 7.3
.

[Non-linear Markov process]

Note that (
7.1
) is a
non-linear
Markov process: the evolution not only depends on the current state
z(t)
, but also on the current law
\mathcal{L}[z(t)]
via the expectation
\mathbb{E}[x(t)]
appearing in the SSDE (
7.2
). This is different from the model without seed-bank, where the non-linearity is replaced by a drift towards
\theta
that is constant in time. In equilibrium we can replace
\mathbb{E}[x(t)]
by
\theta
in (
7.2
), but before equilibrium is reached we cannot, because
t\mapsto\mathbb{E}[x(t)]
is not constant, as is clear from (
4.51
). Note that
\mathbb{E}[x(t)]
is a linear functional of
z(0)
. This fact will play an important role in the renormalisation analysis in Section
13
.

\blacksquare

7.2
Mean-field system and McKean-Vlasov limit

In this section we consider a simplified version of the SSDE in (
2.20
), namely, we restrict to the finite geographic space

(7.12)

??=\{0,1,\ldots,N-1\},\qquad N\in\mathbb{N}.

In this simplified version, the migration kernel
a^{\Omega_{N}}(\cdot,\cdot)
is replaced by
a^{[N]}(\xi,\eta)=cN^{-1}
for all
(\xi,\eta)\in[N]
, where
c\in(0,\infty)
is a constant. The seed-bank consists of only
one colour
and the exchange rates between active and dormant are given by
Ke,e
. The state space is

(7.13)

S=\mathfrak{s}^{[N]},\qquad\mathfrak{s}=[0,1]^{2},

the system is denoted by

(7.14)

Z^{[N]}(t)=\big(X^{[N]}(t),Y^{[N]}(t)\big)_{t\geq 0},\qquad\big(X^{[N]}(t),Y^{[N]}(t)\big)=\big(x^{[N]}_{i}(t),y^{[N]}_{i}(t)\big)_{i\in[N]},

and its components evolve according to the SSDE

(7.15)

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle d}x^{[N]}_{i}(t)=\frac{c}{N}\sum_{j\in[N]}\big[x^{[N]}_{j}(t)-x^{[N]}_{i}(t)\big]\,{\rm d}t+\sqrt{g\big(x^{[N]}_{i}(t)\big)}\,{\rm d}w_{i}(t)+Ke\,\big[y^{[N]}_{i}(t)-x^{[N]}_{i}(t)\big]\,{\rm d}t,

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle d}y^{[N]}_{i}(t)=e\,\big[x^{[N]}_{i}(t)-y^{[N]}_{i}(t)\big]\,{\rm d}t,\qquad i\in[N],

which is the special case of (
2.20
) obtained by setting
a^{\Omega_{N}}(\eta,\xi)=0
if
d(\eta,\xi)>1
and
K_{m}=e_{m}=0
for
m\geq 1
. It is natural to take an
exchangeable random initial state
, because the evolution preserves exchangeability. According to De Finetti’s theorem, there is no loss of generality in taking an i.i.d. initial state, i.e.,

(7.16)

\mathcal{L}\big[X^{[N]}(0),Y^{[N]}(0)\big]=\mu^{\otimes[N]},\qquad\qquad\mu\in\mathcal{P}\left([0,1]^{2}\right).

By
[
SS80
, Theorem 3.1]
, the SSDE in (
7.15
) is the unique weak solution of a well-posed martingale problem. By
[
SS80
, Theorem 3.2]
, for every deterministic initial state
(X^{[N]}(0),Y^{[N]}(0))
, (
7.15
) has a unique strong solution. We are interested in the limit
N\to\infty
. For the limiting process we define

(7.17)

(Z(t))_{t\geq 0}=(X(t),Y(t))_{t\geq 0}=\big((x_{i}(t),y_{i}(t))_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\big)_{t\geq 0}

with components evolving according to (
7.2
), i.e.,

(7.18)

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle d}x_{i}(t)=c\,[\mathbb{E}[x_{i}(t)]-x_{i}(t)]\,{\rm d}t+\sqrt{g(x_{i}(t))}\,{\rm d}w(t)+Ke\,[y_{i}(t)-x_{i}(t)]\,{\rm d}t,

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle d}y_{i}(t)=e\,[x_{i}(t)-y_{i}(t)]\,{\rm d}t,\qquad i\in\mathbb{N}_{0},

with
\mathcal{L}\left[(X(0),Y(0))\right]=\mu
for some exchangeable
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(([0,1]^{2})^{\otimes[\mathbb{N}_{0}]}
. Note that (
7.18
) consists of i.i.d. copies of the single-colony McKean-Vlasov process in (
7.1
), labelled by
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

Proposition 7.4
.

[Infinite-system McKean-Vlasov limit: convergence]

Suppose that
\mathcal{L}[(X^{[N]}(0),Y^{[N]}(0))]=\mu^{[N]}
is exchangeable and

(7.19)

\theta=\mathbb{E}^{\mu^{[N]}}\left[\frac{x(0)+Ky(0)}{1+K}\right].

Then

(7.20)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathcal{L}\Big[\big(X^{[N]}(t),Y^{[N]}(t)\big)_{t\geq 0}\Big]=\mathcal{L}\big[(X(t),Y(t))_{t\geq 0}\big]

with

(7.21)

\mathcal{L}\big[(X(0),Y(0))_{t\geq 0}\big]=\mu,\qquad\mu=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mu^{[N]},

where the limit is the McKean-Vlasov process in (
7.1
)–(
7.2
).

The proof of Proposition
7.4
is given in Section
7.4
. For the system without seed-bank the McKean-Vlasov limit was proved in
[
Gar88
]
. The fact that the components decouple is a property referred to as
propagation of chaos
.

7.3
Proof of equilibrium and ergodicity

In this section we prove Proposition
7.2
.

Proof.

Note that, by (
4.51
), we can rewrite (
7.2
) as

(7.22)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle d}x(t)=c\,\left[\theta+\frac{K}{1+K}(\theta_{x}-\theta_{y})\,{\rm e}^{-(K+1)et}-x(t)\right]\,{\rm d}t+\sqrt{g(x(t))}\,{\rm d}w(t)

\displaystyle+Ke\,[y(t)-x(t)]\,{\rm d}t,

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle d}y(t)=e\,[x(t)-y(t)]\,{\rm d}t.

Existence and uniqueness of a strong solution is again standard (see e.g.
[
YW71
, Theorem 1]
and recall Remark
7.1
). We start by proving existence and uniqueness of the equilibrium. Afterwards we show that the solution converges to this equilibrium.

Consider two copies
(x_{1},y_{1})
and
(x_{2},y_{2})
of the system defined in (
7.22
), with
\mathcal{L}[(x_{1}(0),y_{1}(0))]=\mu_{1}
and
\mathcal{L}[(x_{2}(0),y_{2}(0))]=\mu_{2}
, where
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
satisfy

(7.23)

\mathbb{E}^{\mu_{1}}\left[\frac{x_{1}(0)+Ky_{1}(0)}{1+K}\right]=\theta=\mathbb{E}^{\mu_{2}}\left[\frac{x_{2}(0)+Ky_{2}(0)}{1+K}\right]

for some
\theta\in[0,1]
. Write

(7.24)

\theta_{x_{1}}=\mathbb{E}^{\mu_{1}}[x_{1}(0)],\quad\theta_{y_{1}}=\mathbb{E}^{\mu_{1}}[y_{1}(0)],\quad\theta_{x_{2}}=\mathbb{E}^{\mu_{2}}[x_{2}(0)],\quad\theta_{y_{2}}=\mathbb{E}^{\mu_{2}}[y_{2}(0)].

Couple the two systems by coupling their Brownian motions. Denote the coupled process by

(7.25)

\displaystyle(\bar{z}(t))_{t\geq 0}=(z_{1}(t),z_{2}(t))_{t\geq 0},\quad z_{1}(t)=(x_{1}(t),y_{1}(t)),\quad z_{2}(t)=(x_{2}(t),y_{2}(t)),

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\bar{z}(0))=\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2},

which has a unique strong solution. Put

(7.26)

\Delta(t)=x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t),\qquad\delta(t)=y_{1}(t)-y_{2}(t).

To show that the equilibrium is unique, it is enough to show that

(7.27)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[|\Delta(t)|+EK|\delta(t)|\right]=0.

Using a generalised form of Itô’s formula, we find

(7.28)

\displaystyle{\rm d}|\Delta(t)|

\displaystyle=({\rm sign}\,\Delta(t))\,{\rm d}\Delta(t)+{\rm d}L_{t}^{0}

\displaystyle=({\rm sign}\,\Delta(t))\,c\,\left[\frac{K}{1+K}\big((\theta_{x_{1}}-\theta_{x_{2}})-(\theta_{y_{1}}-\theta_{y_{2}})\big)\,{\rm e}^{-(K+1)et}-\Delta(t)\right]\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle+({\rm sign}\,\Delta(t))\,\left(\sqrt{g(x_{1}(t))}-\sqrt{g(x_{2}(t))}\,\right){\rm d}w(t)

\displaystyle+({\rm sign}\,\Delta(t))\,Ke\,[\delta(t)-\Delta(t)]\,{\rm d}t,

where we use that the local time
L_{t}^{0}
(see
[
RW00
, Section IV.43]
) of
\Delta(t)
at
0
equals
0
, since
g
is Lipschitz (see
[
RW00
, Proposition V.39.3]
). Again using Itô’s formula, we also find

(7.29)

{\rm d}|\delta(t)|=({\rm sign}\,\delta(t))\,{\rm d}\delta(t)=({\rm sign}\,\delta(t))\,e\,[\Delta(t)-\delta(t)]\,{\rm d}t.

Taking expectations in (
7.28
)–(
7.29
), we get

(7.30)

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\mathbb{E}[|\Delta(t)|+K|\delta(t)|]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[c\,\left[({\rm sign}\,\Delta(t))\frac{K}{1+K}\big((\theta_{x_{1}}-\theta_{x_{2}})-(\theta_{y_{1}}-\theta_{y_{2}})\big)\,{\rm e}^{-(K+1)et}-\left|\Delta(t)\right|\right]\right]

\displaystyle+Ke\,\mathbb{E}\Big[\left({\rm sign}\,\Delta(t)-{\rm sign}\,\delta(t)\right)(\delta(t)-\Delta(t))\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[c\,({\rm sign}\,\Delta(t))\frac{K}{1+K}\big((\theta_{x_{1}}-\theta_{x_{2}})-(\theta_{y_{1}}-\theta_{y_{2}})\big)\,{\rm e}^{-(K+1)et}\right]

\displaystyle-c\,\mathbb{E}[\left|\Delta(t)\right|]

\displaystyle-2Ke\,\mathbb{E}\left[1_{\{{\rm sign}\,\delta(t)\neq{\rm sign}\Delta(t)\}}\left(|\delta(t)|+|\Delta(t)|\right)\right].

Define

(7.31)

h(t)=c\,\mathbb{E}[\left|\Delta(t)\right|]+2Ke\,\mathbb{E}\left[1_{\{{\rm sign}\,\delta(t))\neq{\rm sign}\,\Delta(t)\}}\left(|\delta(t)|+|\Delta(t)|\right)\right].

Then
h(t)
satisfies

1.

h(t)>0
.

2.

0\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}t\,h(t)\leq 1+K+c\left|(\theta_{x_{1}}-\theta_{x_{2}})-(\theta_{y_{1}}-\theta_{y_{2}})\right|\frac{K}{K+1}\frac{1}{e(K+1)}\left[1-{\rm e}^{-(K+1)et}\right]
.

3.

h
is differentiable with
h^{\prime}
bounded (see
[
GHO20
, Appendix D]
).

Hence it follows that
\lim_{t\to\infty}h(t)=0
, which implies that

(7.32)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[|\Delta(t)|\right]=0.

We are left to prove that
\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[|\delta(t)|]=0
. To do so, we define

(7.33)

f(t)=\mathbb{E}[|\delta(t)|],\qquad G(t)=e\,\mathbb{E}[({\rm sign}\,\delta(t))\Delta(t)].

Note that
G
is bounded and continuous. Taking expectations in (
7.29
), we find

(7.34)

\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}f(t)=-ef(t)+G(t),

Solving (
7.34
) explicitly, we find that

(7.35)

f(t)=f(r)\,{\rm e}^{-e(t-r)}+\int_{r}^{t}{\rm d}s\,{\rm e}^{-e(t-s)}G(s),\qquad r,t\in\mathbb{R},\,t>r\geq 0.

By (
7.32
), for each
\epsilon>0
we can find an
r\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\mathbb{E}[|\Delta(s)|]<\epsilon
for all
s>r
, and hence
\sup_{t>r}|G(t)|<\epsilon
. Therefore

(7.36)

f(t)\leq f(r)\,{\rm e}^{-e(t-r)}+\epsilon

and, since
|f|<1
, we find, for each
\epsilon>0
,

(7.37)

\lim_{t\to\infty}f(t)<\epsilon.

Therefore
\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[|\delta(t)|]=0
, which completes the proof of uniqueness of the equilibrium for given
\theta
.

To prove existence of the equilibrium, let
(t_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be any increasing sequenc eof times such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}t_{n}=\infty
. Let
\mu=\mathcal{L}[(x(0),y(0))]
be any initial measure of the system in (
7.22
) with
\mathbb{E}^{\mu}[\frac{x(0)+Ky(0)}{1+K}]=\theta
, and let
\mu(t_{n})=\mathcal{L}[(x(t_{n}),y(t_{n}))]
. Since the state space is compact, the sequence
(\mu(t_{n}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is tight, and by Prohorov’s theorem we can find a converging subsequence
(\mu(t_{n_{k}}))_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
. Put
\nu=\lim_{k\to\infty}\mu(t_{n_{k}})
. We will show that
\nu
is invariant. To that end, recall from Section
7.1
that

(7.38)

\mathbb{E}^{\mu}\left[\frac{x(t)+Ky(t)}{1+K}\right]=\theta,\qquad t\geq 0.

Hence we can use the coupling in (
7.25
) to show that the system starting in
\mu
and the system starting
\mu(t)
converge to the same law as
t\to\infty
, from which it follows that
\lim_{k\to\infty}\mu(t+t_{n_{k}})=\nu
. Let
(S_{t})_{t\geq 0}
denote the semigroup of the system in (
7.22
). By the Feller property for semigroups,

(7.39)
