f\in\mathcal{C}_{b}([0,1]\times[0,1]^{3})
and
\delta>0
such that for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
there exists an
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
n>N
such that

(11.157)

\left|\mathbb{E}[f(z_{i}^{\mathrm{aux},n}(t_{n}))]-\mathbb{E}^{\Gamma^{(1)}_{\bar{\Theta}(t_{2})}}[f]\right|>\delta.

Hence we can construct a subsequence
(z_{i}^{\mathrm{aux},n_{k}}(t_{1}))_{t_{1}>0,\,k\in\mathbb{N}}
such that (
11.157
) holds for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
. However, also for this sequence
(\mathcal{L}[z_{i}^{\mathrm{aux},n_{k}}(0)])_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is tight. Passing to a possibly further subsequence of converging initial distributions, we argue like before to obtain that along this subsequence

(11.158)

\lim_{k\to\infty}\left|\mathbb{E}[f(z_{i}^{\mathrm{aux},n_{k}}(t_{n_{k}}))]-\mathbb{E}^{\Gamma^{(1)}_{\bar{\Theta}(t_{2})}}[f]\right|=0.

This contradicts (
11.157
) and so (
11.143
) is indeed true.
∎

Proof of Proposition
11.13

Proof.

Lemma
11.20
implies (
11.102
). Therefore Lemma
11.18
implies (
11.103
).
∎

11.3.7
Convergence of 2-block process

In this section we derive the limiting evolution of the effective
2
-block process.

Lemma 11.21
.

[Convergence of the 2-block averages]

Assume that
(N_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathbb{N}
is a subsequence satisfying

(11.159)

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathcal{L}\left[y_{2,1}^{[N_{k}^{2}]}(N_{k}t_{2})\Big|{\bf\Theta}^{(2),[N_{k}^{2}]}(N_{k}^{2}t_{2})\right]=P^{z_{2}(t_{2})},

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathcal{L}\left[\left(Y_{1,0}^{[N_{k}^{2}]}(N^{2}_{k}t_{2}+N_{k}t_{1}),Y_{2,0}^{[N_{k}^{2}]}(N^{2}_{k}t_{2})\right)\Big|{\bf\Theta}^{\mathrm{aux},(1),[N_{k}^{2}]}(N^{2}_{k}t_{2}+N_{k}t_{1})\right]

\displaystyle=P^{{z}_{1}^{\mathrm{eff}}(t_{1})}.

Then, for the effective
2
-block estimator process defined in (
11.23
),

(11.160)

\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathcal{L}\left[\left({\bf\Theta}^{\mathrm{eff},(2),[N_{k}^{2}]}(N_{k}^{2}t_{2})\right)_{t_{2}>0}\right]=\mathcal{L}\left[\left(z_{2}^{\mathrm{eff}}(t_{2})\right)_{t_{2}>0}\right],

where the limit is determined by the unique solution of the SSDE (
11.35
) with initial state

(11.161)

\displaystyle z_{2}^{\mathrm{eff}}(0)=\left(x_{2}^{\mathrm{eff}}(0),y^{\mathrm{eff}}_{2}(0)\right)=\left(\vartheta_{1},\theta_{y_{2}}\right).

Proof.

Again we use
[
JM86
, Theorem 3.3.1]
. By a similar argument as used in the proof of Lemma
10.15
we can show that

(11.162)

\lim_{t_{2}\downarrow 0}\mathcal{L}\left[{\bf\Theta}^{\mathrm{eff},(2),[N_{k}^{2}]}(N_{k}^{2}t_{2})\right]=\delta_{(\vartheta_{1},\theta_{y_{2}})}.

Note that by steps 1-4 of the scheme in Section
11.2
we can choose the subsequence
(N_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
such that both (
11.80
) and (
11.101
) hold. Since we already established the tightness of the
2
-block in Lemma
11.4
, we are left to show that, for all
t_{2}>0
,

(11.163)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big|G_{\dagger}^{(2),[N_{k}^{2}]}(f,{\bf\Theta}^{\mathrm{eff},(2),[N_{k}^{2}]}(N_{k}^{2}t_{2}),t_{2},\omega)

\displaystyle-G^{(2)}f\left({\bf\Theta}^{\mathrm{eff},(2),[N_{k}^{2}]}(N_{k}^{2}t_{2})\right)\Big|\Big]=0,

where
G_{\dagger}^{(2),[N_{k}^{2}]}
is the
\mathcal{D}
-semi-martingale operator defined in (
11.68
),
G^{(2)}
is the generator of the process
(z_{2}^{\mathrm{eff}}(t_{2}))_{t_{2}>0}
defined in (
11.35
), and both generators work on a probability space driven by one set of Brownian motions. Note that, for all
t_{2}>0
,

(11.164)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big|G_{\dagger}^{(2),[N_{k}^{2}]}\left(f,\left(\bar{\Theta}^{(2),[N_{k}^{2}]}(N_{k}^{2}t_{2}),\Theta^{(2),[N_{k}^{2}]}_{y_{2}}(N_{k}^{2}t_{2})\right),t_{2},\omega\right)

\displaystyle-G^{(2)}f\left(\bar{\Theta}^{(2),[N_{k}^{2}]}(N_{k}^{2}t_{2}),\Theta^{(2),[N_{k}^{2}]}_{y_{2}}(N_{k}^{2}t_{2})\right)\Big|\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{K_{2}e_{2}}{1+K_{0}+K_{1}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\bar{\Theta}^{(2),[N_{k}^{2}]}(N_{k}^{2}t_{2})-\Theta_{x}^{(2),[N_{k}^{2}]}(N_{k}^{2}t_{2},\omega)\right|\left|\,\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}\right|\right]

\displaystyle+e_{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\Theta_{x}^{(2),[N_{k}^{2}]}(N_{k}^{2}t_{2},\omega)-\bar{\Theta}^{(2),[N_{k}^{2}]}(N_{k}^{2}t_{2})\right|\left|\,\frac{\partial f}{\partial y}\right|\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{(1+K_{0}+K_{1})^{2}}

\displaystyle\times\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{1}{N^{2}_{k}}\sum_{i\in[N_{k}^{2}]}g(x_{i}^{[N^{2}_{k}]}(N_{k}^{2}t_{2},\omega))-(\mathcal{F}^{(2)}g)(\bar{\Theta}^{(2),[N_{k}^{2}]}(N_{k}^{2}t_{2}))\right|\left|\,\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial x^{2}}\right|\right].

The first and second term on the right-hand side tend to
0
as
k\to\infty
by a similar argument as used in (
11.128
) and below. For the third let
[N]_{i}
denote the
1
-block that contains site
i
and let
(z^{\nu_{\bar{\Theta}_{i}^{(1)}}}(t))_{t>0}
be the limiting single colony system, with drift towards the random variable
\bar{\Theta}_{i}
and starting from the equilibrium measure
\nu_{\bar{\Theta}_{i}^{(1)}}
. We construct the single colony system
Z^{[N^{2}_{k}]}(N^{2}_{k}t_{2}-L(N)+t)_{t\geq 0}
and the limiting system
(z^{\nu_{\bar{\Theta}_{i}^{(1)}}}(t))_{t>0}
on one probability space, such that by Skohorod’s theorem, we can assume that the convergence is almost surely. Note that
\bar{\Theta}_{i}
is the limiting one block. Then we can write

(11.165)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{1}{N^{2}_{k}}\sum_{i\in[N_{k}^{2}]}g(x_{i}^{[N_{k}^{2}]}(N_{k}^{2}t_{2},\omega))-(\mathcal{F}^{(2)}g)(\bar{\Theta}^{(2),[N_{k}^{2}]}(N_{k}^{2}t_{2}))\right|\left|\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial x^{2}}\right|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{i\in[N]}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{j\in[N]_{i}}g(x_{j}^{[N_{k}^{2}]}(N_{k}^{2}t_{2},\omega))-\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{j\in[N]_{i}}g(x_{j}^{\nu_{\bar{\Theta}_{i}^{(1)}}}(L(N))\right|\left|\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial x^{2}}\right|\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{i\in[N]}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{j\in[N]_{i}}g(x_{j}^{\nu_{\bar{\Theta}_{i}^{(1)}}}(L(N))-(\mathcal{F}^{(1)}g)(\bar{\Theta}_{i}^{(1)})\right|\left|\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial x^{2}}\right|\right]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{i\in[N]}(\mathcal{F}^{(1)}g)(\bar{\Theta}_{i}^{(1)})-(\mathcal{F}^{(2)}g)(\bar{\Theta}_{i}^{(2)})\right|\left|\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial x^{2}}\right|\right]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\left[\left|(\mathcal{F}^{(2)}g)(\bar{\Theta}_{i}^{(2)})-(\mathcal{F}^{(2)}g)(\bar{\Theta}_{i}^{(2)}(N_{k}^{2}t_{2}))\right|\left|\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial x^{2}}\right|\right].

The first term on the right-hand side tends to zero by Lipschitz continuity for
g
and Corollary
11.12
. The second term tends to zero by the law of large numbers, since the limiting single colonies are i.i.d. given the value of the random variable
\bar{\Theta}_{i}^{(1)}
. The third term tends to zero since by Proposition
11.13
also the limiting
1
-blocks become independent given the value of the
2
-block. Hence we can again apply the law of large numbers. Finally, for the last term, note that since we construct the single components and the limiting process on one probability space, we can argue like in the proof of Lemma
10.11
that

(11.166)

\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\bar{\Theta}_{i}^{(2)}-\bar{\Theta}_{i}^{(2)}(N_{k}^{2}t_{2})\right|\right]=0.

Hence the last term tends to zero by the Lipschitz property of
\mathcal{F}^{(2)}g
.
∎

Remark 11.22
.

Instead of
[
JM86
, Theorem 3.3.1]
we could have used a similar strategy as in the proof of Lemma
11.18
to obtain Lemma
11.21
.

\blacksquare

11.3.8
State of the slow seed-banks

On time scale
t_{0}
, i.e., space-time scale
0
, the colour-
1
seed-bank is a “slow seed-bank,” since it does not move on this time scale. Because we study the two-layer three-colour mean-field system from time
N^{2}t_{2}
onwards, the
1
-block averages of the colour
1
-dormant population are already in equilibrium. As a consequence we can exactly describe the single
1
-dormant colonies, which turn out to be in a state that equals the current
1
-block average of the dormant population of colour
1
. To obtain the formal result we will first prove the following lemma.

Lemma 11.23
.

[Slow seed-banks]

Fix
t_{2},t_{1}>0
, for
i\in[N^{2}]
and all
t_{0}\geq 0
,

(11.167)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\left[y^{[N^{2}]}_{i,1}(N^{2}t_{2}+Nt_{1}+t_{0})-\Theta_{y_{1},i}^{(1),[N^{2}]}(N^{2}t_{2}+Nt_{1}+t_{0})\right]=0\qquad\text{a.s.},

where
\Theta_{y_{1},i}^{(1),[N^{2}]}
is the
1
-block average to which
y^{[N^{2}]}_{i,1}
contributes.

To prove Lemma
11.23
, we need the kernel
b^{[N^{2}]}(\cdot,\cdot)
defined in
2.30
, which becomes in the current setting

(11.168)

b^{[N^{2}]}((i,R_{i}),(j,R_{j}))=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1_{\{d_{[N^{2}]}(i,j)\leq 1\}}\frac{c_{0}}{N}+\frac{c_{1}}{N^{3}},&\text{ if }R_{i}=R_{j}=A,\\
K_{m}\frac{e_{m}}{N^{m}},&\text{ if }i=j,\ R_{i}=A,\ R_{j}=D_{m},\,m\in\{0,1,2\},\\
\frac{e_{m}}{N^{m}},&\text{ if }i=j,\ R_{i}=D_{m},\ R_{j}=A,\,m\in\{0,1,2\},\\
0,&\mbox{ otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

The corresponding semigroup of the kernel
b^{[N^{2}]}(\cdot,\cdot)
is denoted by
b^{[N^{2}]}_{t}(\cdot,\cdot)
.

To prove Lemma
11.23
we will use the following lemma, which was proved in
[
GHO20
]
[Lemma 6.1] and for our setting reads as follows.

Lemma 11.24
.

[First and second moment]

Let
\mathbb{E}_{z^{[N^{2}]}}
the expectation if the process start from some state
z^{[N^{2}]}\in([0,1]\times[0,1]^{3})^{[N^{2}]}
. For
z^{[N^{2}]}\in([0,1]\times[0,1]^{2})^{[N^{2}]}
,
t\geq 0
and
(i,R_{i}),(j,R_{j})\in[N^{2}]\times\{A,D_{0},D_{1},D_{2}\}
,

(11.169)

\mathbb{E}_{z^{[N^{2}]}}[z^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,R_{i})}(t)]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(k,R_{k})\in\\
\Omega_{N}\times\{A,D_{0},D_{1},D_{2}\}\end{subarray}}b^{[N^{2}]}_{t}\big((i,R_{i}),(k,R_{k})\big)\,z^{[N^{2}]}_{(k,R_{k})}

and

(11.170)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{z^{[N^{2}]}}[z^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,R_{i})}(t)z^{[N^{2}]}_{(j,R_{j})}(t)]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(k,R_{k}),(l,R_{l})\in\\
\Omega_{N}\times\{A,D_{0},D_{1},D_{2}\}\end{subarray}}b^{[N^{2}]}_{t}\big((i,R_{i}),(k,R_{k})\big)\,b^{[N^{2}]}_{t}\big((j,R_{j}),(l,R_{l})\big)\,z^{[N^{2}]}_{(k,R_{k})}z^{[N^{2}]}_{(l,R_{l})}

\displaystyle+\,2\int_{0}^{{t}}{\rm d}s\sum_{k\in\Omega_{N}}b^{[N^{2}]}_{(t-s)}((i,R_{i}),(k,A))\,b^{[N^{2}]}_{(t-s)}((j,R_{j}),(k,A))\,\mathbb{E}_{z}^{[N^{2}]}[g(x^{[N^{2}]}_{k}(s))].

Proof of Lemma
11.23
.

The argument is given in such a way that it can easily be generalised to more complicated systems, which we treat later. Let
\bar{t}(N)=N^{2}t_{2}+Nt_{1}+t_{0}
. We will show that if
i,j\in[N]_{i}
, i.e.,
i
and
j
belong to the same
1
-block, then

(11.171)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(y^{[N^{2}]}_{i,1}(\bar{t}(N))-y^{[N^{2}]}_{j,1}(\bar{t}(N))\right)^{2}\right]=0.

This implies (
11.167
). By Lemma
11.24
, we can write

(11.172)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(y^{[N^{2}]}_{i,1}(\bar{t}(N))-y^{[N^{2}]}_{j,1}(\bar{t}(N))\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(k,R_{k}),(l,R_{l})\in\\
[N^{2}]\times\{A,D_{0},D_{1},D_{2}\}\end{subarray}}\left(b^{[N^{2}]}_{\bar{t}(N)}\left((i,D_{1}),(k,R_{k})\right)-b^{[N^{2}]}_{\bar{t}(N)}\left((j,D_{1}),(k,R_{k})\right)\right)

\displaystyle\times\left(b^{[N^{2}]}_{\bar{t}(N)}\left((i,D_{1}),(l,R_{l})\right)-b^{[N^{2}]}_{\bar{t}(N)}\left((j,D_{1}),(l,R_{l})\right)\right)\mathbb{E}[z^{[N^{2}]}_{(k,R_{k})}z^{[N^{2}]}_{(l,R_{l})}]

\displaystyle+\,2\int_{0}^{\bar{t}(N)}{\rm d}s\sum_{k\in[N^{2}]}\left(b^{[N^{2}]}_{(\bar{t}(N)-s)}((i,D_{1}),(k,A))-b^{[N^{2}]}_{(\bar{t}(N)-s)}((j,D_{1}),(k,A))\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\times\mathbb{E}[g(x^{[N^{2}]}_{k}(s))].

Using a coupling argument, we show that both terms in (
11.172
) tend to
0
as
N\to\infty
. To prove that the first term tends to
0
, we will show that

(11.173)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(k,R_{k})\in\\
[N^{2}]\times\{A,D_{0},D_{1},D_{2}\}\end{subarray}}\left|b^{[N^{2}]}_{\bar{t}(N)}\left((i,D_{1}),(k,R_{k})\right)-b^{[N^{2}]}_{\bar{t}(N)}\left((j,D_{1}),(k,R_{k})\right)\right|=0.

To do so, let
(RW^{[N^{2}]}(t))_{t\geq 0}
and
(RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}(t))_{t\geq 0}
be two independent random walks, starting from
RW^{[N^{2}]}(0)=(i,D_{1})
and
RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}(0)=(j,D_{1})
, where
i
and
j
are in the same
1
-block. Let
RW^{[N^{2}]}
and
RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}
both evolve according to the kernel
b^{[N^{2}]}(\cdot,\cdot)
, so
b^{[N^{2}]}_{t}(\cdot,\cdot)
is their corresponding semigroup. Since
RW^{[N^{2}]}
and
RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}
both start from the colour
1
-seed-bank, we can perfectly couple their switches between
A
,
D_{0}
,
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
. Since this implies that both
RW^{[N^{2}]}
and
RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}
are always simultaneously active, we can also couple the times when they jump due to migration and the distance over which they migrate. However, we do not couple their migrations, i.e.
RW^{[N^{2}]}
and
RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}
jump at the same time and over the same distance, but they can jump to different sites. This implies that the coupled process
(RW^{[N^{2}]}(t),RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}(t))_{t\geq 0}
has transition rates

(11.174)

((i,R_{i}),(j,R_{i}))\rightarrow\begin{cases}((k,A),(l,A))&\text{ if }R_{i}=R_{j}=A\text{ and }d_{[N^{2}]}(i,k)=d_{[N^{2}]}(j,l)\\
&\text{ at rate }1_{\{d_{[N^{2}]}(i,j)\leq 1\}}\frac{c_{0}}{N}+\frac{c_{1}}{N^{3}},\\
((i,D_{m}),(j,D_{m}))&\text{ if }R_{i}=R_{j}=A\text{ at rate }\frac{K_{m}e_{m}}{N^{m}},\,m\in\{0,1,2\},\\
((i,A),(j,A))&\text{ if }R_{i}=R_{j}=D_{m}\text{ at rate }\frac{e_{m}}{N^{m}},\,m\in\{0,1,2\}.\end{cases}

Define the event

(11.175)

H_{t}^{[N^{2}]}=\{RW^{[N^{2}]}\text{ has migrated at least once up to time }t\}.

Note that if
H_{t}^{[N^{2}]}
has happened, then also
RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}
has migrated. Hence

(11.176)

\displaystyle b^{[N^{2}]}_{\bar{t}(N)}

\displaystyle\left((i,D_{1}),(k,R_{k})\right)-b^{[N^{2}]}_{\bar{t}(N)}\left((j,D_{1}),(k,R_{k})\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{(i,D_{1})}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(\bar{t}(N))=(k,R_{k})\right)-\mathbb{P}_{(j,D_{1})}\left(RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}(\bar{t}(N))=(k,R_{k})\right)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{(i,D_{1}),(j,D_{1})}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(\bar{t}(N))=(k,R_{k}),\,H_{\bar{t}(N)}^{[N^{2}]}\right)

\displaystyle+\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{(i,D_{1}),(j,D_{1})}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(\bar{t}(N))=(k,R_{k}),\,(H_{\bar{t}(N)}^{[N^{2}]})^{c}\right)

\displaystyle-\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{(i,D_{1}),(j,D_{1})}\left(RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}(\bar{t}(N))=(k,R_{k}),\,H_{\bar{t}(N)}^{[N^{2}]}\right)

\displaystyle-\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{(i,D_{1}),(j,D_{1})}\left(RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}(\bar{t}(N))=(k,R_{k}),\,(H_{\bar{t}(N)}^{[N^{2}]})^{c}\right)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{(i,D_{1}),(j,D_{1})}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(\bar{t}(N))=(k,R_{k}),\,(H_{\bar{t}(N)}^{[N^{2}]})^{c}\right)

\displaystyle-\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{(i,D_{1}),(j,D_{1})}\left(RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}(\bar{t}(N))=(k,R_{k}),\,(H_{\bar{t}(N)}^{[N^{2}]})^{c}\right),

where the last equality follows because, once the random walks have just jumped once,
RW^{[N^{2}]}
and
RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}
are uniformly distributed over
[N]\times A
if their jump horizon was
1
and they are uniformly distributed of
[N^{2}]\times A
if they jumped over distance
2
. Hence if
H_{t}^{[N^{2}]}
has occurred, then
RW^{[N^{2}]}
and
RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}
have the same distribution. Therefore

(11.177)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(k,R_{k})\in\\
[N^{2}]\times\{A,D_{0},D_{1},D_{2}\}\end{subarray}}\left|b^{[N^{2}]}_{\bar{t}(N)}\left((i,D_{1}),(k,R_{k})\right)-b^{[N^{2}]}_{\bar{t}(N)}\left((j,D_{1}),(k,R_{k})\right)\right|\leq 2\tilde{\mathbb{P}}((H_{\bar{t}(N)}^{[N^{2}]})^{c})

and we are left to show that

(11.178)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\tilde{\mathbb{P}}((H_{\bar{t}(N)}^{[N^{2}]})^{c})=0.

The event
(H_{\bar{t}(N)}^{[N^{2}]})^{c}
occurs either when the random walks do not wake up before time
\bar{t}(N)
or when the random walks wake up before time
\bar{t}(N)
but do not migrate. By the coupling we only have to consider one of the random walks. Therefore the probability that
RW^{[N^{2}]}
and
RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}
do not wake up before time
\bar{t}(N)
is given by

(11.179)

\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{(i,D_{1}),(j,D_{1})}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}\text{ does not wake up before }\bar{t}(N)\right)={\rm e}^{-\frac{e_{1}}{N}\bar{t}(N)}={\rm e}^{-\frac{e_{1}(N^{2}t_{2}+Nt_{1}+t_{0})}{N}}

and hence

(11.180)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{(i,D_{1}),(j,D_{1})}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}\text{ does not wake up before }\bar{t}(N)\right)=0.

The probability that the random walks do wake up, but do not migrate is a little more complicated, since each time they wake up with positive probability they go to sleep before they migrate. Define

(11.181)

\displaystyle C^{[N^{2}]}(t)

\displaystyle=\{\#\,\text{times $RW^{[N^{2}]}$ gets active before time }t\},

\displaystyle T_{A}^{[N^{2}]}(t)

\displaystyle=\{\text{total time $RW^{[N^{2}]}$ is active up to time }t\},

\displaystyle T_{D}^{[N^{2}]}(t)

\displaystyle=\{\text{total time $RW^{[N^{2}]}$ is dormant up to time }t\}.

Thus,
C^{[N^{2}]}(t)
counts the number of active/dormant cycles. Define
T^{[N^{2}]}_{A,n},\ T^{[N^{2}]}_{D,n}
as the active respectively, dormant time during the
n
th cycle. Define

(11.182)

\chi=K_{0}e_{0}+\frac{K_{1}e_{1}}{N}+\frac{K_{2}e_{2}}{N^{2}},

so
\chi
is the total rate at which
RW
and
RW^{\prime}
become dormant when they are active. Define

(11.183)

c=c_{0}+\frac{c_{1}}{N},

so
c
is the total rate at which
RW
and
RW^{\prime}
migrate when they are active. Then

(11.184)

T^{[N^{2}]}_{A}(t)=\sum_{n=1}^{C^{[N^{2}]}(t)}T^{[N^{2}]}_{A,n},\qquad T^{[N^{2}]}_{D}(t)=\sum_{n=1}^{C^{[N^{2}]}(t)}T^{[N^{2}]}_{D,n},

where
T^{[N^{2}]}_{A,n}\,{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{d}}}\,\exp(\chi)
and
T^{[N^{2}]}_{D,n}\,{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{d}}}\,\frac{1}{\chi}K_{0}e_{0}\,\exp(e_{0})+\frac{1}{\chi}\frac{K_{1}e_{1}}{N}\,\exp(\frac{e_{1}}{N})+\frac{1}{\chi}\,\frac{K_{2}e_{2}}{N^{2}}\,\exp(\frac{e_{2}}{N^{2}})
. Once awake,
RW^{[N^{2}]}
migrates at rate
c
and hence the probability to migrate before time
\bar{t}(N)
is given by
1-{\rm e}^{-cT^{[N^{2}]}_{A}(\bar{t}(N))}
. Therefore we are left to show that

(11.185)

\lim_{N\to\infty}cT_{A}^{[N^{2}]}(\bar{t}(N))=\lim_{N\to\infty}c\sum_{n=1}^{C^{[N^{2}]}(\bar{t}(N))}T^{[N^{2}]}_{A,n}=\infty,\qquad a.s.

Since
T^{[N^{2}]}_{A,n}\,{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{d}}}\,\text{exp}(\chi)
, it is enough to show that

(11.186)

\lim_{N\to\infty}C^{[N^{2}]}(\bar{t}(N))=\infty\qquad a.s.

To do so, we assume the contrary, i.e., there exists an
R\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
\bar{N}\in\mathbb{N}
there exists an
N>\bar{N}
such that

(11.187)

\mathbb{P}_{(i,D_{1})}(C^{[N^{2}]}(\bar{t}(N))\leq R)>0.

Let
L(N)
be such that
\lim_{N\to\infty}L(N)=\infty
and
\lim_{N\to\infty}L(N)/N=0
. Note that, by (
11.180
), we can condition on the first wake-up time and estimate

(11.188)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{(i,D_{1})}(C^{[N^{2}]}(\bar{t}(N))\leq R)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\bar{t}(N)}{\rm d}s\,\mathbb{P}_{(i,A)}(C^{[N^{2}]}(\bar{t}(N)-s)\leq R)\ \frac{e_{1}}{N}{\rm e}^{-\frac{e_{1}}{N}s}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\bar{t}(N)-L(N)}{\rm d}s\,\mathbb{P}_{(i,A)}(C^{[N^{2}]}(\bar{t}(N)-s)\leq R)\ \frac{e_{1}}{N}{\rm e}^{-\frac{e_{1}}{N}s}

\displaystyle+\int_{\bar{t}(N)-L(N)}^{\bar{t}(N)}{\rm d}s\,\mathbb{P}_{(i,A)}(C^{[N^{2}]}(\bar{t}(N)-s)\leq R)\ \frac{e_{1}}{N}{\rm e}^{-\frac{e_{1}}{N}s}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{(i,A)}(C^{[N^{2}]}(L(N))\leq R)+{\rm e}^{-\frac{e_{1}}{N}\bar{t}(N)}\left[{\rm e}^{\frac{e_{1}}{N}L(N)}-1\right].

Note that the second term in the last inequality tends to
0
as
N\to\infty
. For the first term, note that we are now looking at time
L(N)
, i.e., time scale
N^{0}
. Since

(11.189)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{i,A}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}\text{ jumps to $D_{1}$ or $D_{2}$ before time }L(N)\right)

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}1-{\rm e}^{-\left(\frac{K_{1}e_{1}}{N}+\frac{K_{2}e_{2}}{N^{2}}\right)L(N)}=0.

we have
\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{i,A}\left(\{RW^{[N^{2}]}(s)\in\{A,D_{0}\}\text{ for }s\in[0,L(N)]\}\right)=1
. Hence, conditioned on the event
\{RW^{[N^{2}]}\in\{A,D_{0}\}\}
,
T_{A,n}^{[N^{2}]}\,{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{d}}}\,\exp(K_{0}e_{0})
and
T_{D,n}^{[N^{2}]}\,{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{d}}}\,\exp(e_{0})
. We therefore obtain

(11.190)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{(i,A)}(C^{[N^{2}]}(L(N))\leq R)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{(i,A)}\left(\sum_{n=1}^{R}(T_{A,n}^{[N^{2}]}+T_{D,n}^{[N^{2}]})\geq L(N)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{R}{L(N)}\mathbb{E}_{(i,A)}\left[T_{A,n}^{[N^{2}]}+T_{D,n}^{[N^{2}]}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{R}{L(N)}\left[\frac{1}{K_{0}e_{0}}+\frac{1}{e_{0}}\right].

Taking the limit
N\to\infty
in (
11.190
) and combining this with (
11.188
), we conclude that (
11.186
) indeed holds. Hence also (
11.178
) and (
11.173
) hold.

We are left to show that

(11.191)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}2\int_{0}^{\bar{t}(N)}\,{\rm d}s\sum_{k\in[N^{2}]}\left(b^{[N^{2}]}_{(\bar{t}(N)-s)}((i,D_{1}),(k,A))-b^{[N^{2}]}_{(\bar{t}(N)-s)}((j,D_{1}),(k,A))\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\times\mathbb{E}[g(x_{k}(s))]=0.

Also here the idea is to make a similar coupling. As soon as the random walks migrate, they are equally distributed. On time scale
N
, after waking up from the colour
1
seed-bank they will almost immediately migrate, since migration happens on time scale
1
, i.e., by time
L(N)
they have migrated with probability tending to
1
. This will again be the key to show that (
11.191
) tends to
0
as
N\to\infty
.

Note that, by (
11.172
),

(11.192)

\displaystyle\Bigg|2\int_{0}^{\bar{t}(N)}{\rm d}s\sum_{k\in[N^{2}]}\left(b^{[N^{2}]}_{(\bar{t}(N)-s)}((i,D_{1}),(k,A))-b^{[N^{2}]}_{(\bar{t}(N)-s)}((j,D_{1}),(k,A))\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\times\mathbb{E}[g(x_{k}^{[N^{2}]}(s))]\Bigg|\leq 2.

We will again use the coupling in (
11.175
). Define

(11.193)

\tau^{[N^{2}]}=\inf\{t\geq 0:RW^{[N^{2}]}(t)=(k,A)\text{ for some }k\in[N^{2}]\}.

Then, for all
l\in[N^{2}]
,

(11.194)

\mathbb{P}_{(l,D_{1})}(\tau^{[N^{2}]}\leq t)=1-{\rm e}^{-\frac{e_{1}}{N}t}.

Setting
s=\bar{t}(N)-s
, we can rewrite the integral in (
11.191
) as

(11.195)

\displaystyle 2\int_{0}^{\bar{t}(N)}{\rm d}s\sum_{k\in[N^{2}]}\Big(\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,D_{1}),(j,D_{1})}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(s)=(k,A)\right)

\displaystyle-\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,D_{1}),(j,D_{1})}\left(RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}(s)=(k,A),\right)\Big)^{2}\mathbb{E}[g(x_{k}^{[N^{2}]}(\bar{t}(N)-s))]

\displaystyle=2\int_{0}^{\bar{t}(N)}{\rm d}s\sum_{k\in[N^{2}]}\Bigg[\int_{0}^{s}{\rm d}r\,\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(\tau^{[N^{2}]}=r)

\displaystyle\left(\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(s-r)=(k,A)\right)-\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}(s-r)=(k,A)\right)\right)\Bigg]

\displaystyle\times\Big(\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,D_{1}),(j,D_{1})}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(s)=(k,A)\right)-\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,D_{1}),(j,D_{1})}\left(RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}(s)=(k,A)\right)\Big)

\displaystyle\times\mathbb{E}[g(x_{k}^{[N^{2}]}(\bar{t}(N)-s))].

In what follows we will abbreviate

(11.196)

\displaystyle P_{\Delta^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}}(t,((l,R_{l}),(j,R_{j})))

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(t)=(l,R_{l})\right)-\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}(t)=(j,R_{j})\right),

and similarly, for
m\in\{0,1,2\}
,

(11.197)

\displaystyle P_{\Delta^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,D_{m}),(j,D_{m})}}(t,((l,R_{l}),(j,R_{j})))

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{(i,D_{m}),(j,D_{m})}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(t)=(l,R_{l})\right)-\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{(i,D_{m}),(j,D_{m})}\left(RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}(t)=(j,R_{j})\right).

By (
11.192
), we can use Fubini to swap the order of integration and subsequently substitute
v=s-r
, to obtain

(11.198)

\displaystyle 2\int_{0}^{\bar{t}(N)}\,{\rm d}r\,\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(\tau^{[N^{2}]}=r)\int_{0}^{\bar{t}(N)-r}{\rm d}v\sum_{k\in[N^{2}]}P_{\Delta^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}}(v,((k,A),(k,A)))

\displaystyle\times P_{\Delta^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,D_{1}),(j,D_{1})}}(v+r,((k,A),(k,A)))\mathbb{E}[g(x_{k}^{[N^{2}]}(\bar{t}(N)-r-v))]

\displaystyle=2\int_{0}^{\bar{t}(N)}{\rm d}r\,\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(\tau^{[N^{2}]}=r)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(l,R_{l})\in\\
[N^{2}]\times\{A,D_{0},D_{1},D_{2}\}\end{subarray}}\Big({\mathbb{P}}_{(i,D_{1})}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(r)=(l,R_{l})\right)-{\mathbb{P}}_{(j,D_{1})}\left(RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}(r)=(l,R_{l})\right)\Big)

\displaystyle\times\int_{0}^{\bar{t}(N)-r}{\rm d}v\,\sum_{k\in[N^{2}]}P_{\Delta^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}}(v,((k,A),(k,A)))

\displaystyle\times\mathbb{P}_{(l,R_{l})}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(v)=(k,A)\right)\mathbb{E}[g(x_{k}^{[N^{2}]}(\bar{t}(N)-r-v))],

where in the last equality we use that the random walks move according to the same kernel
b(\cdot,\cdot)
. We can continue by writing

(11.199)

\displaystyle 2\int_{0}^{\bar{t}(N)}{\rm d}r\,\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(\tau^{[N^{2}]}=r)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(l,R_{l})\in\\
[N^{2}]\times\{A,D_{0},D_{1},D_{2}\}\end{subarray}}\Big({\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,D_{1})}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(r)=(l,R_{l})\right)-{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(j,D_{1})}\left(RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}(r)=(l,R_{l})\right)\Big)

\displaystyle\times\int_{0}^{\bar{t}(N)-r}{\rm d}v\,\sum_{k\in[N^{2}]}\bigg(\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(v)=(k,A)\right)

\displaystyle-\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}(v)=(k,A)\right)\bigg)

\displaystyle\times\mathbb{P}^{[N^{2}]}_{(l,R_{l})}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(v)=(k,A)\right)\mathbb{E}[g(x_{k}^{[N^{2}]}(\bar{t}(N)-r-v))]

\displaystyle=2\int_{0}^{\bar{t}(N)}{\rm d}r\,\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(\tau^{[N^{2}]}=r)\int_{0}^{r}{\rm d}u\,\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(\tau^{[N^{2}]}=u)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(l,R_{l})\in\\
[N^{2}]\times\{A,D_{0},D_{1},D_{2}\}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\Big(\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(r-u)=(l,R_{l})\right)-\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}(r-u)=(l,R_{l})\right)\Big)

\displaystyle\times\int_{0}^{\bar{t}(N)-r}{\rm d}v\,\sum_{k\in[N^{2}]}\bigg(\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(v)=(k,A)\right)

\displaystyle-\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}(v)=(k,A)\right)\bigg)

\displaystyle\times\mathbb{P}^{[N^{2}]}_{(l,R_{l})}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(v)=(k,A)\right)\mathbb{E}[g(x_{k}^{[N^{2}]}(\bar{t}(N)-r-v))]

\displaystyle+2\int_{0}^{\bar{t}(N)}{\rm d}r\,\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(\tau^{[N^{2}]}=r)\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(\tau^{[N^{2}]}\geq r)

\displaystyle\times\int_{0}^{\bar{t}(N)-r}{\rm d}v\,\sum_{k\in[N^{2}]}\bigg(\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(v)=(k,A)\right)

\displaystyle-\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}(v)=(k,A)\right)\bigg)

\displaystyle\times\Big(\mathbb{P}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,D_{1})}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(v)=(k,A)\right)-\mathbb{P}^{[N^{2}]}_{(j,D_{1})}\left(RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}(v)=(k,A)\right)\Big)

\displaystyle\times\mathbb{E}[g(x_{k}^{[N^{2}]}(\bar{t}(N)-r-v))].

We will show that both terms in the last equality of (
11.199
) tends to
0
as
N\to\infty
.

For the first term note that, by (
11.170
), (
11.172
) and (
11.194
), we have

(11.200)

\displaystyle 2\int_{0}^{\bar{t}(N)}{\rm d}r\,\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(\tau^{[N^{2}]}=r)\int_{0}^{r}{\rm d}u\,\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(\tau^{[N^{2}]}=u)

\displaystyle\times\Bigg[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(l,R_{l})\in\\
[N^{2}]\times\{A,D_{0},D_{1},D_{2}\}\end{subarray}}\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(r-u)=(l,R_{l})\right)

\displaystyle-\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}(r-u)=(l,R_{l})\right)\Bigg]

\displaystyle\times\int_{0}^{\bar{t}(N)-r}{\rm d}v\,\sum_{k\in[N^{2}]}\bigg(\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(v)=(k,A)\right)

\displaystyle-\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}(v)=(k,A)\right)\bigg)

\displaystyle\times\mathbb{P}^{[N^{2}]}_{(l,R_{l})}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(v)=(k,A)\right)\mathbb{E}[g(x_{k}(\bar{t}(N)-r-v))]

\displaystyle\leq 4\int_{0}^{\bar{t}(N)}{\rm d}r\,\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(\tau^{[N^{2}]}=r)\int_{0}^{r}{\rm d}u\,\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(\tau^{[N^{2}]}=u)

\displaystyle\times\Bigg|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(l,R_{l})\in\\
[N^{2}]\times\{A,D_{0},D_{1},D_{2}\}\end{subarray}}\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(r-u)=(l,R_{l})\right)

\displaystyle-\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}(r-u)=(l,R_{l})\right)\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq 4\int_{0}^{\bar{t}(N)}{\rm d}r\,\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(\tau^{[N^{2}]}=r)\int_{0}^{r-L(N)}{\rm d}u\,\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(\tau^{[N^{2}]}=u)

\displaystyle\times\Bigg|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(l,R_{l})\in\\
[N^{2}]\times\{A,D_{0},D_{1},D_{2}\}\end{subarray}}\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(r-u)=(l,R_{l})\right)

\displaystyle-\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}(r-u)=(l,R_{l})\right)\Bigg|

\displaystyle+8\int_{L(N)}^{\bar{t}(N)}{\rm d}r\,\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(\tau^{[N^{2}]}=r)\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(\tau^{[N^{2}]}\in[r-L(N),r])+8\mathbb{P}[\tau^{[N^{2}]}\in[0,L(N)]]

\displaystyle\leq 4\int_{0}^{\bar{t}(N)}{\rm d}r\,\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(\tau^{[N^{2}]}=r)\int_{0}^{r-L(N)}{\rm d}u\,\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(\tau^{[N^{2}]}=u)

\displaystyle\times\Bigg|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(l,R_{l})\in\\
[N^{2}]\times\{A,D_{0},D_{1},D_{2}\}\end{subarray}}\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(r-u)=(l,R_{l})\right)

\displaystyle-\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}(r-u)=(l,R_{l})\right)\Bigg|

\displaystyle+16[1-{\rm e}^{-\frac{e_{1}}{N}L(N)}].

Hence the last term in the last inequality tends to
0
as
N\to\infty
.

To show that the first term in the last inequality tends to
0
we use the coupling again. Recall the definition of
H_{t}^{[N^{2}]}
in (
11.175
). Note that we can rewrite the sum as

(11.201)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(l,R_{l})\in\\
[N^{2}]\times\{A,D_{0},D_{1},D_{2}\}\end{subarray}}\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(r-u)=(l,R_{l})\right)-\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}(r-u)=(l,R_{l})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(l,R_{l})\in\\
[N^{2}]\times\{A,D_{0},D_{1},D_{2}\}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(l^{\prime},R_{l^{\prime}})\in\\
[N^{2}]\times\{A,D_{0},D_{1},D_{2}\}\end{subarray}}\Big({\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A)}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(L(N))=(l^{\prime},R_{l}^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 17.00024pt\hskip 17.00024pt\hskip 17.00024pt\hskip 17.00024pt\hskip 17.00024pt\hskip 17.00024pt\hskip 17.00024pt-{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}(L(N))=(l^{\prime},R^{\prime}_{l})\right)\Big)

\displaystyle\times\mathbb{P}_{(l^{\prime},R_{l}^{\prime})}^{[N^{2}]}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(r-u-L(N))=(l,R_{l})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(l^{\prime},R_{l^{\prime}})\in\\
[N^{2}]\times\{A,D_{0},D_{1},D_{2}\}\end{subarray}}\left({\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A)}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(L(N))=(l^{\prime},R_{l}^{\prime})\right)-{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(j,A)}\left(RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}(L(N))=(l^{\prime},R^{\prime}_{l})\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(l^{\prime},R_{l^{\prime}})\in\\
[N^{2}]\times\{A,D_{0},D_{1},D_{2}\}\end{subarray}}\Big(\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(L(N))=(l^{\prime},R_{l}^{\prime}),(H_{t}^{[N^{2}]})^{c}\right)

\displaystyle-\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}(L(N))=(l^{\prime},R^{\prime}_{l}),(H_{t}^{[N^{2}]})^{c}\right)\Big)

\displaystyle\leq 2\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left((H_{t}^{[N^{2}]})^{c}\right).

To show that

(11.202)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left((H_{t}^{[N^{2}]})^{c}\right)=0,

we can use a similar strategy as between (
11.188
) and (
11.190
), but note that we now start from two active sites instead of two
1
-dormant sites. Therefore (
11.186
) directly follows from (
11.189
) and (
11.190
).

To show the second term in (
11.199
) tends to
0
, we write it as

(11.203)

\displaystyle 2\int_{0}^{\bar{t}(N)}{\rm d}r\,\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(\tau^{[N^{2}]}=r)\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(\tau^{[N^{2}]}\geq r)

\displaystyle\times\int_{0}^{\bar{t}(N)-r}{\rm d}v\,\sum_{k\in[N^{2}]}\left(\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(v)=(k,A)\right)-\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}(v)=(k,A)\right)\right)

\displaystyle\times\left[\mathbb{P}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,D_{1})}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(v)=(k,A)\right)-\mathbb{P}^{[N^{2}]}_{(j,D_{1})}\left(RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}(v)=(k,A)\right)\right]\mathbb{E}[g(x_{k}^{[N^{2}]}(\bar{t}(N)-r-v))]

\displaystyle=2\int_{0}^{\bar{t}(N)}{\rm d}r\,\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(\tau^{[N^{2}]}=r)\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(\tau^{[N^{2}]}\geq r)

\displaystyle\times\int_{0}^{\bar{t}(N)-r}{\rm d}v\,\sum_{k\in[N^{2}]}\left(\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(v)=(k,A)\right)-\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}(v)=(k,A)\right)\right)

\displaystyle\times\int_{0}^{v}\,{\rm d}u\,\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}(\tau^{[N^{2}]}=u)

\displaystyle\Big(\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(v-u)=(k,A)\right)-\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}(v-u)=(k,A)\right)\Big)

\displaystyle\times\mathbb{E}[g(x_{k}^{[N^{2}]}(\bar{t}(N)-r-v))].

Changing the order of integration and setting
w=v-u
, we obtain

(11.204)

\displaystyle 2\int_{0}^{\bar{t}(N)}{\rm d}r\,\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(\tau^{[N^{2}]}=r)\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(\tau^{[N^{2}]}\geq r)

\displaystyle\times\int_{0}^{\bar{t}(N)-r}{\rm d}u\,\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}(\tau^{[N^{2}]}=u)\int_{u}^{\bar{t}(N)-r}\,{\rm d}v\,

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in[N^{2}]}\left[\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(v)=(k,A)\right)-\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}(v)=(k,A)\right)\right]

\displaystyle\times\left[\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(v-u)=(k,A)\right)-\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}(v-u)=(k,A)\right)\right]

\displaystyle\times\mathbb{E}[g(x_{k}^{[N^{2}]}(\bar{t}(N)-r-v))]

\displaystyle=2\int_{0}^{\bar{t}(N)}{\rm d}r\,\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(\tau^{[N^{2}]}=r)\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(\tau^{[N^{2}]}\geq r)

\displaystyle\times\int_{0}^{\bar{t}(N)-r}{\rm d}u\,\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}(\tau^{[N^{2}]}=u)\int_{0}^{\bar{t}(N)-r-u}\,{\rm d}w\,

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in[N^{2}]}\left[\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(w+u)=(k,A)\right)-\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}(w+u)=(k,A)\right)\right]

\displaystyle\times\left[\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(w)=(k,A)\right)-\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}(w)=(k,A)\right)\right]

\displaystyle\times\mathbb{E}[g(x_{k}^{[N^{2}]}(\bar{t}(N)-r-u-w))].

This can be rewritten as

(11.205)

\displaystyle 2\int_{0}^{\bar{t}(N)}{\rm d}r\,\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(\tau^{[N^{2}]}=r)\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(\tau^{[N^{2}]}\geq r)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\bar{t}(N)-r}{\rm d}u\,\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}(\tau^{[N^{2}]}=u)

\displaystyle\times\sum_{(l,R_{l})\in[N^{2}]}\left[\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(u)=(l,R_{l})\right)-\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}(u)=(l,R_{l})\right)\right]

\displaystyle\times\int_{0}^{\bar{t}(N)-r-u}\,{\rm d}w\,\sum_{k\in[N^{2}]}\left[\mathbb{P}^{[N^{2}]}_{(l,R_{l})}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(w)=(k,A)\right)\right]

\displaystyle\times\left[\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(w)=(k,A)\right)-\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}(w)=(k,A)\right)\right]

\displaystyle\times\mathbb{E}[g(x_{k}^{[N^{2}]}(\bar{t}(N)-r-u-w))]

\displaystyle\leq 8\int_{L(N)}^{\bar{t}(N)}{\rm d}r\,\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(\tau^{[N^{2}]}=r)\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(\tau^{[N^{2}]}\geq r)

\displaystyle\int_{L(N)}^{\bar{t}(N)-r}{\rm d}u\,\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}(\tau^{[N^{2}]}=u)

\displaystyle\times\sum_{(l,R_{l})\in[N^{2}]}\left[\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(u)=(l,R_{l})\right)-\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}(u)=(l,R_{l})\right)\right]

\displaystyle+8\int_{L(N)}^{\bar{t}(N)}{\rm d}r\,\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(\tau^{[N^{2}]}=r)\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(\tau^{[N^{2}]}\geq r)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{L(N)}{\rm d}u\,\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}(\tau^{[N^{2}]}=u)

\displaystyle\times\sum_{(l,R_{l})\in[N^{2}]}\left[\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{[N^{2}]}(u)=(l,R_{l})\right)-\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{[N^{2}]}_{(i,A),(j,A)}\left(RW^{\prime[N^{2}]}(u)=(l,R_{l})\right)\right]

\displaystyle+16\int_{0}^{L(N)}{\rm d}r\,\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(\tau^{[N^{2}]}=r)\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(\tau^{[N^{2}]}\geq r).

This tends to
0
by (
11.201
) and the reasoning below (
11.202
).
∎

11.3.9
Limiting evolution of the estimator processes

In this section we show that the results along the subsequences used in steps 5-8 of the scheme for the two-level three-colour mean-field system actually hold for all subsequences. Therefore the limiting evolution holds for
N\to\infty
. Recall that Lemma
11.23
tells us that all single
1
-dormant colonies equal the value of the
1
-dormant
1
-block average. Therefore the second assumption in (
11.80
) in Proposition (
11.8
) can be replaced by

(11.206)

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathcal{L}\left[\left(Y_{2,0}^{[N_{k}^{2}]}(N^{2}_{k}t_{2})\right)\Big|{\bf\Theta}^{\mathrm{aux},(1),[N_{k}^{2}]}(N^{2}_{k}t_{2}+N_{k}t_{1})\right]=P^{{z}_{1}^{\mathrm{eff}}(t_{1})},

since, by Lemma
11.23
, the limiting law

(11.207)

\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathcal{L}\left[\left(Y_{1,0}^{[N_{k}^{2}]}(N^{2}_{k}t_{2})\right)\right]

is completely determined by the first line in (
11.80
). Hence, the assumptions in Proposition
11.13
and Lemma
11.21
can be weakened in the same way. Using that in Proposition
11.1
we assume (
11.37
) and (
11.38
), we find that the
2
-block convergence stated in Lemma
11.21
holds along all subsequences we choose in Step 5. We conclude that Proposition
11.1
(a) is indeed true. Combining Proposition
11.1
(a) with steps 1-4 of the scheme and Lemma
11.23
, we find that the assumptions in Proposition
11.13
are true for all subsequences, and we obtain the limiting evolution of the
1
-block estimator process. Projecting this limiting evolution onto the active
1
-block average and the
1
-dormant
1
-block average, we obtain Proposition
11.1
(b). Finally, combining Proposition
11.1
(a), steps1-4, and the fact that Proposition
11.13
is true along all subsequences, we obtain Proposition
11.1
(c) and (f).

11.3.10
Convergence in the Meyer-Zheng topology

In this section we show how the results on the effective and estimator processes can be use to show convergence of the full
1
- and
2
-block processes.

Lemma 11.25

(
[Convergence of
1
process in the Meyer-Zheng topology]).

Assume that for the
1
-block estimator process defined in (
11.21
)

(11.208)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathcal{L}\left[\left({\bf\Theta}^{\mathrm{aux},(1),[N^{2}]}(N^{2}t_{2}+Nt_{1})\right)_{t_{1}>0}\,\right]=\mathcal{L}\left[(z_{1}^{\mathrm{aux}}(t_{1}))_{t_{1}>0}\right],

where, conditional on
x^{\mathrm{eff}}_{2}(t_{2})=u
, the limit process is the unique solution of the SSDE in (
11.29
) with
\theta
replaced by
u
and with initial measure
\Gamma_{u}^{\mathrm{eff},(1)}
. Then

(11.209)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathcal{L}\left[\left({\bf\Theta}^{(1),[N^{2}]}(N^{2}t_{2}+Nt_{1})\right)_{t_{1}>0}\right]=\mathcal{L}\left[(z_{1}^{\Gamma^{(1)}(t_{2})}(t_{1}))_{t_{1}>0}\right]

\displaystyle\text{ in the Meyer-Zheng topology},

where
\Gamma^{(1)}(t_{2})
is defined as in (
11.45
) and
(z_{1}^{\Gamma^{(1)}(t_{2})}(t_{1}))_{t_{1}>0}
is the process moving according to (
11.27
) with initial measure
\Gamma^{(1)}(t_{2})
.

Proof.

By assumption
11.208
and Lemma
11.9
, we can proceed as in the proof of Proposition
10.16
to find (
11.209
).
∎

Lemma 11.26

(
[Convergence of
2
-block process in the Meyer-Zheng topology]).

Assume that for the effective
2
-block process defined in (
11.23
)

(11.210)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathcal{L}\left[\left({\bf\Theta}^{\mathrm{eff},(2),[N^{2}]}(N^{2}t_{2})\right)_{t_{2}>0}\,\right]=\mathcal{L}\left[(z_{2}^{\mathrm{aux}}(t_{2}))_{t_{2}>0}\right],

where
(z^{\mathrm{eff}}_{2}(t_{2}))_{t_{2}>0}
is the process evolving according to (
11.35
) and starting from
(\vartheta_{1},\theta_{y_{2}})
. Then for
the
2
-block estimator process defined in (
11.23
)

(11.211)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathcal{L}\left[\left({\bf\Theta}^{(2),[N^{2}]}(N^{2}t_{2})\right)_{t_{2}>0}\right]=\mathcal{L}\left[\left(z_{2}(t_{2})\right)_{t_{2}>0}\right]

\displaystyle\text{in the Meyer-Zheng topology},

where
\left(z_{2}(t_{2})\right)_{t_{2}>0}
is the process evolving according to (
11.34
) and starting in state
(\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{1},\theta_{y_{2}})
.

Proof.

Combining Lemmas
11.9
and
11.16
, we find for
t_{2}>0

(11.212)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\bar{\Theta}^{(2),[N^{2}]}(N^{2}t_{2})-\Theta_{x}^{(2),[N^{2}]}(N^{2}t_{2})\right|\right]

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\bar{\Theta}^{(2),[N^{2}]}(N^{2}t_{2})-\Theta_{y_{0}}^{(2),[N^{2}]}(N^{2}t_{2})\right|\right]

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\bar{\Theta}^{(2),[N^{2}]}(N^{2}t_{2})-\Theta_{y_{1}}^{(2),[N^{2}]}(N^{2}t_{2})\right|\right]

\displaystyle=0.

Therefore we can again proceed as in the proof of Proposition
10.16
to find (
11.211
).
∎

11.3.11
Proof of the two-level three-colour mean-field finite-systems scheme

In Section
11.3.9
we already proved Proposition
11.1
(a),(b),(c) and (f). The proof of Proposition
11.1
(d) follows from Proposition
11.1
(a) by applying Lemma
11.26
. The proof of Proposition
11.1
(e) follows from Proposition
11.1
(b) by applying Lemma
11.25
. This completes the proof of Proposition
11.1
.

12
Proofs:
N\to\infty
, hierarchical multi-scale limit theorems

In this section we prove the hierarchical multi-scale limit theorems stated in Theorem
4.2
and Theorem
4.4
. In Section
12.1
we first introduce the finite-level mean-field finite-systems scheme. In Section
12.2
we given an outline of how to prove the finite-level mean-field finite-systems scheme. In Section
12.3
we show how Theorems
4.2
–
4.4
can be obtained by a simple generalisation of the finite-level mean-field finite-systems scheme. The proof of the finite-level mean-field finite-systems scheme follows a similar lin3 of argument as in Section
11.2
once we incorporate more levels. Since the proofs for the finite-level mean-field systems scheme are similar as the proofs in Section
11.3
, we will not write out the full proof, but only give an outline and a sketch.

12.1
Finite-level mean-field finite-systems scheme and interaction chain

In this section we extend the two-level three-colour system to a
k
-level
(k+1)
-colour system with an “outside world” for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
. This outside world allows also the highest level, the
k
-block, to start from equilibrium. It is also needed to generalize the results in this subsection to the infinite hierarchical group.

\blacktriangleright
Definitions.

To set up the system, fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
and consider the geographic space ??obtained by truncating the hierarchical group
\Omega_{N}
(recall (
2.2
)) after hierarchical level
k+1
, i.e.,
\Omega_{N}^{k+1}=B_{k+1}(0)
the
(k+1)
-block centred at the origin (recall (
2.2
), (
2.4
) and Fig.
2
). Note that the
k+1
-block consists of
N

k
-blocks i.e.,
B_{k+1}(0)=\bigcup_{i=0}^{N}B_{k}(i)
and
B_{k+1}(0)=[N^{k+1}]
. The seed-bank in this model consists of the
k+2
layers corresponding to colours
\{0,\cdots,k\}\cup\{k+1\}.
On this space we again consider a restricted version of the SSDE in (
2.20
) to the geographic space
\Omega_{N}^{k+1}
. The migration kernel
a^{\Omega_{N}}(\cdot,\cdot)
is restricted to
\Omega_{N}^{k+1}
by setting all migration outside
B_{k+1}(0)
equal to 0, i.e.,

(12.1)

a^{[\Omega_{N}^{k+1}]}(\xi,\eta)=\sum_{l=1}^{k+1}1_{\big\{d_{\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(\xi,\eta)\leq l\big\}}\frac{c_{l}}{N^{l-1}}\frac{1}{N^{l}},

where
d_{\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}
is the hierarchical distance
d_{\Omega_{N}}
restricted to the space
\Omega_{N}^{k+1}
. The colour-
l
dormant population exchanges individuals with the active population at rates
\frac{e_{l}}{N^{l}}
,
\frac{K_{l}e_{l}}{N^{l}}
for all
0\leq l\leq k
. We set the interaction of the active population with the colour
(k+1)
-dormant population equal to
0
. This seed-bank is only needed later, namely for the “outside world”.

The state space of the finite-level mean-field system is

(12.2)

S=(\mathfrak{s}^{k+1})^{\Omega_{N}^{k+1}},\quad\mathfrak{s}^{k+1}=[0,1]\times[0,1]^{k+2},

and the system is denoted by

(12.3)

\displaystyle Z^{\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}=(Z^{\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(t))_{t\geq 0},\qquad Z^{\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(t)=(z^{\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{\xi}(t))_{\xi\in\Omega_{N}^{k+1}},

\displaystyle z^{\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{\xi}(t)=(x^{\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{\xi}(t),(y^{\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{\xi,m}(t))_{m=0}^{k+1}).

The components evolve according to the SSDE

(12.4)

\displaystyle{\rm d}x^{\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{\xi}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=1}^{k}\,\frac{c_{l-1}}{N^{l-1}}\,\frac{1}{N^{l}}\sum_{\eta\in B_{l}(\xi)}\,\left[x^{\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{\eta}(t)-x^{\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{\xi}(t)\right]\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle+\sqrt{g(x^{\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{\xi}(t))}\,{\rm d}w_{\xi}(t)+\sum_{m=0}^{k}\frac{K_{m}e_{m}}{N^{m}}\,\left[y^{\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{\xi,m}(t)-x^{\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{\xi}(t)\right]\,{\rm d}t,

\displaystyle{\rm d}y^{\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{\xi,m}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{e_{m}}{N^{m}}\,\left[x^{\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{\xi}(t)-y^{\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{\xi,m}(t)\right]\,{\rm d}t,\quad 0\leq m\leq k,

\displaystyle{\rm d}y^{\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{\xi,m}(t)

\displaystyle=0,\qquad\xi\in\Omega_{N}^{k+1},

with
B_{l}(\xi)
the ball of radius
l
around
\xi\in\Omega_{N}^{k+1}
.

Note that this system is the hierarchical SSDE in (
2.20
) with all interactions at distance
>k
switched off (i.e.,
c_{l}=0
for
l>k+1
), and also the exchange with dormant populations of colour
m>k
is switched off. As before, by
[
SS80
]
the martingale problem associated with (
12.4
) is well-posed, and for every initial state in
S
the SSDE has a unique strong solution. We will analyse (
12.4
) on time scales
1,N,N^{2},\cdots,N^{k}
. If for
0\leq l\leq k
time runs on time scale
N^{l}
, then we write
N^{l}t_{l}
with
t_{l}>0
.

To study the
k
-level mean-field system, we analyse the equivalent of the block averages defined in (
2.3
). For the
k
-level mean-field system these are given by

(12.5)

\displaystyle x^{\Omega^{k+1}_{N}}_{l}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{l}}\sum_{\eta\in B_{l}(0)}x^{\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{\eta}(N^{l}t),

\displaystyle y^{\Omega^{k+1}_{N}}_{m,l}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{l}}\sum_{\eta\in B_{l}(0)}y^{\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{\eta,m}(N^{l}t),\qquad 0\leq m\leq k+1,\qquad 0\leq l\leq k.

For
0\leq l\leq k
these block averages evolve according to the SSDE

(12.6)

\displaystyle{\rm d}{x}_{l}^{\Omega^{k+1}_{N}}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{k-(l-1)}\frac{c_{l+n-1}}{N^{n-1}}\big[{x}_{l+n}^{\Omega^{k+1}_{N}}(N^{-n}t)-{x}_{l}^{\Omega^{k+1}_{N}}(t)\big]\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle+\sqrt{\frac{1}{N^{l}}\sum_{i\in B_{l}(0)}g(x_{i}(N^{l}t))}\,\,{\rm d}w_{l}(t)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{m=0}^{k}N^{l}\frac{K_{m}e_{m}}{N^{m}}\big[{y}_{m,l}^{\Omega^{k+1}_{N}}(t)-{x}_{l}^{\Omega^{k+1}_{N}}(t)\big]\,{\rm d}t,

(12.7)

\displaystyle{\rm d}{y}_{m,l}^{\Omega^{k+1}_{N}}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle N^{l}\frac{e_{m}}{N^{m}}\big[{x}_{l}^{\Omega^{k+1}_{N}}(t)-{y}_{m,l}^{\Omega^{k+1}_{N}}(t)\big]\,{\rm d}t,\qquad 0\leq m\leq k,

(12.8)

\displaystyle{\rm d}y^{\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{k+1,l}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

In the limit as
N\to\infty
, the active
l
-block average feels a drift towards the active
(l+1)
-block average, which is not moving on time scale
N^{l}
, at rate
c_{l}
. The diffusion term for the
l
-block average becomes the average diffusion over the
l
-block. The drift of the active
l
-block average towards the
l
-block average of
m
-dormant populations
y_{m,l}^{\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}
with
m>l
vanishes in the limit as
N\to\infty
. Therefore, the
m>l

m
-dormant populations are
slow seed-banks
on space-time scale
l
. The
l
-block average of the colour-
l
dormant population
y_{l,l}^{\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}
has a non-trivial drift towards the active
l
-block average, written
x_{l}^{\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}
. Therefore the
l
-dormant population is the
effective seed-bank
on space-time scale
l
. For the colour
m
-dormant populations
y_{m,l}^{\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}
with
m<l
, we see that infinite rates appear. Therefore the
m
-dormant populations with
m<l
are
fast seed-banks
on space-time scale
l
. We again need the Meyer-Zheng topology to show that
\lim_{N\to\infty}y_{m,l}^{\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}=\lim_{N\to\infty}x_{l}^{\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}
. On space-time scale
l
, the colour-
l
dormant population is the effective seed-bank. To get rid of the infinite rates we again look at combinations. From the above discussion and the SSDE in (
12.6
)–(
12.6
), we see that if we consider the quantity

(12.9)

\frac{x_{l}^{\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(t)+\sum_{m=0}^{l-1}K_{m}y_{l,m}^{\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(t)}{1+\sum_{m=0}^{l-1}K_{m}},

then all infinite rates cancel out. Therefore

(12.10)

\left(\frac{x_{l}^{\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(t)+\sum_{m=0}^{l-1}K_{m}y_{l,m}^{\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(t)}{1+\sum_{m=0}^{l-1}K_{m}},y_{l,l}^{\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(t)\right)_{t>0}

is called the
effective process
on space-time scale
l
. Like in the simpler mean-field finite-systems scheme, the effective process allows us to analyse our system in path space.

An important difference between the finite-level mean-field system in (
12.6
)–(
12.7
) and the two-level three-colour mean-field system in Section (
11.1
) is that in the finite-level mean-field system also the highest level
k
has a drift towards the outside world. This outside world is the active
k+1
-block average, which does not evolve on time scale
N^{k}
. This drift allows the finite-level mean-field system to equilibrate to a non-trivial equilibrium. In the two-level mean-field system, the highest level, i.e., the active
2
-block average, does not feel a drift due to migration. Consequently, the
2
-block averages will eventually cluster.

\blacktriangleright
Scaling limit.

To state and prove the finite-level multi-scale limit, we need the following three limiting processes. Recall (
4.5
) and (
4.3
). For
0\leq l\leq k
, let

(12.11)

(z_{l,(\theta,(y_{m,l})_{m=0}^{k+1})}(t))_{t\geq 0})=(x_{l}(t),(y_{m,l}(t))_{m=0}^{k+1})_{t\geq 0})

be the process evolving according to

(12.12)

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle d}x_{l}(t)=E_{l}\Bigg[c_{l}[\theta-x_{l}(t)]\,{\rm d}t+\sqrt{\mathcal{F}^{(l)}g(x_{1}(t))}\,{\rm d}w(t)+K_{l}e_{l}\,[y_{l,l}(t)-x_{l}(t)]\,{\rm d}t\Bigg],

\displaystyle y_{m,l}(t)=x_{l}(t),\qquad\text{ for }0\leq m<l

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle d}y_{l,l}(t)=e_{l}\,[x_{l}(t)-y_{l,l}(t)]\,{\rm d}t,

\displaystyle y_{m,l}(t)=y_{m,l},\qquad\text{ for }l<m\leq k+1,

where
\theta\in[0,1]
and
y_{m,l}\in[0,1]
for
l<m\leq k+1
.

For
0\leq l\leq k
, let

(12.13)

(z_{l,(\theta,(y_{m,l})_{m=l+1}^{k+1})}^{\mathrm{aux}}(t))_{t\geq 0}=(x^{\mathrm{aux}}_{l}(t),(y^{\mathrm{aux}}_{m,l}(t))_{m=l+1}^{k+1})_{t\geq 0}

be the process evolving according to

(12.14)

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle d}x^{\mathrm{aux}}_{l}(t)=E_{l}\Bigg[c_{l}[\theta-x^{\mathrm{aux}}_{l}(t)]\,{\rm d}t+\sqrt{\mathcal{F}^{(l)}g(x^{\mathrm{aux}}_{1}(t))}\,{\rm d}w(t)+K_{l}e_{l}\,[y^{\mathrm{aux}}_{l,l}(t)-x^{\mathrm{aux}}_{l}(t)]\,{\rm d}t\Bigg],

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle d}y^{\mathrm{aux}}_{l,l}(t)=e_{l}\,[x^{\mathrm{aux}}_{l}(t)-y^{\mathrm{aux}}_{l,l}(t)]\,{\rm d}t,

\displaystyle y^{\mathrm{aux}}_{m,l}(t)=y_{m,l},\qquad\text{ for }l<m\leq k+1,

where
\theta\in[0,1]
and
y^{\mathrm{aux}}_{m,l}\in[0,1]
, for
l<m\leq k+1
.

For
0\leq l\leq k
, let

(12.15)

(z^{\mathrm{eff}}_{l,\theta}(t))_{t\geq 0}=\left(x^{\mathrm{eff}}_{l}(t),y^{\mathrm{eff}}_{l,l}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}

be the
effective process
evolving according to

(12.16)

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle d}x^{\mathrm{eff}}_{l}(t)=E_{l}\left[c_{l}\,[\theta-x^{\mathrm{eff}}_{l}(t)]\,{\rm d}t+\sqrt{(\mathcal{F}^{(l)}g)(x^{\mathrm{eff}}_{l}(t))}\,{\rm d}w(t)+K_{l}e_{l}\,[y^{\mathrm{eff}}_{l,l}(t)-x^{\mathrm{eff}}_{l}(t)]\,{\rm d}t\right],

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle d}y^{\mathrm{eff}}_{l,l}(t)=e_{l}\,[x^{\mathrm{eff}}_{l}(t)-y^{\mathrm{eff}}_{l,l}(t)]\,{\rm d}t.

Comparing (
12.12
) with (
12.16
), we see that the effective process looks at the non-trivial components of the full process. The auxiliary process in (
12.14
) looks at the active population, the effective seed-bank and the slow seed-banks.

To state and prove the finite-level multi-scale limit, we need the following list of ingredients:

1.

For
t>0
and for
0\leq l\leq k
, define the
l
-block estimators

(12.17)

\displaystyle\bar{\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{l}}\sum_{i\in B_{l}}\frac{x^{\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{i}(t)+\sum_{m=0}^{l-1}K_{m}y^{\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{i,0}(t)}{1+K_{0}},

\displaystyle\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{x}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{l}}\sum_{i\in B_{l}}x^{\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{i}(t),

\displaystyle\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{y_{m}}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{l}}\sum_{i\in B_{l}}y^{\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{i,m}(t),\qquad 0\leq m\leq k+1,

and put

(12.18)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(t),

\displaystyle=\left(\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{x}(t),\big(\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{y_{m}}(t)\big)_{m=0}^{k+1}\right),

\displaystyle??

\displaystyle=\left(\bar{\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(t),\left(\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{y_{l}}(t)\right)_{m=l}^{k+1}\right),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{\mathrm{eff},(l),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(t)

\displaystyle=\left(\bar{\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(t),\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{y_{l}}(t)\right).

We call
(\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(t))_{t>0}
the
l
-block estimator process
,
(\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{\mathrm{aux},(l),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(t))_{t>0}
the
auxiliary
l
-block estimator process
and
(\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{\mathrm{eff},(l),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(t))_{t>0}
the
effective
l
-block estimator process
.

2.

For
0\leq l\leq k
, define the
time scales

N^{l}
such that

(12.19)

\mathcal{L}[\bar{\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(N^{l}t_{l}-L(N)N^{l-1})-\bar{\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(N^{l}t_{l})]=\delta_{0}

for all
L(N)
such that
\lim_{N\to\infty}L(N)=infty
and
\lim_{N\to\infty}L(N)/N=0
, but not for
L(N)=N
. In words,
N
is the time scale on which
\bar{\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(\cdot)
starts evolving, i.e.,
\left(\bar{\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(N^{l}t_{l})\right)_{t_{l}>0},
is no longer a fixed process.

3.

The
invariant measure
for the evolution of the
l
-block average in (
12.12
), denoted by

(12.20)

\Gamma_{\theta,y_{l}}^{(l)},\qquad y_{l}=(y_{m,l})_{m=0}^{k+1}.

The
invariant measures
of the auxiliary
l
-block process in (
12.15
) and the effective
l
-block process in (
12.16
), denoted by, respectively,

(12.21)

\Gamma_{\theta,y_{l}}^{(l),\mathrm{aux}},\qquad y_{l}=(y_{m,l})_{m=l+1}^{k+1}

and

(12.22)

\Gamma_{\theta}^{(l),\mathrm{eff}}.

4.

For
0\leq l\leq k
, let
\mathcal{F}^{E_{l},c_{l},K_{l},e_{l}}
denote the
renormalisation transformation
acting on
\mathcal{G}
defined by

(12.23)

(\mathcal{F}^{E_{l},c_{l},K_{l},e_{l}}g)(\theta)=\int_{[0,1]^{2}}g(x)\,\Gamma_{\theta}^{(l)}({\rm d}x,({\rm d}y)_{m=0}^{k+1}),\qquad\theta\in[0,1],

and define the
iterates

\mathcal{F}^{(n)}
,
0\leq n\leq k
, of the renormalisation transformation as the compositions

(12.24)

\mathcal{F}^{(l)}=\mathcal{F}^{E_{n-1},c_{n-1},K_{n-1},e_{n-1}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathcal{F}^{E_{0},c_{0},K_{0},e_{0}},\qquad 0\leq l\leq k.

(Recall (
4.17
).)

5.

To give a detailed description of the multi-scale behaviour of the SSDE in (
2.20
), define the
interaction chain

(12.25)

(M^{k}_{-l})_{-l=-(k+1),-k,\ldots,0}

as the
time-inhomogeneous
Markov chain on
[0,1]\times[0,1]^{k+1}
with initial state

(12.26)

M^{k}_{-(k+1)}=(\vartheta_{k},\overbrace{\vartheta_{k},\cdots,\vartheta_{k}}^{k+1\text{ times}},\theta_{y,k+1})

that evolves according to the transition kernel
Q^{[l]}
from time
-(l+1)
to time
-l
given by

(12.27)

Q^{[l]}(u,{\rm d}v)=\Gamma_{u}^{(l)}({\rm d}v),\qquad 0\leq l\leq k.

(Recall (
4.18
).)

We are now ready to state the scaling limit for the evolution of the averages in (
10.7
).

Proposition 12.1
.

[Finite-level mean-field: finite-systems scheme]

Suppose that the initial state of the system in (
12.4
) is given by
\mu(0)=\mu^{\otimes[\Omega_{N}^{k+1}]}
for some
\mu\in\mathcal{P}([0,1]\times[0,1]^{k+2})
. Let
L(N)
be such that
\lim_{N\to\infty}L(N)=\infty
and
\lim_{N\to\infty}L(N)/N=0
, and for
t_{k},\ldots,t_{0}\in(0,\infty)
set
\bar{t}=L(N)N^{k}+\sum_{n=0}^{k}t_{n}N^{n}
.

(a)

For every
t_{k},\ldots,t_{0}\in(0,\infty)
,

(12.28)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathcal{L}\Bigg[\Bigg({\bf\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}\left(\bar{t}\right)\Bigg)_{l=k+1,k,\ldots,0}\Bigg]=\mathcal{L}\left[(M^{k}_{-l})_{-l=-(k+1),-k,\ldots,0}\right],

where
(M^{k}_{-l})_{-l=-(k+1),-k,\ldots,0}
is the interaction chain in (
12.25
) starting from

(12.29)

M^{k}_{-(k+1)}=(\vartheta_{k},\overbrace{\vartheta_{k},\cdots,\vartheta_{k}}^{k+1\text{ times}},\theta_{y,k+1}).

(b)

For all
0\leq l\leq k
,

(12.30)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathcal{L}\left[\left(\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(\bar{t}+t_{l}N^{l})\right)_{t_{l}>0}\right]=\mathcal{L}\left[\left(z_{l,M^{k}_{-(l+1)}}(t_{l})\right)_{t_{l}>0}\right],

\displaystyle\text{in the Meyer-Zheng topology},

where

(12.31)

(z_{l,M^{k}_{-(l+1)}}(t_{l}))_{t_{l}>0}

is the processes defined (
12.12
) with
\theta
,
(y_{m},l)_{m=l+1}^{k+1}
given by the corresponding components in
M^{k}_{-(l+1)}
and with initial measure

(12.32)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\left[z_{l,M^{k}_{-(l+1)}}(0)\right]=\Gamma^{(l)}_{M^{k}_{-(l+1)}}

\displaystyle\Gamma^{(l)}_{M^{k}_{-(l+1)}}=\int_{\mathfrak{s}^{k+1}}\cdots\int_{\mathfrak{s}^{k+1}}\int_{\mathfrak{s}^{k+1}}\Gamma^{(k)}_{M^{k}_{-(k+1)}}({\rm d}u_{k})\Gamma^{(k-1)}_{u_{k}}({\rm d}u_{k-1})\cdots\Gamma^{(l+1)}_{u_{2}}({\rm d}u_{l+2})\Gamma^{(l)}_{u_{l+1}}.

In Part (a), the limit does not depend on the choice of the times
t_{k},\ldots,t_{0}
, since we let time start from a time larger than
L(N)N^{k}
, so that in the limit as
N\to\infty
all the
l
-block averages with
l\leq k
are already in quasi-equilibrium. In Part (b), for
l<k
the center of the drift for the active population is
random
and is determined by the first component of the interaction chain. Also the states of the
m
-dormant populations with
l<m\leq k+1
are determined by the interaction chain.

Remark 12.2
.

In contrast to Propositions
10.2
–
11.1
, there are no assumptions on the seed-bank behaviour in Proposition
12.1
. This is because all the block-averages that we consider are in equilibrium at time
\bar{t}
. Consequently on space-time scales
l<m
the
m
-dormant
l
-block average will equal the state of the
m
-dormant
m
-block average at time
\bar{t}
. Therefore we say that the state of the
slow seed-banks
is determined by the space-time scaleon which this seed-bank is effective. Hence the state of the slow seed-banks is determined by the interaction chain.

\blacksquare

The proof of Proposition
12.1
will be given in Section
12.2
.

12.2
Proof of the mean-field finite-systems scheme: finite-level

We give a sketch of the proof Proposition
12.1
. The proof uses a similar scheme as the proof of Proposition
11.1
. We state the scheme and indicate at each step how it can be proved.

1.

Tightness of the auxiliary
l
-block estimator processes, for
0\leq l\leq k
,

(12.33)

\left(\left({\bf\Theta}^{\mathrm{aux},(l),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(N^{l}t_{l})\right)_{t_{l}>0}\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}.

Proof.

For each
0\leq l\leq k
we use the tightness criterion in
[
JM86
, Proposition 3.2.3.]
.
∎

2.

Stability property of the 2-block estimators, i.e., for
L(N)
such that
\lim_{N\to\infty}L(N)=\infty
and
\lim_{N\to\infty}L(N)/N=0
,

(12.34)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\sup_{0\leq t\leq L(N)}\left|\bar{\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(N^{l}t_{l})-\bar{\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(N^{l}t_{l}-N^{l-1}t)\right|=0\text{ in probability}

and, for all
l\leq m\leq k+1
,

(12.35)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\sup_{0\leq t\leq L(N)}\left|\Theta_{y_{m}}^{(l),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(N^{l}t_{l})-\Theta_{y_{m}}^{(l),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(N^{l}t_{l}-N^{l-1}t_{l})\right|=0\text{ in probability.}

Proof.

Use a similar computation as in the proof of Lemma
11.7
.
∎

3.

We analyse the behaviour of the slow seed-banks by proving the following lemma.

Lemma 12.3
.

[Slow seed-banks in the multi-level system]

Let
\Theta^{(l)}_{y_{m},i}
denote the
m
-dormant
l
-block average containing colony
i\in\Omega_{N}^{k+1}
. Then for all
i\in\Omega_{N}^{k+1}
,
m<k+1
,
l<m
and
t_{l}>0
,

(12.36)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\left[y_{i,m}^{\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(\bar{t}+N^{l}t_{l})-\Theta_{y_{m},i}^{(m),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(\bar{t}+N^{l}t_{l})\right]=0\quad a.s.

and hence

(12.37)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\left[\Theta_{y_{m},i}^{(l),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(\bar{t}+N^{l}t_{l})-\Theta_{y_{m},i}^{(m),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(\bar{t}+N^{l}t_{l})\right]=0\quad a.s.

Proof.

We can proceed as in the proof of Lemma
11.23
, after adapting the kernel
b^{[N^{2}]}(\cdot,\cdot)
to the kernel
b^{\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(\cdot,\cdot)
. Then we can use that, from each of the
m<k+1

m
-dormant populations, individuals wake up before time
\bar{t}
with probability
1
. For individuals starting from an
m
-dormant state, we define the coupling event

(12.38)

H_{t}^{m,\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}=\{RW^{\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}\text{ has migrated over distance $m$ at least once up to time }t\}.

The migration over distance
m
is needed because we need
m
-dormant individuals to be uniformly distributed over the
m
-block in order to almost surely equal the state of the
m
-block.
∎

4.

We prove the convergence of the single components. Recall that there are
N^{k+1-l}

l
-blocks in
\Omega_{N}^{k+1}
. Since tightness of components implies tightness of the process, step 1 implies that for
0\leq l\leq k
the full
l
-block processes

(12.39)

\left(\left({\bf\Theta}_{i}^{\mathrm{aux},(l),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(\bar{t}+N^{l}t_{l})\right)_{t_{l}>0,\,i\in[N^{k+1-l}]}\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}

are tight. From the tightness in steps 1 we can construct a subsequence
(N_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
along which, for all
0\leq l\leq k
,

(12.40)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{L}\left[\left({\bf\Theta}_{i}^{\mathrm{aux},(1),\Omega_{N_{n}}^{k+1}}(\bar{t}+N^{l}_{n}t_{l})\right)_{t_{l}>0,\,i\in[N_{n}^{k+1-l}]}\right]

exists. Note that
\bar{t}
depends on the subsequence. For example, along the subsequence
(N_{\tilde{n}})_{\tilde{n}\in\mathbb{N}}
,

(12.41)

\bar{t}=L(N)N^{k}+\sum_{n=0}^{k}t_{n}N_{\tilde{n}}^{n}.

We define the measure

(12.42)

\nu^{(0)}_{\bf\Theta}=\prod_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\Gamma^{(0)}_{\Theta_{i}}(\bar{t}),

where

(12.43)

\Theta_{i}\in\mathfrak{s}^{k+1}.

In this step we show that along the same subsequence the single components converge to the infinite system. We show that if

(12.44)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{L}[({\bf\Theta}^{\mathrm{aux},(1),\Omega_{N_{n}}^{k+1}}(\bar{t}))_{i\in[N_{n}^{k}]}]=P^{(1)},

then

(12.45)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{L}\Big[\Big(Z^{\Omega_{N_{n}}^{k+1}}(\bar{t}+t_{0})\Big)_{t_{0}\geq 0}\Big]=\mathcal{L}\left[(Z^{\nu^{(0)}(\bar{t})}(t_{0}))_{t_{0}\geq 0}\right],

where

(12.46)

\nu^{(0)}(\bar{t})=\int\nu^{(0)}_{u}P^{(1)}({\rm d}u).

Here,
(Z^{\nu^{(0)}(\bar{t})}(t_{0}))_{t_{0}\geq 0}
is the process starting from
\nu^{(0)}(\bar{t})
with components evolving according to (
11.18
), where
\theta
is now a random variable that inherits its law from

(12.47)

{}\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{L}[({\bf\Theta}^{\mathrm{aux},(1),\Omega_{N_{n}}^{k+1}}(\bar{t}))_{i\in[N_{n}^{k}]}]

and, similarly, the laws of
y_{m,0}
,
1\leq l\leq k+1
in the limiting process
(Z^{\nu^{(0)}(t_{2})}(t_{0}))_{t_{0}\geq 0}
are determined by

(12.48)

{}\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{L}[({\bf\Theta}^{\mathrm{aux},(1),\Omega_{N_{n}}^{k+1}}(\bar{t}))_{i\in[N_{n}^{k}]}].

Note that we choose the subsequence
(N_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in such a way that we know that the law
P^{(1)}
in (
12.44
) exists.

Proof.

Proceed as in the proof of Proposition
11.8
. Note that the assumptions on the seed-banks in Proposition
11.8
follow from the choice of the subsequence and Lemma
12.3
.
∎

5.

Using the limiting evolution of the single colonies obtained in step 4, we can identify the limiting
l
-block process along the same subsequence. For
1\leq l\leq k
, we show that if

(12.49)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{L}[({\bf\Theta}^{\mathrm{aux},(l+1),\Omega_{N_{n}}^{k+1}}(\bar{t}))_{i\in[N_{n}^{k}]}]=P^{(l+1)},

then

(12.50)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{L}\left[\left({\bf\Theta}^{\mathrm{aux},(l),\Omega_{N_{n}}^{k+1}}(\bar{t}+N^{l}_{n}t_{l})\right)_{t_{l}>0,\,i\in[N_{n}^{k+1-l}]}\right]=\mathcal{L}\left[(z_{l,{\bf\Theta}^{(l+1)}}^{\mathrm{aux}}(t))_{t\geq 0}\right],

where
{\bf\Theta}^{(l+1)}=({\bf\Theta}^{(l+1)}_{x},({\bf\Theta}_{y_{m},l}^{(l+1)})_{m=l+1}^{k+1})\in[0,1]\times[0,1]^{k+2-(l+1)}
,

(12.51)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\left[z_{l,{\bf\Theta}^{(l)}}^{\mathrm{aux}}(0)\right]

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{{\bf\Theta}^{(l+1)}}^{(l),\mathrm{aux}},

\displaystyle\Gamma_{{\bf\Theta}^{(l+1)}}^{(l),\mathrm{aux}}

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{k+2-(l+1)}}\Gamma_{u}^{(l),\mathrm{aux}}P^{(l+1)}({\rm d}u)

and
(z_{l,{\bf\Theta}^{(l+1)}}^{\mathrm{aux}}(t))_{t\geq 0}
is the process evolving according to (
12.14
) with
\theta,(y_{m,l})_{m=l}^{k+1}
replaced by the random variables
{\bf\Theta}^{(l+1)}_{x},({\bf\Theta}^{(l+1)}_{y_{m},l})_{m=l}^{k+1}
. Note that by the choice of the subsequence
(N_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
we know that for
1\leq l\leq k
the limiting laws in (
12.49
) exist.

Proof.

The proof goes by induction. Using the convergence of the single components, we can proceed as in the proof of Proposition
11.13
to prove the convergence of the
1
-blocks averages

(12.52)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{L}\left[\left({\bf\Theta}_{i}^{\mathrm{aux},(1),\Omega_{N_{n}}^{k+1}}(\bar{t}+N_{n}t_{1})\right)_{t_{1}>0,\,i\in[N_{n}^{k+1-l}]}\right].

Then, assuming that we have the convergence for all
0\leq l\leq L
, we get

(12.53)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{L}\left[\left({\bf\Theta}_{i}^{\mathrm{aux},(l),\Omega_{N_{n}}^{k+1}}(\bar{t}+N^{l}_{n}t_{l})\right)_{t_{l}>0,\,i\in[N_{n}^{k+1-l}]}\right],

and we prove the convergence of

(12.54)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{L}\left[\left({\bf\Theta}_{i}^{\mathrm{aux},(L+1),\Omega_{N_{n}}^{k+1}}(\bar{t}+N^{(L+1)}_{n}t_{(L+1)})\right)_{t_{(L+1)}>0,\,i\in[N_{n}^{k+1-(L+1)}]}\right].

This is done using a similar proof strategy as in the proof of Proposition
11.13
. In particular, we need to derive the
l
-level equivalent of Lemma
11.16
. Since this lemma is also key to proving convergence in the Meyer-Zheng topology, we state it explicitly below.
∎

Lemma 12.4
.

[
l
-block averages]

Define

(12.55)

\displaystyle\Delta^{(l),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{\Sigma}(N^{l-1}t_{l-1})

\displaystyle=\frac{\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{x}(N^{l-1}t_{l-1})+\sum_{m=0}^{l-2}K_{m}\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{y_{m}}(N^{l-1}t_{l-1})}{1+\sum_{m=0}^{l-2}K_{m}}-\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{y_{l-1}}(N^{l-1}t_{l-1})

and

(12.56)

R_{l}=\frac{1+\sum_{m=0}^{l-1}K_{m}}{1+\sum_{m=0}^{l-2}K_{m}}.

For
t\geq 0
set
\bar{\Theta}^{(0)}(t)=\Theta_{x}^{(0)}(t)=x_{0}(t)
. Then, for
1\leq l\leq k
,

(12.57)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\Delta^{(l),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{\Sigma}(N^{l-1}t_{l-1})\right|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\Delta^{(l),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{\Sigma}(0)\right)^{2}\right]}{\rm e}^{-e_{l}R_{l}t_{l-1}}

\displaystyle+\sqrt{\int_{0}^{t_{1}}{\rm d}s\,2e_{l}R_{l}{\rm e}^{-2e_{l}R_{l}(t_{1}-s)}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\bar{\Theta}^{(l-1),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(N^{l-1}s)-\Theta^{(l-1),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{x}(N^{l-1}s)\right|\right]}

\displaystyle+\sqrt{\frac{1}{N}\frac{1}{2e_{l}(1+\sum_{m=0}^{l-1}K_{m})}\left[\sum_{n=l+1}^{k}\frac{c_{n-1}}{N^{n-(l+1)}}+\sum_{m=l}^{k}\frac{K_{m}e_{m}}{N^{m-l}}+E_{l-1}||g||\right]}.

Proof.

Proceed like in the proof of Lemma
11.16
, using the SSDE in (
12.4
) instead of the SSDE in (
11.6
).
∎

We obtain the following useful corollary from Lemma
12.4
.

Corollary 12.5
.

For all
1\leq l\leq k
,
s>0
and
\tilde{l}\geq l
,

(12.58)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\bar{\Theta}^{(l-1),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(N^{\tilde{l}-1}s)-\Theta^{(l-1),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{x}(N^{\tilde{l}-1}s)\right|\right]=0.

Proof.

We proceed by induction. The result for
l=1
is trivial. Suppose that the result holds for
l=L
. Then for
l=L+1
we obtain

(12.59)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\bar{\Theta}^{(L),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(N^{L}s)-\Theta^{(L),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{x}(N^{L}s)\right|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\bar{\Theta}^{(L),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(N^{L}s)-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\bar{\Theta}^{(L-1),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{i}(N^{L}s)\right|\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\bar{\Theta}^{(L-1),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{i}(N^{L}s)-{\Theta}^{(L-1),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{x,i}(N^{L}s)\right|\right].

Note that the second term in the right-hand side of (
12.59
) tends to
0
as
N\to\infty
by the induction hypothesis. For the first term in the right-hand side of (
12.59
), note that

(12.60)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\bar{\Theta}^{(L),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(N^{L}s)-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\bar{\Theta}^{(L-1),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{i}(N^{L}s)\right|\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{\Theta^{(L),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{x}(N^{L}s)+\sum_{m=0}^{L-1}K_{m}\Theta^{(L),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{y_{m}}(N^{L}s)}{1+\sum_{m=0}^{L-1}K_{m}}\right.\right.

\displaystyle-\left.\left.\frac{\Theta^{(L),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{x}(N^{L}s)+\sum_{m=0}^{L-2}K_{m}\Theta^{(L),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{y_{m}}(N^{L}s)}{1+\sum_{m=0}^{L-2}K_{m}}\right|\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{K_{L-1}}{1+\sum_{m=0}^{L-1}K_{m}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\Theta^{(L),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{y_{L-1}}(N^{L}s)-\bar{\Theta}^{(L),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(N^{L}s)\right|\right].

Invoking Lemma
12.4
and using the induction hypothesis, we see that for
s>0
and
\tilde{l}\geq L
indeed

(12.61)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\bar{\Theta}^{(L),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(N^{\tilde{l}}s)-\Theta^{(L),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}_{x}(N^{\tilde{l}}s)\right|\right]=0.

∎

6.

Show that the convergence in step 4 and step 5 actually holds along each subsequence. Therefore we obtain the limiting evolution of the single colonies, the auxiliary
1
-block process and the effective
2
-block process. This follows from the fact that the auxiliary
k
-estimator process converges to the same limit along every subsequence. Consequently, the same holds for the auxiliary
k-1
-estimator process. In this way we can traverse back through the levels to obtain that all
l
-estimator process converges as
N\to\infty
.

Define, for
0\leq l\leq k
,

(12.62)

\mathfrak{s}^{k+1}_{l}=[0,1]\times[0,1]^{k+2-l}.

We obtain, for
0\leq l\leq k-1
,

(12.63)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathcal{L}[({\bf\Theta}^{\mathrm{aux},(l+1),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(\bar{t}))]]=\Gamma_{{\bf\Theta}^{(l+2)}}^{(l+1),\mathrm{aux}},

\displaystyle\Gamma_{{\bf\Theta}^{(l+2)}}^{(l+1),\mathrm{aux}}=\int_{\mathfrak{s}^{k+1}_{l+2}}\cdots\int_{\mathfrak{s}^{k+1}_{k}}\Gamma^{(k),\mathrm{aux}}_{\vartheta_{k}}({\rm d}u_{k})\cdots\Gamma^{(l+2),\mathrm{aux}}_{u_{l+3}}({\rm d}u_{l+2})\Gamma^{(l+1),\mathrm{aux}}_{u_{l+2}}.

Therefore by step 5

(12.64)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathcal{L}\left[\left({\bf\Theta}^{\mathrm{aux},(l),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(\bar{t}+N^{l}_{n}t_{l})\right)_{t_{l}>0,\,i\in[N^{k+1-l}]}\right]=\mathcal{L}\left[(z_{l,{\bf\Theta}^{(l+1)}}^{\mathrm{aux}}(t))_{t\geq 0}\right],

where
{\bf\Theta}^{(l+1)}=({\bf\Theta}^{(l+1)}_{x},({\bf\Theta}_{y_{m},l}^{(l+1)})_{m=l}^{k+1})\in\mathfrak{s}^{(k+1)}_{l}
are random variables with law

(12.65)

\mathcal{L}\left[{\bf\Theta}^{(l+1)}\right]=\Gamma_{{\bf\Theta}^{(l+2)}}^{(l+1),\mathrm{aux}}.

The initial state of the limiting process in (
12.64
) is given by

(12.66)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\left[z_{l,{\bf\Theta}^{(l)}}^{\mathrm{aux}}(0)\right]

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{{\bf\Theta}^{(l+1)}}^{(l),\mathrm{aux}},

\displaystyle\Gamma_{{\bf\Theta}^{(l+1)}}^{(l),\mathrm{aux}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathfrak{s}^{k+1}_{l+2}}\cdots\int_{\mathfrak{s}^{k+1}_{k}}\Gamma^{(k),\mathrm{aux}}_{\vartheta+k}({\rm d}u_{k})\cdots\Gamma^{(l+2),\mathrm{aux}}_{u_{l+2}}({\rm d}u_{l+1})\Gamma^{(l+1),\mathrm{aux}}_{u_{l+1}}

and
(z_{l,{\bf\Theta}^{(l+1)}}^{\mathrm{aux}}(t))_{t\geq 0}
is the process evolving according to (
12.14
) with
\theta,(y_{m,l})_{m=l+1}^{k+1}
replaced by the random variables
{\bf\Theta}^{(l+1)}_{x},({\bf\Theta}^{(l+1)}_{y_{m}})_{m=l+1}^{k+1}
. Recall that, by Lemma
12.3
, we have, for
l+1\leq m\leq k+1
,

(12.67)

{\bf\Theta}^{(l+1)}_{y_{m}}={\bf\Theta}^{(m)}_{y_{m}}.

7.

Use the Meyer-Zheng topology to obtain Proposition
12.1
(b).

Proof.

Note that Lemma
12.4
and Corollary
12.5
together imply

(12.68)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\bar{\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(N^{l}t_{l})-\Theta_{x}^{(l),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(N^{l}t_{l})\right|\right]

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\bar{\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(N^{l}t_{l})-\Theta_{y_{m}}^{(l),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}(N^{l}t_{l})\right|\right]

\displaystyle=0,\text{ for }0\leq m\leq l-1.

Combining the result obtained in step 6 with the proof strategy followed in Section
11.3.10
, we get the claim.
∎

8.

Finally, we prove Proposition
12.1
(a).

Proof.

Step 6 and step 7 yield the laws of the components
\mathcal{L}[M_{l}^{k}]
of the interaction chain
(M_{-l}^{k})_{-l=-(k+1)}^{0}
. Note that the state space
([0,1]\times[0,1]^{k+2})^{k+2}
is compact, and therefore the sequence of random variables

(12.69)

\left(\Bigg({\bf\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}\left(\bar{t}\right)\Bigg)_{l=k+1,k,\ldots,0}\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}

is tight. For any

(12.70)

\displaystyle f\colon\,([0,1]\times[0,1]^{k+2})^{n+2}\to\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle f(x)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}(x_{i}),

\displaystyle f_{i}\in\mathcal{C}_{b}([0,1]\to\mathbb{R}),

we can use conditioning on the previous block average to obtain

(12.71)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[f\left(\Bigg({\bf\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}^{k+1}}\left(\bar{t}\right)\Bigg)_{l=k+1,k,\ldots,0}\right)\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[f\left((M_{-l}^{k})_{-l=-(k+1)}^{0}\right)\right].

Using that the set of functions of the form (
12.70
) is separating, we obtain the claim.
∎

12.3
Proof: of the hierarchical multi-scale limit theorems.

In this section we prove Theorems
4.2
and
4.4
. We start by proving Theorem
4.4
. Theorem
4.2
will follow from Theorem
4.4
by projection onto the effective components.

Proof of Theorem
4.2

Proof.

Recall the estimators in (
4.11
). Like for the finite-level hierarchical mean-field system, we can define the auxiliary estimator process by

(12.72)

\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{(l),\mathrm{aux},\Omega_{N}}(t)=\big(\bar{\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}}(t),\big(\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{y_{m}}(t)\big)_{m=l}^{\infty}\big).

For
l,k\in\mathbb{N}
the processes
({\bf\Theta}^{(l),\mathrm{aux},\Omega_{N}}(\bar{t}+N^{k}t))_{t>0}
evolve according to (recall, (
4.54
))

(12.73)

\displaystyle{\rm d}\bar{\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}}(N^{k}t)

\displaystyle=E_{l}\sum_{n=l+1}^{\infty}\frac{c_{n-1}}{N^{n-1-k}}\left[\Theta_{x}^{(n),\Omega_{N}}(N^{k}t)-\Theta_{x}^{(l),\Omega_{N}}(N^{k}t)\right]\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle+E_{l}\sqrt{\frac{N^{k}}{N^{2l}}\sum_{\xi\in B_{l}}g\big(x_{\xi}(N^{k}t)\big)}\,{\rm d}w(t)

\displaystyle+E_{l}\sum_{m=l}^{\infty}\frac{K_{m}e_{m}}{N^{m-k}}\left[\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{y_{m}}(N^{k}t)-\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{x}(N^{k}t)\right]\,{\rm d}t,

\displaystyle{\rm d}\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{y_{m}}(N^{k}t)

\displaystyle=\frac{e_{m}}{N^{m-k}}\left[\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{x}(N^{k}t)-\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{y_{m}}(N^{k}t)\right]\,{\rm d}t,\qquad l\leq m\leq\infty.

Therefore, for
l>k
and all
\epsilon>0
,

(12.74)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigg[\sup_{0\leq t\leq L(N)}\Bigg|\bar{\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}}(\bar{t})-\bar{\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}}(\bar{t}+N^{k}t)\Bigg|>\epsilon\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\Bigg[\sup_{0\leq t\leq L(N)}E_{l}\Bigg|\int_{\bar{t}}^{\bar{t}+N^{k}t}{\rm d}r\,\sum_{n=l+1}^{\infty}\frac{c_{n-1}}{N^{n-1}}\left[\Theta_{x}^{(n),\Omega_{N}}(r)-\Theta_{x}^{(l),\Omega_{N}}(r)\right]

\displaystyle+\int_{\bar{t}}^{\bar{t}+N^{k}t}{\rm d}r\,\sum_{m=l}^{\infty}\frac{K_{m}e_{m}}{N^{m}}\left[\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{y_{m}}(r)-\Theta^{(l),\Omega_{N}}_{x}(r)\right]

\displaystyle+\int_{\bar{t}}^{\bar{t}+N^{k}t}{\rm d}w_{i}(r)\,\sqrt{\frac{1}{N^{2l}}\sum_{\xi\in B_{l}}g\big(x_{\xi}(r)\big)}\,\Bigg|>\epsilon\Bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\Bigg[\sup_{0\leq t\leq L(N)}\,E_{l}\Bigg|\int_{\bar{t}}^{\bar{t}+N^{k}t}{\rm d}w_{i}(r)\,\sqrt{\frac{1}{N^{2l}}\sum_{\xi\in B_{l}}g\big(x_{\xi}(r)\big)}\,\,\Bigg|

\displaystyle>\epsilon-t\left[\sum_{n=l+1}^{\infty}\frac{c_{n-1}}{N^{n-1-k}}+\sum_{m=l}^{\infty}\frac{K_{m}e_{m}}{N^{m-k}}\right]\Bigg].

Note that, since
l>k
,

(12.75)

\lim_{N\to\infty}t\left[\sum_{n=l+1}^{\infty}\frac{c_{n-1}}{N^{n-1-k}}+\sum_{m=l}^{\infty}\frac{K_{m}e_{m}}{N^{m-k}}\right]=0.

Hence, like in the proof of Lemma
11.7
, we can use an optional stopping argument to obtain

(12.76)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\sup_{0\leq t\leq L(N)}\Bigg|\bar{\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}}(\bar{t})-\bar{\Theta}^{(l),\Omega_{N}}(\bar{t}+N^{k}t)\Bigg|=0\qquad\text{ in probability}.

Using a similar computation as in (
12.74
), we can show

(12.77)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\sup_{0\leq t\leq L(N)}\Bigg|\bar{\Theta}_{y_{m}}^{(l),\Omega_{N}}(\bar{t})-{\Theta}_{y_{m}}^{(l),\Omega_{N}}(\bar{t}+N^{k}t)\Bigg|=0\qquad\text{ in probability}.

Hence we obtain that, on time scale
N^{k}
as
N\to\infty
, the process
({\bf\Theta}^{(l),\mathrm{aux},\Omega_{N}}(\bar{t}+N^{k}t))_{t>0}
does not evolve and therefore is still in its initial state
({\bf\Theta}^{(l),\mathrm{aux},\Omega_{N}}(\bar{t}))
.

Using that the
l
-auxiliary estimator processes do not move for
l>k
, they function like the “outside world” for the finite-level mean-field system in Section
12.1
. Therefore we can proceed as in the proof of Proposition
12.1

to prove the second and third line in (
4.29
) in Theorem
4.2
. The
l
-block estimator process
({\bf\Theta}^{(l),\mathrm{aux},\Omega_{N}}(\bar{t}+N^{l}t))_{t>0}
evolves according to (
12.73
) with
l=k
. Note that the extra interactions due to migration over larger blocks
l>k
and exchange with deeper seed-banks
m>k
in (
4.66
) are of order
\mathcal{O}(1/N)
. Therefore these terms vanish as
N\to\infty
, and we can just proceed as in the scheme of Section
12.2
, to obtain the second and third line in (
4.29
) in Theorem
4.2
. Using these results, we obtain that, for
l>k
,

(12.78)

{\bf\Theta}^{(l),\mathrm{aux},\Omega_{N}}(\bar{t})=\delta_{M^{k}_{-(k+1)}}.

∎

13
Proofs:
N\to\infty
, orbit of renormalisation transformation

In this section we analyse the orbit of the renormalisation transformation and show that it has the Fisher-Wright diffusion as a global attractor. In Section
13.1
we write down moment relations for the equilibrium defined in (
4.8
) for single colonies (Proposition
13.1
) and for block averages (Proposition
13.2
). In Section
13.2
we derive the iterates of these moment relations for single colonies (Proposition
13.3
) and for blocks (Proposition
13.4
). In Section
13.3
we prove clustering (Propositions
13.5
–
13.6
). In Section
13.4
we prove Theorems
5.1
and
5.3
, and work out the dichotomy of a finite seed-bank (
\rho<\infty
) versus infinite seed-bank (
\rho=\infty
).

13.1
Moment relations

We use Itô-calculus to compute the mixed moments. Recall
\theta_{x},(\theta_{y_{m}})
as defined in (
2.21
),
\vartheta_{k}
as defined in (
4.3
) and
\bar{\vartheta}^{(k)}
(
5.2
).
Also recall
E_{k}
as defined in (
4.5
). Abbreviate

(13.1)
