A_{0}^{n}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{E_{k}}{c_{k}}\frac{(E_{k}c_{k}+e_{k})}{(E_{k}c_{k}+e_{k})+E_{k}K_{k}e_{k}},\qquad n\in\mathbb{N},

and

(13.2)

B_{0}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{E_{0}^{2}}{(E_{0}c_{0}+e_{0})+E_{0}K_{0}e_{0}}.

In the following proposition, the first five equations are first and second moment relations, while the last equation is the definition of the renormalisation transformation. Later we will see that this set of equations can be iterated.

Proposition 13.1
.

[Moment relations: single colonies]

Let
\vartheta_{0}
be as defined in (
4.3
), and let
\Gamma_{(\vartheta_{0},y_{l})}^{(0)}=\Gamma_{(\vartheta_{0},y_{l})}^{g,c_{0},E_{0},K_{0},e_{0}}
be the equilibrium of (
4.8
) measure defined in (
4.14
) with
k=0
, with
g\in\mathcal{G}
,
c_{0}\in(0,\infty)
,
E_{0}\in[0,1]
and
K_{0},e_{0}\in(0,\infty)
. Then the following moment relations hold:

(13.3)

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}x_{0}\;\Gamma_{(\vartheta_{0},y_{l})}^{g,E_{0},c_{0},K_{0},e_{0}}({\rm d}z_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vartheta_{0},

(13.4)

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}y_{0,0}\;\Gamma_{(\vartheta_{0},y_{l})}^{g,E_{0},c_{0},K_{0},e_{0}}({\rm d}z_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vartheta_{0},

(13.5)

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}x_{0}y_{0,0}\;\Gamma_{(\vartheta_{0},y_{l})}^{g,E_{0},c_{0},K_{0},e_{0}}({\rm d}z_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}y_{0,0}^{2}\;\Gamma_{(\vartheta_{0},y_{l})}^{g,E_{0},c_{0},K_{0},e_{0}}({\rm d}z_{0}),

(13.6)

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}x_{0}^{2}\;\Gamma_{(\vartheta_{0},y_{l})}^{g,E_{0},c_{0},K_{0},e_{0}}({\rm d}z_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vartheta_{0}^{2}+A_{0}^{0}(\mathcal{F}g)(\vartheta_{0}),

(13.7)

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}y_{0,0}^{2}\;\Gamma_{(\vartheta_{0},y_{l})}^{g,E_{0},c_{0},K_{0},e_{0}}({\rm d}z_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vartheta_{0}^{2}+(A_{0}^{0}-B_{0})(\mathcal{F}g)(\vartheta_{0}),

(13.8)

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}g(x_{0})\;\Gamma_{(\vartheta_{0},y_{l})}^{g,E_{0},c_{0},K_{0},e_{0}}({\rm d}z_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathcal{F}g)(\vartheta_{0}).

Proof.

For ease of notation we write
x,y_{0}
instead of
x_{0},y_{0,0}
for the single colonies. We use Itô’s formula to compute the first and second moments, and invoke the equilibrium condition to get the above formulas, except for the last formula, which is the definition of
\mathcal{F}
in (
4.16
).

1.
We begin with the first moments of
x
and
y_{0}
. For
k=0
, (
4.8
) becomes

(13.9)

\displaystyle{\rm d}x(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E_{0}\left[c_{0}\big[\vartheta_{0}-x(t)\big]\,{\rm d}t+\sqrt{g(x(t))}\,{\rm d}w_{0}(t)+K_{0}e_{0}\,[y_{0}(t)-x(t)]\,{\rm d}t\right],

(13.10)

\displaystyle{\rm d}y_{0}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e_{0}\,[x(t)-y_{0}(t)]\,{\rm d}t,

\displaystyle{\rm d}y_{m}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

In equilibrium the distribution of
x(t)
is constant in time, and so
\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\mathbb{E}[x(t)]=0
, where
\mathbb{E}
denotes expectation w.r.t.
\Gamma_{\theta}^{g,c_{0},E_{0},K_{0},e_{0}}
. Integrating (
13.9
) and taking the expectation, we get

(13.11)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[x(t)-x(0)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E_{0}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}s\,c_{0}\big[\vartheta_{0}-x(s)\big]+K_{0}e_{0}\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}s\,[y_{0}(s)-x(s)]\right]\right],

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E_{0}\left\{\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}s\,\mathbb{E}\Big[c_{0}\big[\vartheta_{0}-x(s)\big]+K_{0}e_{0}\big[y_{0}(s)-x(s)\big]\Big]\right\},

where in the second equation we use Fubini. Turning back to differential notation, we see from
(
13.11
) that

(13.12)

\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\,\mathbb{E}[x(0)]=0=E_{0}\left\{\mathbb{E}\Big[c_{0}\big[\vartheta_{0}-x(t)\big]+K_{0}e_{0}\big[y_{0}(t)-x(t)\big]\Big]\right\},

and it follows that

(13.13)

\mathbb{E}\Big[c_{0}\big[\vartheta_{0}-x\big]+K_{0}e_{0}\big[y_{0}-x\big]\Big]=0.

In the same way it follows from (
13.10
) that

(13.14)

\mathbb{E}\Big[e_{0}\big[x-y_{0}\big]\Big]=0.

Therefore we obtain from (
13.13
)–(
13.14
) that

(13.15)

\mathbb{E}[x]=\mathbb{E}[y_{0}]=\vartheta_{0}.

2.

We next compute the second moments. By Itô’s formula,

(13.16)

\displaystyle{\rm d}(x(t))^{2}=2x(t)\,{\rm d}x(t)+({\rm d}x(t))^{2}

\displaystyle=2c_{0}x(t)E_{0}[\vartheta_{0}-x(t)]\,{\rm d}t+2x(t)E_{0}\sqrt{g(x(t))}\,{\rm d}w_{0}(t)

\displaystyle\qquad+E_{0}\big[2K_{0}e_{0}\,x(t)y_{0}(t)-2K_{0}e_{0}\,x^{2}_{t}\big]\,{\rm d}t+E_{0}^{2}g(x(t))\,{\rm d}t.

Taking expectations and using that we are in equilibrium, we get

(13.17)

\displaystyle 0=2c_{0}\vartheta_{0}^{2}-2c_{0}\mathbb{E}[x^{2}]+2K_{0}e_{0}\mathbb{E}[xy_{0}]-2K_{0}e_{0}\mathbb{E}[x^{2}]+E_{0}\mathbb{E}[g(x)].

Using
\mathbb{E}[g(x)]=(\mathcal{F}g)(\vartheta_{0})
, we find

(13.18)

\mathbb{E}[x^{2}]=\frac{c_{0}}{(c_{0}+K_{0}e_{0})}\vartheta_{0}^{2}+\frac{K_{0}e_{0}}{(c_{0}+K_{0}e_{0})}\mathbb{E}[xy_{0}]+\frac{E_{0}}{2(c_{0}+K_{0}e_{0})}(\mathcal{F}g)(\vartheta_{0}).

In the same way we find

(13.19)

\mathbb{E}[y_{0}^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[xy_{0}],

and for the mixed second moment

(13.20)

\mathbb{E}[xy_{0}]=\frac{E_{0}c_{0}}{(E_{0}c_{0}+e_{0})}\vartheta_{0}^{2}+\frac{e_{0}}{(E_{0}c_{0}+e_{0})}\mathbb{E}[x^{2}].

Substituting (
13.20
) into (
13.18
), we find
\mathbb{E}[x^{2}]
and hence also
\mathbb{E}[y_{0}^{2}]
and
\mathbb{E}[x_{0}y_{0}]
. This finishes the proof of Proposition
13.1
.
∎

Similar moment relations can be derived for the equilibrium measures of the block averages. Define

(13.21)

A_{m}^{n}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=m}^{n}\frac{E_{k}}{c_{k}}\frac{(E_{k}c_{k}+e_{k})}{(E_{k}c_{k}+e_{k})+E_{k}K_{k}e_{k}},\qquad m\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\,n\in\mathbb{N},

and

(13.22)

B_{m}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{E_{m}^{2}}{(E_{m}c_{m}+e_{m})+E_{m}K_{m}e_{m}},\qquad m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

Recall the definition of
\mathcal{F}^{(n)}
in (
4.17
).

Proposition 13.2
.

[Moment relations: blocks]

Let
\vartheta_{m}
be as defined in (
4.3
), and let
\Gamma_{(\vartheta_{m},y_{m})}^{(m)}=\Gamma_{(\vartheta_{m},y_{m})}^{\mathcal{F}^{(m)}g,c_{m},E_{m},K_{m},e_{m}}
be the equilibrium measure of (
4.8
) with
k=m
, with
g\in\mathcal{G}
,
c_{0}\in(0,\infty)
,
E_{0}\in[0,1]
and
K_{0},e_{0}\in(0,\infty)
. Then the following moment relations hold:

(13.23)

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}x_{m}\;\Gamma_{(\vartheta_{m},y_{m})}^{\mathcal{F}^{(m)}g,E_{m},c_{m},K_{m},e_{m}}({\rm d}z_{m})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vartheta_{m},

(13.24)

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}y_{m,m}\;\Gamma_{(\vartheta_{m},y_{m})}^{\mathcal{F}^{(m)}g,E_{m},c_{m},K_{m},e_{m}}({\rm d}z_{m})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vartheta_{m},

(13.25)

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}x_{m}y_{m,m}\;\Gamma_{(\vartheta_{m},y_{m})}^{\mathcal{F}^{(m)}g,E_{m},c_{m},K_{m},e_{m}}({\rm d}z_{m})

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}y_{m,m}^{2}\;\Gamma_{(\vartheta_{m},y_{m})}^{\mathcal{F}^{(m)}g,E_{m},c_{m},K_{m},e_{m}}({\rm d}z_{m}),

(13.26)

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}x_{m}^{2}\;\Gamma_{(\vartheta_{m},y_{m})}^{\mathcal{F}^{(m)}g,E_{m},c_{m},K_{m},e_{m}}({\rm d}z_{m})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vartheta_{m}^{2}+A_{m}^{m}(\mathcal{F}^{(m+1)}g)(\vartheta_{m}),

(13.27)

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}y_{m,m}^{2}\;\Gamma_{(\vartheta_{m},y_{m})}^{\mathcal{F}^{(m)}g,E_{m},c_{m},K_{m},e_{m}}({\rm d}z_{m})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vartheta_{m}^{2}+(A_{m}^{m}-B_{m})(\mathcal{F}^{(m+1)}g)(\vartheta_{m}),

(13.28)

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}(\mathcal{F}^{(m)}g)(x_{m})\;{\rm d}\Gamma_{(\vartheta_{m},y_{m})}^{\mathcal{F}^{(m)}g,E_{m},c_{m},K_{m},e_{m}}({\rm d}z_{m})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathcal{F}^{(m+1)}g)(\vartheta_{m}).

Proof.

The proof follows the same line of argument as the proof of Proposition
13.1
.
∎

13.2
Iterate moment relations

Recall the kernels defined in (
4.20
), the iterates of the kernels defined in (
5.1
) and
\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)}
.
Recall that
Q^{(n)}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},{\rm d}z_{0})
is the probability density to see the population of a single colony in state
z_{0}
given that the
(n+1)
-block averages equal
\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)}
.

Proposition 13.3
.

[Iterated moment relations: single components]

For
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

(13.29)

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}x_{0}\;Q^{(n)}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},{\rm d}z_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vartheta_{n},

(13.30)

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}y_{0,0}\;Q^{(n)}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},{\rm d}z_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vartheta_{n},

(13.31)

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}x_{0}^{2}\;Q^{(n)}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},{\rm d}z_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\vartheta_{n}^{2}+A_{0}^{n}\;(\mathcal{F}^{(n+1)}g)(\vartheta_{n}),

(13.32)

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}y_{0,0}^{2}\;Q^{(n)}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},{\rm d}z_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\vartheta_{n}^{2}+(A_{0}^{n}-B_{0})(\mathcal{F}^{(n+1)}g)(\vartheta_{n}),

(13.33)

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}x_{0}y_{0,0}\;Q^{(n)}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},{\rm d}z_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}y^{2}_{0,0}\;Q^{(n)}(\vartheta_{n},{\rm d}z_{0}),

(13.34)

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}g(x)\;Q^{(n)}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},{\rm d}z_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathcal{F}^{(n+1)}g)(\vartheta_{n}).

Proof.

We prove the claim for
x_{0}^{2}
only. The other relations follow in a similar way. The proof proceeds by induction. The result for
n=0
follows directly from Proposition
13.1
. Assume the result holds true for
n=n
, for
n=n+1
we write

(13.35)

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}x_{0}^{2}\;Q^{(n+1)}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n+1)},{\rm d}z_{0})

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}x_{0}^{2}\,(Q^{[n+1]}\circ Q^{(n)})(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n+1)},{\rm d}z_{0})

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}x_{0}^{2}\,Q^{[n+1]}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n+1)},{\rm d}z_{n+1})\,Q^{(n)}(z_{n+1},{\rm d}z_{0})

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}\left[\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}x_{0}^{2}\,Q^{(n)}(z_{n+1},{\rm d}z_{0})\right]\,Q^{[n+1]}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n+1)},{\rm d}z_{n+1})

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}\Big[x_{n+1}^{2}+A_{0}^{n}\;(\mathcal{F}^{(n+1)}g)(x_{n+1})\Big]\Gamma_{\bar{\vartheta}^{(n+1)}}({\rm d}z_{n+1})

\displaystyle\quad=\vartheta_{n+1}^{2}+A_{0}^{n}\;(\mathcal{F}^{(n+2)}g)(\vartheta_{n+1})+A_{n+1}^{n+1}\;(\mathcal{F}^{(n+2)}g)(\vartheta_{n+1})

\displaystyle\quad=\theta_{n+1}^{2}+A_{0}^{n+1}\;(\mathcal{F}^{(n+2)}g)(\vartheta_{n+1}).

The first and second equality use the definition in (
5.1
), the third equality uses Fubini, the fourth equality is the induction step, the fifth equality uses Proposition
13.2
, in particular, (
13.26
) and (
13.28
).
∎

Similar iterate moment relations hold for blocks. Define, for
m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
n\geq m
,

(13.36)

{}Q_{m}^{(n)}=Q^{[n]}\circ\cdots\circ Q^{[m]}.

Proposition 13.4
.

[Iterated moment relations: blocks of components]

For
n,m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
n\geq m
,

(13.37)

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}x_{m}\;Q_{m}^{(n)}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},{\rm d}z_{m})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vartheta_{n},

(13.38)

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}y_{m,m}\;Q_{m}^{(n)}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},{\rm d}z_{m})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vartheta_{n},

(13.39)

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}x_{m}^{2}\;Q_{m}^{(n)}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},{\rm d}z_{m})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\vartheta_{n}^{2}+A_{m}^{n}\;(\mathcal{F}^{(n+1)}g)(\vartheta_{n}),

(13.40)

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}y_{m,m}^{2}\;Q_{m}^{(n)}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},{\rm d}z_{m})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\vartheta_{n}^{2}+(A_{m}^{n}-B_{m})(\mathcal{F}^{(n+1)}g)(\vartheta_{n}),

(13.41)

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}x_{m}y_{m,m}\;Q_{m}^{(n)}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},{\rm d}z_{m})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}y^{2}_{m,m}\;Q_{m}^{(n)}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},{\rm d}z_{m}),

(13.42)

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}(\mathcal{F}^{(m)}g)(x_{m})\;Q_{m}^{(n)}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},{\rm d}z_{m})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathcal{F}^{(n+1)}g)(\vartheta_{n}).

Proof.

Follow a similar induction argument as in the proof of Proposition
13.3
.
∎

13.3
Clustering

To prove Theorem
5.1
, we proceed as in
[
BCGdH95
]
. The following clustering property holds for the kernels associated with single colonies.

Proposition 13.5
.

[Clustering: single colonies]

Assume

(13.43)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\vartheta_{n}=\theta.

Then

(13.44)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}Q^{(n)}\big(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},\{(x_{0},y_{(0,0)})=(0,0)\}\big)=1-\theta,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}Q^{(n)}\big(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},\{(x_{0},y_{(0,0)})=(1,1)\}\big)=\theta,

if and only if

(13.45)

\lim_{n\to\infty}A_{0}^{n}=\infty.

Consequently,

(13.46)

{}\lim_{n\to\infty}Q^{(n)}\big(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},\{(x_{0},y_{0,0})\}\notin\{(0,0),(1,1)\}\big)=0.

Proof.

The proof exploits the iterated moment relations. First assume (
13.45
)

1.

By Proposition
13.3

(13.47)

\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}x_{0}(1-x_{0})\,Q^{(n)}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},{\rm d}z_{0})=\vartheta_{n}(1-\vartheta_{n})-A_{0}^{n}(\mathcal{F}^{(n+1)}g)(\vartheta_{n}).

Because
x_{0}(1-x_{0})\geq 0
for
x\in[0,1]
, we have

(13.48)

\vartheta_{n}(1-\vartheta_{n})\geq A_{0}^{n}(\mathcal{F}^{(n+1)}g)(\vartheta_{n})\qquad\forall\,n\in\mathbb{N}.

Since
\lim_{n\to\infty}A_{0}^{n}=\infty
, it follows that
\lim_{n\to\infty}(\mathcal{F}^{(n+1)}g)(\vartheta_{n})=0
. On the other hand,

(13.49)

(\mathcal{F}^{(n+1)}g)(\vartheta_{n})=\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}g(x_{0})\,Q^{(n)}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},{\rm d}z_{0})

and, because
g(x)>0
for
x\in(0,1)
,
Q^{(n)}(\vartheta_{n},{\rm d}z_{0})
puts all its mass on
x_{0}=0
and
x_{0}=1
in the limit as
n\to\infty
. Let

(13.50)

{}Q^{(n)}\big(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},\{x_{0}=0\text{ or }x_{0}=1\}\big)=\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}\mathbf{1}_{\{x_{0}=0\text{ or }x_{0}=1\}}(z_{0})\,Q^{(n)}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},{\rm d}z_{0}),

then

(13.51)

{}\lim_{n\to\infty}Q^{(n)}\big(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},\{x_{0}=0\text{ or }x_{1}=1\}\big)=1.

The first moment of
x_{0}
converges to (recall (
4.4
))

(13.52)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}x_{0}\,Q^{(n)}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},{\rm d}z_{0})=\lim_{n\to\infty}\vartheta_{n}=\theta.

Hence

(13.53)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}Q^{(n)}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},\{x_{0}=0\})=1-\theta,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}Q^{(n)}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},\{x_{0}=1\})=\theta,

and

(13.54)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}x_{0}^{2}\,Q^{(n)}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},{\rm d}z_{0})

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}x_{0}^{2}\Big(\mathbf{1}_{\{1\}}(x_{0})+\mathbf{1}_{\{0\}}(x_{0})+\mathbf{1}_{\{(0,1)\}}(x_{0})\Big)\,Q^{(n)}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},{\rm d}z_{0})=\theta.

On the other hand,

(13.55)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}x_{0}^{2}\;Q^{(n)}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},{\rm d}z_{0})=\theta^{2}+\lim_{n\to\infty}A_{0}^{n}(\mathcal{F}^{(n+1)}g)(\theta),

and so, combining (
13.54
)–(
13.55
), we obtain

(13.56)

\lim_{n\to\infty}A_{0}^{n}(\mathcal{F}^{(n+1)}g)(\theta)=\theta(1-\theta).

2.

We know also that

(13.57)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}x_{0}y_{0,0}\,Q^{(n)}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},{\rm d}z_{0})

\displaystyle\qquad=\lim_{n\to\infty}\;\vartheta_{n}^{2}+\left(A_{0}^{n}-\frac{E_{0}^{2}}{E_{0}c_{0}+e_{0}+E_{0}K_{0}e_{0}}\right)(\mathcal{F}^{(n+1)}g)(\vartheta_{n})

\displaystyle\qquad=\theta^{2}+\theta(1-\theta)=\theta

and

(13.58)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}xy_{0}\,Q^{(n)}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},{\rm d}z_{0})

\displaystyle\quad=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}x_{0}y_{0,0}\,\Big(\mathbf{1}_{\{1\}}(x_{0})+\mathbf{1}_{\{0\}}(x_{0})+\mathbf{1}_{\{(0,1)\}}(x_{0})\Big)\,Q^{(n)}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},{\rm d}z_{0})

\displaystyle\quad=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}y_{0,0}\,\mathbf{1}_{\{1\}}(x_{0})\,Q^{(n)}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},{\rm d}z_{0}).

Therefore

(13.59)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}y_{0,0}\,\mathbf{1}_{\{1\}}(x_{0})\,Q^{(n)}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},{\rm d}z_{0})=\lim_{n\to\infty}\vartheta_{n}=\theta,

and hence

(13.60)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}(1-y_{(0,0)})\,\mathbf{1}_{\{1\}}(x_{0})\,Q^{(n)}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},{\rm d}z_{0})=\theta-\theta=0.

Since
1-y_{(0,0)}\geq 0
, we conclude that if
x_{0}=1
, then
Q^{(n)}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},{\rm d}z_{0})
puts all its mass on
y_{0,0}=1
in the limit as
n\to\infty
. Hence

(13.61)

{}\lim_{n\to\infty}Q^{(n)}\big(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},\{(x_{0},y_{0,0})=(1,1)\}\big)=\theta.

From Proposition
13.4
it also follows that

(13.62)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}(1-x_{0})(1-y_{0,0})\,Q^{(n)}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},{\rm d}z_{0})

\displaystyle\quad=1-\theta-\theta+\theta^{2}+\lim_{n\to\infty}\left(A_{0}^{n}-\frac{E_{0}^{2}}{E_{0}c_{0}+e_{0}+E_{0}K_{0}e_{0}}\right)(\mathcal{F}^{(n+1)}g)(\vartheta_{n})

\displaystyle\quad=1-\theta.

On the other hand,

(13.63)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}(1-x_{0})(1-y_{0,0})\,Q^{(n)}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},{\rm d}z_{0})

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}(1-x_{0})(1-y_{0,0})\,\Big(\mathbf{1}_{\{1\}}(x_{0})+\mathbf{1}_{\{0\}}(x_{0})+\mathbf{1}_{\{(0,1)\}}(x_{0})\Big)

\displaystyle\times Q^{(n)}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},{\rm d}z_{0})

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}(1-y_{0,0})\,\mathbf{1}_{\{0\}}(x_{0})\,Q^{(n)}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},{\rm d}z_{0})

\displaystyle=1-\theta.

Since
y\in[0,1]
and

(13.64)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}\mathbf{1}_{\{0\}}(x_{0})\,Q^{(n)}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},{\rm d}z_{0})=1-\theta,

it follows that

(13.65)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}y_{0,0}\mathbf{1}_{\{0\}}(x_{0})\,Q^{(n)}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},{\rm d}z_{0})=0.

This implies that if
x_{0}=0
, then
Q^{(n)}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},{\rm d}z_{0})
puts all its mass on
y_{0,0}=0
in the limit as
n\to\infty
. Hence

(13.66)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}Q^{(n)}\big(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},\{(x_{0},y_{0,0})=(0,0)\}\big)=1-\theta,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}Q^{(n)}\big(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},\{(x_{0},y_{0,0})=(1,1)\}\big)=\theta.

Now assume (
13.44
). Then (
13.54
) still holds. On the other hand, also (
13.55
) still holds by Proposition
13.3
. Therefore we obtain (
13.56
). On the other hand, by (
13.44
)

(13.67)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{F}^{(n+1)}g=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}g(x_{0})Q^{(n)}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},{\rm d}z_{0})=0.

Hence (
13.45
) holds.
∎

A similar clustering property holds for the kernels associated with blocks.

Proposition 13.6
.

[Clustering: blocks]

Assume

(13.68)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\vartheta_{n}=\theta.

Then

(13.69)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}Q_{m}^{(n)}\big(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},\{(x_{m},y_{(m,m)})=(0,0)\}\big)=1-\theta,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}Q_{m}^{(n)}\big(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},\{(x_{m},y_{(m,m)})=(1,1)\}\big)=\theta,

if and only if

(13.70)

\lim_{n\to\infty}A_{m}^{n}=\infty.

Consequently,

(13.71)

{}\lim_{n\to\infty}Q^{(n)}_{m}\big(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},\{(x_{m},y_{m,m})\}\notin\{(0,0),(1,1)\}\big)=0.

Proof.

We can proceed exactly as in the proof of Proposition
13.5
.
∎

Finally, we can prove Theorem
5.1
.

Proof.

Note that (
13.45
) implies (
13.70
). Recall that single colonies of deep seed-banks that have already interacted and reached their quasi-equilibrium equal the block average of the level on which they interact (see Theorem
4.4
). It follows that, for
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

(13.72)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}Q^{(n)}\big(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},{(x_{0},y_{0,m})=(1,1)}\big)=\theta,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}Q^{(n)}\big(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},{(x_{0},y_{0,m})=(0,0)}\big)=1-\theta.

Therefore, for
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

(13.73)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}Q^{(n)}\left(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},\bigcap_{m=0}^{N}\big\{(x_{0},y_{0,m})=(1,1)\text{ or }(x_{0},y_{0,m})=(0,0)\big\}\right)

\displaystyle=1-\lim_{n\to\infty}Q^{(n)}\left(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},\bigcup_{m=0}^{N}\big\{(x_{0},y_{0,m})\in[0,1]^{2}\backslash\{(0,0),(1,1)\}\big\}\right)

\displaystyle\geq 1-\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{m=0}^{N}Q^{(n)}\Big(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},\big\{(x_{0},y_{0,m})\in[0,1]^{2}\backslash\{(0,0),(1,1)\}\big\}\Big)=1-0=1.

Note that

(13.74)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}Q^{(n)}\left(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},\bigcap_{m=0}^{N}\big\{(x_{0},y_{0,m})=(1,1)\text{ or }(x_{0},y_{0,m})=(0,0)\big\}\right)

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}Q^{(n)}\Big(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},\big\{(x_{0},(y_{0,m})_{0\leq m\leq N})=(0,0^{N+1})

\displaystyle\text{ or }(x_{0},(y_{0,m})_{0\leq m\leq N})=(1,1^{N+1})\big\}\Big)=1.

On the other hand,

(13.75)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}Q^{(n)}\Big(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},\{(x_{0},(y_{0,m})_{0\leq m\leq N})=(1,1^{N+1})\}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}Q^{(n)}\big(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},\{(x_{0},y_{0,0})=(1,1)\}\big)=\theta

and

(13.76)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}Q^{(n)}\Big(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},\{(x_{0},(y_{0,m})_{0\leq m\leq N})=(0,0^{N+1})\}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}Q^{(n)}\big(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},\{(x_{0},y_{0,0})=(0,0)\}\big)=1-\theta.

Hence we conclude that

(13.77)

{}\lim_{n\to\infty}Q^{(n)}\Big(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},\{(x_{0},(y_{0,m})_{0\leq m\leq N})=(1,1^{N+1})\}\Big)=\theta

and

(13.78)

{}\lim_{n\to\infty}Q^{(n)}\Big(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},\{(x_{0},(y_{0,m})_{0\leq m\leq N})=(0,0^{N+1})\}\Big)=1-\theta.

We can do the same for all finite-dimensional distributions. Since
[0,1]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is compact, the process
z_{0}=(x_{0},(y_{0,m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}})
is tight. Therefore, by (
13.77
)–(
13.78
) we find for every converging subsequence

(13.79)

\lim_{k\to\infty}Q^{(n_{k})}\Big(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n_{k})},\cdot\Big)=(1-\theta)\,\delta_{(0,0^{\mathbb{N}_{0}})}+\theta\,\delta_{(1,1^{\mathbb{N}_{0}})}.

We conclude that

(13.80)

\lim_{n\to\infty}Q^{(n)}(\bar{\vartheta}^{(n)},{\rm d}z_{0})=(1-\theta)\,\delta_{(0,0^{\mathbb{N}_{0}})}+\theta\,\delta_{(1,1^{\mathbb{N}_{0}})},

which is the claim in (
5.4
).
∎

13.4
Dichotomy finite versus infinite seed-bank

In this section we prove Theorem
5.3
.

Proof.

We investigate for what choices of the sequences
\underline{c},\underline{K},\underline{e}
defined in (
2.5
) and (
2.10
) we meet the
clustering criterion

\lim_{n\to\infty}A_{n}\to\infty
in (
5.5
). Recall from (
4.5
) and (
5.3
) that

(13.81)

A_{n}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{E_{k}}{c_{k}}\frac{(E_{k}c_{k}+e_{k})}{(E_{k}c_{k}+e_{k})+E_{k}K_{k}e_{k}},\qquad E_{k}=\frac{1}{1+\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}K_{m}}.

We distinguish between three regimes as
k\to\infty
:

1.

E_{k}c_{k}+e_{k}\gg E_{k}K_{k}e_{k}
.

2.

E_{k}c_{k}+e_{k}\asymp E_{k}K_{k}e_{k}
.

3.

E_{k}c_{k}+e_{k}\ll E_{k}K_{k}e_{k}
.

These regimes correspond to the following scaling for
A_{n}
as
n\to\infty
:

1.

A_{n}\sim\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{E_{k}}{c_{k}}
.

2.

A_{n}\asymp\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{E_{k}}{c_{k}}
.

3.

A_{n}\sim\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{E_{k}c_{k}+e_{k}}{c_{k}K_{k}e_{k}}
.

Recall from that (
2.14
) that

(13.82)

\rho=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}K_{m}.

Different behaviour shows up for finite seed-bank (
\rho<\infty
) and infinite seed-bank (
\rho=\infty
).

(I)
\rho<\infty
.

Note that
k\mapsto E_{k}
is non-increasing and converges to
1/(1+\rho)>0
. Since
E_{k}\leq 1
, we have
\lim_{k\to\infty}E_{k}K_{k}=0
and hence we are in regime 1. Therefore

(13.83)

{}A_{n}\sim\frac{1}{2(1+\rho)}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{1}{c_{k}},\qquad n\to\infty,

and clustering occurs if and only if
\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\frac{1}{c_{k}}=\infty
, which is the same criterion as for the system without seed-bank.

(II)
\rho=\infty
.

We focus on the settings in (
3.10
) and (
3.11
), which fall in regimes 1 and 2.

Asymptotically polynomial.

Suppose that

(13.84)

{}K_{k}\sim Ak^{-\alpha},\quad k\to\infty,\,A\in(0,\infty),\,\alpha\in(-\infty,1).

Then

(13.85)

E_{k}\sim\frac{1-\alpha}{A}k^{-(1-\alpha)},\qquad E_{k}K_{k}\sim\frac{1-\alpha}{A}k^{-1},\qquad k\to\infty.

Hence we are in regime 1. Suppose that

(13.86)

{}c_{k}\sim Fk^{-\phi},\quad k\to\infty,\,F\in(0,\infty),\,\phi\in\mathbb{R}.

Then

(13.87)

A_{n}\sim\frac{1-\alpha}{2AF}\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}k^{-1+\alpha+\phi},\qquad n\to\infty,

and clustering occurs if and only if
-\phi\leq\alpha<1
. In this case

(13.88)

\displaystyle-\phi<\alpha\colon\quad A_{n}\sim\frac{1-\alpha}{2AF(\alpha+\phi)}\,n^{\alpha+\phi},

\displaystyle-\phi=\alpha\colon\quad A_{n}\sim\frac{1-\alpha}{2AF}\,\log n.

The case
\alpha=1
can be included. Then (
13.85
) becomes

(13.89)

E_{k}\sim\frac{1}{A\log k},\qquad E_{k}K_{k}\sim\frac{1}{k\log k},\qquad k\to\infty,

so that we are again in regime 1. Now (
13.87
) becomes

(13.90)

A_{n}\sim\tfrac{1}{2AF}\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\frac{k^{\phi}}{\log k},\qquad n\to\infty,

and clustering occurs if and only if
-\phi\leq 1
. In this case

(13.91)

\displaystyle-\phi<1\colon\quad A_{n}\sim\frac{1}{2AF(1+\phi)}\,\frac{n^{1+\phi}}{\log n},

\displaystyle-\phi=1\colon\quad A_{n}\sim\frac{1}{2AF}\,\log\log n.

Pure exponential.

Suppose that

(13.92)

{}K_{k}=K^{k},\quad k\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\,K\in(1,\infty).

Then

(13.93)

E_{k}=\frac{1}{1+\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}K^{m}}=\frac{1}{1+\frac{K^{k}-1}{K-1}}=\frac{K-1}{K^{k}+K-2}.

Suppose that

(13.94)

e_{k}=e^{k},\quad c_{k}=c^{k},\quad k\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\,e,c\in(0,\infty).

Then

(13.95)

{}E_{k}c_{k}+e_{k}=\frac{K-1}{K^{k}+K-2}c^{k}+e^{k},\qquad E_{k}K_{k}e_{k}=\frac{K-1}{K^{k}+K-2}K^{k}e^{k},

and so

(13.96)

E_{k}c_{k}+e_{k}\sim(K-1)\left(\frac{c}{K}\right)^{k}+e^{k},\qquad E_{k}K_{k}e_{k}\sim(K-1)e^{k},\qquad k\to\infty.

For
c\leq Ke
we are in regime 2, and hence

(13.97)

A_{n}\sim\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{K-1}{(Kc)^{k}}\,\frac{(K-1)(\frac{c}{K})^{k}+e^{k}}{(K-1)(\frac{c}{K})^{k}+Ke^{k}},\qquad n\to\infty,

which simplifies to

(13.98)

\displaystyle c<Ke\colon\quad A_{n}\sim\frac{1}{2K}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{K-1}{(Kc)^{k}},

\displaystyle c=Ke\colon\quad A_{n}\sim\frac{K-1}{2(2K-1)}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{K-1}{(Kc)^{k}}.

Clustering occurs if and only if
Kc\leq 1
. In this case

(13.99)

\displaystyle Kc<1\colon\quad\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{1}{(Kc)^{k}}\sim\frac{1}{1-Kc}\,(Kc)^{-(n-1)},

\displaystyle Kc=1\colon\quad\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{1}{(Kc)^{k}}\sim n.

For
c>Ke
, on the other hand, we are in regime 1, and hence

(13.100)

{}A_{n}\sim\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{K-1}{(Kc)^{k}},\qquad n\to\infty,

for which we can again use (
13.99
).

The case
K=1
can be included. Then
E_{k}=1/k
and (
13.96
) becomes

(13.101)

E_{k}c_{k}+e_{k}\sim\frac{1}{k}c^{k}+e^{k},\qquad E_{k}K_{k}e_{k}\sim\frac{1}{k}e^{k},\qquad k\to\infty,

we are again in regime 1. Hence

(13.102)

A_{n}\sim\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{1}{kc^{k}},\qquad n\to\infty,

and clustering occurs if and only if
c\leq 1
. In that case

(13.103)

\displaystyle c<1\colon\quad A_{n}\sim\frac{1}{2(1-c)}\,\frac{1}{n}\,c^{-(n-1)},

\displaystyle c=1\colon\quad A_{n}\sim\frac{1}{2}\,\log n.

∎

In the above computations, only regimes 1 and 2 arise. Regime 3 arises, for instance, when

(13.104)

\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{k}\log K_{k}=\infty,\qquad\lim_{k\to\infty}K_{k}e_{k}/c_{k}=\infty.

Indeed, the first condition implies that
E_{k}\sim 1/K_{k-1}
and
E_{k}K_{k}\gg 1
, while the second implies that
E_{k}K_{k}e_{k}\gg E_{k}c_{k}
. There are two subcases:

(13.105)

\displaystyle K_{k-1}e_{k}\ll c_{k}\colon\quad A_{n}\sim\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{1}{K_{k}K_{k-1}e_{k}},

\displaystyle K_{k-1}e_{k}\gg c_{k}\colon\quad A_{n}\sim\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{1}{K_{k}c_{k}}.

By picking, for instance,
e_{k}=1/K_{k}K_{k-1}
, we find that
A_{n}\sim\tfrac{1}{2}n
in the first subcase and
A_{n}\gg n
in the second subcase. By picking, alternatively,
c_{k}=1/K_{k}
, we find that
A_{n}\gg n
in the first subcase and
A_{n}\sim\tfrac{1}{2}n
in the second subcase.

Appendix A
Computation of scaling coefficients

In Appendices
A.1
–
A.2
we spell out a technical computation for the tail of the wake-up time defined in (
2.39
)–(
2.40
) in the two parameter regimes given by (
3.10
)–(
3.11
). In Appendix
A.3
we carry out a computation that is needed in Section
6.1
.

A.1
Regularly varying coefficients

In (
2.39
), note that for large
t
in the sum over
m
only small values of
e_{m}/N^{m}
contribute, which means large
m
. Hence, by the Euler-MacLaurin approximation formula, we have

(A.1)

P(\tau>t)=\frac{1}{\chi}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}K_{m}\frac{e_{m}}{N^{m}}\,{\rm e}^{-(e_{m}/N^{m})t}\sim\frac{1}{\chi}\int_{c}^{\infty}{\rm d}m\,K_{m}\frac{e_{m}}{N^{m}}\,{\rm e}^{-(e_{m}/N^{m})t},

where
c
is a constant that identifies from which value of
m
onward terms contribute significantly. Make the change of variable
s=\frac{e_{m}}{N^{m}}
. Since
e_{m}\sim Bm^{-\beta}
and
K_{m}\sim Am^{-\alpha}
as
m\to\infty
, we have

(A.2)

s\sim Bm^{-\beta}N^{-m}

and hence

(A.3)

\displaystyle\log s

\displaystyle\sim\log B-\beta\log m-m\log N,

\displaystyle\log\frac{1}{s}

\displaystyle=m\log N\left(-\frac{B}{m\log N}+\frac{\beta\log m}{m\log N}+1\right)=[1+o(1)]\,m\log N,

which gives

(A.4)

m=[1+o(1)]\,\frac{\log(\frac{1}{s})}{\log N}.

Thus,

(A.5)

\frac{1}{s}\frac{{\rm d}s}{{\rm d}m}=-\log N-\frac{\beta}{m}=-[1+o(1)]\,\log N,

which implies

(A.6)

\frac{{\rm d}s}{{\rm d}m}=-[1+o(1)]\,s\log N,

so that
s(m)
is asymptotically decreasing in
m
, and

(A.7)

\frac{{\rm d}m}{{\rm d}s}=-[1+o(1)]\,\left(s\log N\right)^{-1}.

Note that if
c\leq m<\infty
, then asymptotically
0<m^{-\beta}N^{-m}<c^{-\beta}N^{-c}=C_{2}
. Doing the substitution, we get

(A.8)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\tau>t)\sim\frac{1}{\chi}\int_{0}^{C_{2}}{\rm d}s\,K_{m}s\left(s\log N\right)^{-1}\,{\rm e}^{-st}\sim\frac{1}{\chi}\int_{0}^{C_{2}}{\rm d}s\,Am^{-\alpha}\left(\log N\right)^{-1}\,{\rm e}^{-st}

\displaystyle\sim\frac{1}{\chi}\int_{0}^{C_{2}}{\rm d}s\,A\left(\frac{\log(\frac{1}{s})}{\log N}\right)^{-\alpha}\left(\log N\right)^{-1}\,{\rm e}^{-st}\sim\frac{A}{\chi}\left(\frac{1}{\log N}\right)^{-\alpha+1}\int_{0}^{C_{2}}{\rm d}s\,\log\left(\tfrac{1}{s}\right)^{-\alpha}\,{\rm e}^{-st}.

Next, put
st=u
, so
s=\frac{u}{t}
and
\frac{{\rm d}s}{{\rm d}u}=\frac{1}{t}
and
0<u<tC_{2}
. Then

(A.9)

\mathbb{P}(\tau>t)\sim\frac{A}{\chi}\left(\frac{1}{\log N}\right)^{-\alpha+1}\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{C_{2}t}{\rm d}u\,\log\left(\tfrac{t}{u}\right)^{-\alpha}\,{\rm e}^{-u}.

We will show that

(A.10)

\frac{A}{\chi}\left(\frac{1}{\log N}\right)^{-\alpha+1}\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{C_{2}t}{\rm d}u\,\log\left(\tfrac{t}{u}\right)^{-\alpha}\,{\rm e}^{-u}\asymp\frac{A}{\chi}\left(\frac{1}{\log N}\right)^{-\alpha+1}\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{C_{2}t}{\rm d}u\,\log t^{-\alpha}\,{\rm e}^{-u}.

For
\alpha=0
this claim is immediate. For
\alpha\in(-\infty,0)
, note that
\log\left(\frac{t}{u}\right)^{-\alpha}
is a decreasing function on
(0,C_{2}t)
. Therefore we can reason as follows:

(A.11)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{C_{2}t}{\rm d}u\,\log\left(\tfrac{t}{u}\right)^{-\alpha}{\rm e}^{-u}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}u\,\log\left(\tfrac{t}{u}\right)^{-\alpha}{\rm e}^{-u}+\int_{1}^{C_{2}t}{\rm d}u\,\log\left(\tfrac{t}{u}\right)^{-\alpha}{\rm e}^{-u}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}u\,\log\left(\tfrac{t}{u}\right)^{-\alpha}+\int_{1}^{C_{2}t}{\rm d}u\,\log{t}^{-\alpha}{\rm e}^{-u}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{-\alpha}\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{t}}{\rm d}u\,\log\left(\tfrac{1}{u}\right)^{-\alpha}+2^{-\alpha}\int_{\frac{1}{t}}^{1}{\rm d}u\,\log t^{-\alpha}+\log t^{-\alpha}\left[1-{\rm e}^{-1}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2^{-\alpha}\Gamma(-\alpha+1)+2^{-\alpha}\log t^{-\alpha}\left[1-\tfrac{1}{t}\right]+\log t^{-\alpha}\left[1-{\rm e}^{-1}\right]

\displaystyle=\log t^{-\alpha}\left[2^{-\alpha}\tfrac{\Gamma(-\alpha+1)}{\log t^{-\alpha}}+2^{-\alpha}\left[1-\tfrac{1}{t}\right]+\left[1-{\rm e}^{-1}\right]\right]\asymp\log t^{-\alpha}.

For the lower bound, note that

(A.12)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{C_{2}t}{\rm d}u\,\log\left(\tfrac{t}{u}\right)^{-\alpha}{\rm e}^{-u}

\displaystyle\geq\log\left(t\right)^{-\alpha}\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}u\,{\rm e}^{-u}+\log(\tfrac{1}{C_{2}})^{-\alpha}\int_{1}^{C_{2}t}{\rm d}u\,{\rm e}^{-u}

\displaystyle=\log t^{-\alpha}\left[1-e^{-1}+\frac{\log(\tfrac{1}{C_{2}})^{-\alpha}}{\log t^{-\alpha}}\right]\asymp\log t^{-\alpha}.

For
\alpha\in(0,1]
, note that the function
\log\left(\frac{t}{u}\right)^{-\alpha}
is increasing in
u
. For the lower bound estimate

(A.13)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{C_{2}t}{\rm d}u\,\log\left(\tfrac{t}{u}\right)^{-\alpha}{\rm e}^{-u}

\displaystyle\geq\lim_{u\to 0}\log\left(\tfrac{t}{u}\right)^{-\alpha}[1-{\rm e}^{-1}]+\log t^{-\alpha}[{\rm e}^{-1}-e^{-C_{2}t}]

\displaystyle=\log t^{-\alpha}\left[0+{\rm e}^{-1}-{\rm e}^{-C_{2}t}\right]\asymp\log t^{-\alpha}.

For the upper bound estimate

(A.14)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{C_{2}t}{\rm d}u\,\log\left(\tfrac{t}{u}\right)^{-\alpha}{\rm e}^{-u}

\displaystyle\leq\log t^{-\alpha}[1-{\rm e}^{-1}]+\log\left(\frac{t}{\sqrt{C_{2}t}}\right)^{-\alpha}\int_{1}^{\sqrt{C_{2}t}}{\rm d}u\,e^{-u}+\log\left(\frac{1}{C_{2}}\right)^{-\alpha}\int_{\sqrt{C_{2}t}}^{C_{2}t}{\rm d}u\,{\rm e}^{-u}

\displaystyle=\log t^{-\alpha}[1-{\rm e}^{-1}]+(\tfrac{1}{2})^{-\alpha}\log\left(\frac{t}{C_{2}}\right)^{-\alpha}\left[{\rm e}^{-1}-{\rm e}^{-\sqrt{C_{2}t}}\right]+\log\left(\frac{1}{C_{2}}\right)^{-\alpha}\left[{\rm e}^{-\sqrt{C_{2}t}}-{\rm e}^{-C_{2}t}\right]

\displaystyle=\log t^{-\alpha}\Bigg[1-{\rm e}^{-1}+(\tfrac{1}{2})^{-\alpha}\left(\frac{\log t-\log C_{2}}{\log t}\right)^{-\alpha}\left[{\rm e}^{-1}-{\rm e}^{-\sqrt{C_{2}t}}\,\right]

\displaystyle+\log\left(\frac{1}{C_{2}}\right)^{-\alpha}\frac{\left[{\rm e}^{-\sqrt{C_{2}t}}-{\rm e}^{-C_{2}t}\right]}{\log t^{-\alpha}}\Bigg]\asymp\log t^{-\alpha}.

A.2
Pure exponential coefficients

In order to satisfy condition in (
2.12
), we must assume that
Ke<N
. Since
K\geq 1
for
\rho=\infty
, we also have
e<N
. We again use that for large
t
only large
m
contribute to the sum. Hence, again by the Euler-MacLaurin approximation formula, we have

(A.15)

P(\tau>t)=\frac{1}{\chi}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}K_{m}\frac{e_{m}}{N^{m}}\,{\rm e}^{-(e_{m}/N^{m})t}\sim\int_{M}^{\infty}{\rm d}m\,K_{m}\frac{e_{m}}{N^{m}}\,{\rm e}^{-(e_{m}/N^{m})t}.\\

Again we put
s=\frac{e^{m}}{N^{m}}
. Hence

(A.16)

\log s=m\log\left(\frac{e}{N}\right),\qquad m=\frac{\log s}{\log\frac{e}{N}},\qquad\frac{{\rm d}m}{{\rm d}s}=\frac{1}{s\log\frac{e}{N}},

and

(A.17)

K_{m}\sim K^{m}\sim{\rm e}^{\log s\frac{\log K}{\log\frac{e}{N}}}\sim s^{\frac{\log K}{\log\frac{e}{N}}}.

Since
s(m)
is decreasing in
m
, putting
C=(\frac{e}{N})^{M}
we obtain

(A.18)

\mathbb{P}(\tau>t)\sim\int_{0}^{C}{\rm d}s\,K_{m}\frac{s}{s\log\frac{e}{N}}\,{\rm e}^{-st}\sim\frac{1}{\log\frac{e}{N}}\int_{0}^{C}{\rm d}s\,s^{\frac{\log K}{\log\frac{e}{N}}}\,{\rm e}^{-st}.

Substitute
u=st
, i.e.,
\frac{u}{t}=s
, to get

(A.19)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\tau>t)\sim\frac{1}{\log\frac{e}{N}}\,t^{-1-{\frac{\log K}{\log\frac{e}{N}}}}\int_{0}^{Ct}{\rm d}u\,u^{\frac{\log K}{\log\frac{e}{N}}}\,{\rm e}^{-u}

\displaystyle\sim\frac{1}{\log\frac{e}{N}}\,t^{\frac{-\log(\frac{e}{N})-\log K}{\log\frac{e}{N}}}\int_{0}^{Ct}{\rm d}u\,u^{\frac{\log K}{\log\frac{e}{N}}}\,{\rm e}^{-u}\sim\frac{1}{\log\frac{e}{N}}\,t^{-\frac{\log(\frac{N}{Ke})}{\log\frac{N}{e}}}\int_{0}^{Ct}{\rm d}u\,u^{\frac{\log K}{\log\frac{e}{N}}}\,{\rm e}^{-u}.

The last integral converges because
\frac{\log K}{\log(\frac{e}{N})}>-1
, and

(A.20)

\int_{0}^{Ct}{\rm d}u\,u^{\frac{\log K}{\log\frac{e}{N}}}\,{\rm e}^{-u}\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}u\,u^{\frac{\log K}{\log\frac{e}{N}}}\,{\rm e}^{-u}=\Gamma\left(\frac{\log K}{\log(\frac{e}{N})}+1\right).

A.3
Slowly varying functions

Return to Section
6.1
. Note that
t(s)=\varphi(s)^{-1}s^{\gamma}
. Since this is the total time two lineages are active up to time
s
,
t(s)
must be smaller than
s
. By (
3.7
), we have

(A.21)

\displaystyle\frac{{\varphi}(t)}{{\varphi}(s)}=\exp\left[-\int_{t(s)}^{s}\frac{{\rm d}u}{u}\psi(u)\right].

Since we are interested in
s\to\infty
, we may assume that
s\gg 1
and
t(s)>1
, and estimate

(A.22)

\displaystyle\frac{{\varphi}(t)}{{\varphi}(s)}

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left[\int_{t(s)}^{s}\frac{{\rm d}u}{u}\frac{C}{\log u}\right]=\exp\big[C(\log\log s-\log\log t(s))\big]

\displaystyle=\exp\left[C\log\left(\frac{\log s}{\log\left(\varphi(s)^{-1}s^{\gamma}\right)}\right)\right]=\exp\left[-C\log\left(\frac{\gamma\log s-\log\varphi(s)}{\log s}\right)\right].

A similar lower bound holds with the sign reversed. Using that
\lim_{s\to\infty}\frac{\log\varphi(s)}{\log s}=0
, we get

(A.23)

\displaystyle\gamma^{C}\leq\liminf_{s\to\infty}\frac{{\varphi}(t)}{{\varphi}(s)}\leq\limsup_{s\to\infty}\frac{{\varphi}(t)}{{\varphi}(s)}\leq\gamma^{-C}.

Both bounds above are positive, so indeed
\frac{{\varphi}(t)}{{\varphi}(s)}\asymp 1
.

Appendix B
Meyer-Zheng topology

B.1
Basic facts about the Meyer-Zheng topology

In the Meyer-Zheng topology we assign to each real-valued Borel measurable function
(w(t))_{t\geq 0}
a probability law on
[0,\infty]\times\bar{\mathbb{R}}
that is called the pseudopath
\psi_{w}
. Note that the Borel-
\sigma
algebra on
[0,\infty]\times\bar{\mathbb{R}}
is generated by sets of the form
[a,b]\times B
for
B\in\mathcal{B}
and
0<a<b
. For
A=[a,b]\times B
, set

(B.1)

\psi_{w}(A)=\int 1_{A}(t,w(t)){\rm e}^{-t}{\rm d}t=\int_{a}^{b}1_{B}(w(t))e^{-t}{\rm d}t,

i.e.,
\psi_{w}
is the image measure of the mapping
t\to(t,w(t))
under the measure
\lambda({\rm d}t)={\rm e}^{-t}{\rm d}t
. The set of all pseudopaths is denoted by
\Psi
. Note that a pseudopath corresponding to
(w(t))_{t>0}
is simply its occupation measure. The following important facts are stated in
[
MZ84
]
:

•

If two paths
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
are the same Lebesgue a.e., then
\psi_{w_{1}}=\psi_{w_{2}}
.

•

Denote by
\mathbf{D}
the space of càdlàg paths on
[0,\infty]\times\mathbb{R}
. The mapping
\psi\colon\,\mathbf{D}\to\Psi,\,w\mapsto\psi_{w}
is one-to-one on
\mathbf{D}
and hence gives an embedding of
\mathbf{D}
into the compact space
\bar{\mathcal{P}}
, the space of probability measures on
[0,\infty]\times\bar{\mathbb{R}}
.

•

Note if
f
is a function on
[0,\infty]\times\mathbb{R}
and
w\in\mathbf{D}
, then

(B.2)

\psi_{w}(f)=\int_{0}^{\infty}f(t,w(t))\,{\rm e}^{-t}{\rm d}t.

Therefore we say that the sequence of pseudopaths induced by
(w_{n})\subset\mathbf{D}
converges to a pseudopath
w
if, for all continuous bounded function
f(t,w(t))
on
[0,\infty]\times\bar{\mathbb{R}}
,

(B.3)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}f(t,w_{n}(t))\,{\rm e}^{-t}{\rm d}t=\int_{0}^{\infty}f(t,w(t))\,{\rm e}^{-t}{\rm d}t.

Since a pseudopath is a measure, convergence of pseudopaths is convergence of measures.

•

\mathbf{D}
endowed with the pseudopath topology is
not
a Polish space.
\Psi
endowed with the pseudopath topology is a Polish space.

•

According to
[
MZ84
]
[Lemma 1], the pseudopath topology on
\Psi
is convergence in Lebesgue measure on
\mathbf{D}
.

B.2
Pseudopaths of stochastic processes on a general metric separable space

In
[
Kur91
]
the results of
[
MZ84
]
on state space
\mathbb{R}
are generalised to a general metric separable space
E
. Let
(Z(t))_{t>0}
be a stochastic process with state space
E
. Then we assign a random pseudopath to
(Z(t))
as follows: for
\omega\in\Omega
and
A=[a,b]\times B
,
0\leq a<b
and
B\in\mathcal{B}(E)
,

(B.4)

\psi_{(Z(t,\omega))_{t\geq 0}}(A)=\int_{a}^{b}1_{B}(Z(t,\omega))\,{\rm e}^{-t}{\rm d}t.

Hence
\psi_{(Z(t))_{t\geq 0}}
is a random variable with state space
\Psi
, i.e.,
\psi_{(Z(t))_{t\geq 0}}\in\mathcal{M}(\Psi)
, the set of probability measures on pseudopaths. Note that

(B.5)

\mathbb{E}\left[\psi_{(Z(t))_{t\geq 0}}f\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{\infty}f(t,Z(t,\omega))\,{\rm e}^{-t}{\rm d}t\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{\infty}f(t,Z(t))\,{\rm e}^{-t}{\rm d}t\right].

Weak convergence in the Meyer-Zheng topology.

Let
(Z_{n}(t))_{t\geq 0}
and
(Z(t))_{t\geq 0}
be stochastic processes with state-space
E
. We say that

(B.6)

\mathcal{L}\left[(Z_{n}(t))_{t\geq 0}\right]=\mathcal{L}\left[(Z(t))_{t\geq 0}\right]\text{ in the Meyer-Zheng topology}

if, for all
f\in\mathcal{C}_{b}(\Psi)
,

(B.7)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[f(\psi_{(Z^{n}(t))_{t\geq 0}})]=\mathbb{E}[f(\psi_{(Z(t))_{t\geq 0}})].

Let
\mathcal{C}_{m}([0,\infty)\times E)\subset\mathcal{C}_{b}([0,\infty)\times E)
be the set of functions of the form

(B.8)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{m}([0,\infty)\times E)

\displaystyle=\Big\{F\in\mathcal{C}_{b}([0,\infty)\times E):F(t,x(t))=\prod_{i=1}^{m}\int_{0}^{T_{i}}f_{i}(t,x(t)){\rm d}t,

\displaystyle m\in\mathbb{N},\,\forall 1\leq i\leq m,\,f_{i}\in\mathcal{C}_{b}([0,\infty)\times E),\,T_{i}>0\Big\}.

Note that
\mathcal{C}_{m}
is an algebra. Let
M_{E}[0,\infty)
be the space of measurable processes from
[0,\infty)
to
\mathbb{E}
, so
\mathbf{D}\subset M_{E}[0,\infty)
. Note that
\mathcal{C}_{m}
separates points in
M_{E}[0,\infty)
. By
[
Kur91
]
[Proposition 4.5], the set
\mathcal{C}_{m}
is separating in the set of measures on
M_{E}[0,\infty)
. This means that if two stochastic processes
(Z_{1}(t))_{t>0}
and
(Z_{2}(t))_{t\geq 0}
satisfy

(B.9)

\mathbb{E}[F(Z_{1})]=\mathbb{E}[F(Z_{2})]\qquad\forall F\in\mathcal{C}_{m},

then
\mathcal{L}[Z_{1}]=\mathcal{L}[Z_{2}]
.

Define

(B.10)

F(\psi)=\int{\rm d}\psi\prod_{i=1}^{m}\int_{0}^{T_{i}}f_{i}(t,x(t))\,{\rm d}t.

Recall that a pseudopath
\psi
is associated with a path
w\in M_{E}[0,\infty)
. Hence this becomes

(B.11)

F(\psi_{w})=\prod_{i=1}^{m}\int_{0}^{T_{i}}f_{i}(t,w(t))\,{\rm d}t.

Since each pseudopath
\psi\in\Psi
is associated with a path in
M_{E}[0\infty)
,
\mathcal{C}_{m}
also separates points on
\Psi
and hence
\mathcal{C}_{m}
separates measures on
\Psi
. This implies that if

(B.12)

\mathbb{E}[F(\psi_{Z_{1}})]=\mathbb{E}[F(\psi_{Z_{2}})]\qquad\forall F\in\mathcal{C}_{m},

then
\mathcal{L}[\psi_{Z_{1}}]=\mathcal{L}[\psi_{Z_{2}}]
. Therefore
\mathcal{L}[Z_{1}]=\mathcal{L}[Z_{2}]
if and only if
\mathcal{L}[\psi_{Z_{1}}]=\mathcal{L}[\psi_{Z_{2}}]
.

The Meyer-Zheng topology is a weaker than the Skohorod topology.

Lemma B.1
.

Let
(Z_{n}(t))_{t\geq 0}

n\in\mathbb{N}
and
(Z(t))_{t\geq 0}
be stochastic processes with Polish state-space
E
. If

(B.13)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{L}\left[(Z_{n}(t))_{t\geq 0}\right]=\mathcal{L}\left[(Z(t))_{t\geq 0}\right]\text{ in the Skohorod topology},

then

(B.14)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{L}\left[(Z_{n}(t))_{t\geq 0}\right]=\mathcal{L}\left[(Z(t))_{t\geq 0}\right]\text{ in the Meyer-Zheng topology}.

Proof.

Since we do not know whether
\Psi
is compact, the set
\mathcal{C}_{m}
does not have to be convergence determining. Therefore, via Skorohod’s theorem we construct the process
\tilde{Z}^{n}
and
\tilde{Z}
on one probability space, such that
\mathcal{L}[\tilde{Z}^{n}]=\mathcal{L}[{Z}^{n}]
and
\mathcal{L}[\tilde{Z}]=\mathcal{L}[{Z}]
, and

(B.15)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\tilde{Z}_{n}=\tilde{Z}\qquad a.s.

This implies

(B.16)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\psi_{\tilde{Z}^{n}}=\psi_{\tilde{Z}}\qquad a.s.

Consequently, for all
f\in\mathcal{C}_{b}(\Psi)
,

(B.17)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[f(\psi_{\tilde{Z}^{n}})]=\mathbb{E}[f(\psi_{\tilde{Z}})].

Note that, since
\mathcal{L}[\tilde{Z}^{n}]=\mathcal{L}[{Z}^{n}]
and
\mathcal{L}[\tilde{Z}]=\mathcal{L}[{Z}]
, we can use (
B.9
) and (
B.12
) to see that the latter implies
\mathcal{L}[\psi_{Z^{n}}]=\mathcal{L}[\psi_{\tilde{Z}^{n}}]
and
\mathcal{L}[\psi_{Z}]=\mathcal{L}[\psi_{\tilde{Z}}]
. Hence (
B.17
) indeed implies that

(B.18)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{L}[\psi_{Z^{n}}]=\mathcal{L}[\psi_{Z}].

∎

Convergence in probability in the Meyer-Zheng topology.

Let
(S,d)
be a metric space,
\mathcal{B}(S)
denote the Borel-
\sigma
algebra on
S
, and
\mathcal{P}(S)
the set of probability measures on
\mathcal{B}(S)
. Recall (see e.g.
[
EK86
, Chapter 3]
) that the Prohorov metric
d_{P}
on the space
\mathcal{P}(S)
is given by

(B.19)

d_{P}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})=\inf\left\{\epsilon>0\colon\,\mathbb{P}(A)\leq\mathbb{Q}(A^{\epsilon})+\epsilon\,\,\forall\,A\in\mathcal{C}\right\},

where
\mathcal{C}\subset\mathcal{B}(S)
is the set of all closed sets in
S
and
A^{\epsilon}=\{x\in S\colon\,\inf_{y\in A}d(x,y)<\epsilon\}
.

Theorem B.2
.

[
EK86
, Theorem 3.1.2]

Let
(S,d)
be separable and let
\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{P}(S)
. Define
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})
to be the set of all
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(S\times S)
with marginals
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
, i.e.,
\mu(A\times S)=\mathbb{P}(A)
and
\mu(S\times A)=\mathbb{Q}(A)
for all
A\in\mathcal{B}(S)
. Then

(B.20)

d_{P}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})=\inf_{\mu\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})}\inf\{\epsilon>0\colon\,\mu(\{(x,y)\colon\,d(x,y)\geq\epsilon\})\leq\epsilon\}.

Moreover,
[
EK86
, Theorem 3.3.1]
states that convergence of measures in the Prohorov distance,
\lim_{n\to\infty}d_{P}(\mathbb{P}_{n},\mathbb{P})=0
, is the same as weak convergence
\mathbb{P}_{n}\Rightarrow\mathbb{P}
. Hence, since convergence of pseudopaths is weak convergence, we can endow the space of pseudopaths
\Psi
with the metric
d_{P}
.

Let
(\Psi,d_{P})
be the pseudopath space metrized by the Prohorov distance. Let
(Z^{n}(t))_{t>0}
,
(Z(t))_{t>0}
be stochastic processes on the state space
E
, where
E
is endowed with the metric
d(\cdot,\cdot)
. Note that convergence in probability in the Meyer-Zheng topology means that

(B.21)

\forall\,\delta>0\colon\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[d_{P}\left(\psi_{Z^{n}},\psi_{Z}\right)>\delta\right]=0.

Tightness.

Define the
conditional variation
for an
\mathbb{R}
-valued process
(U(t))_{t\geq 0}
with natural filtration
(\mathcal{F}(t))_{t\geq 0}
as follows. For a subdivision
\tau\colon\,0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{n}=\infty
, set

(B.22)

V_{\tau}(U)=\sum\limits_{0\leq i<n}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|\mathbb{E}[U(t_{i+1})-U(t_{i})\mid F(t_{i})]\big|\Big]

(with
U(\infty)=0)
and

(B.23)

V(U)=\sup\limits_{\tau}V_{\tau}(U).

If
V(U)<\infty
, then
U
is called a
quasi-martingale
. Note that we can always stop the process at some finite time and work with compact time intervals.

Lemma B.3
.

[Tightness in the Meyer-Zheng topology]

If
(P_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence of probability laws on
D([0,T],\mathbb{R})
such that under
P_{n}
the coordinate process
(U(t))_{t\geq 0}
is a quasi-martingale with a conditional variation
V_{n}(U)
that is bounded uniformly in
n
, then there exists a subsequence
(P_{n_{k}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
that converges weakly in the Meyer-Zheng topology on
D([0,T],\mathbb{R})
to a probability law
P
, and
(U(t))_{t\geq 0}
is a quasi-martingale under
P
.

(See
[
MZ84
, Theorem 7]
for the identification of the limiting semi-martingale.)

B.3
Proof of key lemmas

\bullet
Proof of Lemma
8.19
.

Proof.

Fix
\delta>0
. Then

(B.24)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[d_{P}(\psi_{Z_{n}},\psi_{Z})>\delta\right]

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[\inf_{\mu\in\mathcal{M}(\psi_{Z_{n}},\psi_{Z})}\inf\{\epsilon>0\colon\,\mu(\{(x,y)\colon\,d(x,y)\geq\epsilon\})\leq\epsilon\}>\delta\right]

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[\,\forall\mu\in\mathcal{M}(\psi_{Z_{n}},\psi_{Z}),\,\inf\{\epsilon>0\colon\,\mu(\{(x,y)\colon\,d(x,y)\geq\epsilon\})\leq\epsilon\}>\delta\right]

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[\,\forall\mu\in\mathcal{M}(\psi_{Z_{n}},\psi_{Z}),\,\mu(\{(x,y)\colon\,d(x,y)\geq\delta\})>\delta\right].

Let
\mu_{n}\in\mathcal{P}(([0,\infty]\times E)^{2})
be the measure defined by

(B.25)

\mu_{n}(A)=\int_{0}^{\infty}1_{A}\left((t,Z_{n}(t)),(t,Z(t))\right)\,{\rm e}^{-t}{\rm d}t,\qquad A\in\mathcal{B}(([0,\infty]\times E)^{2}),

such that, for
B\in\mathcal{B}([0,\infty]\times E)
,

(B.26)

\mu_{n}(B\times S)=\int_{0}^{\infty}1_{B}(t,Z_{n}(t))1_{S}((t,Z(t))\,{\rm e}^{-t}{\rm d}t=\psi_{Z^{n}}(B),

and similarly
\mu_{n}(S\times B)=\psi_{Z}(B)
. Hence
\mu_{n}\in\mathcal{M}(\psi_{Z_{n}},\psi_{Z})
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and we obtain from (
B.24
) that

(B.27)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[d_{P}(\psi_{Z_{n}},\psi_{Z})>\delta\right]

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[\mu_{n}(\{(x,y):d(x,y)\geq\delta\})>\delta\right]

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[\int_{0}^{\infty}1_{\{(x,y):d(x,y)\geq\delta\}}\left((t,Z_{n}(t)),(t,Z(t))\right){\rm e}^{-t}{\rm d}t>\delta\right]

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[\int_{0}^{\infty}1_{\{d(Z_{n}(t),Z(t))\geq\delta\}}\,{\rm e}^{-t}{\rm d}t>\delta\right]

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\delta}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{\infty}d(Z_{n}(t),Z(t))\,{\rm e}^{-t}{\rm d}t\right]

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[d(Z_{n}(t),Z(t))\right]\,{\rm e}^{-t}{\rm d}t=0.

∎

\bullet
Proof of Lemma
8.20
.

Proof.

We have to show that

(B.28)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{L}\left[\psi_{(X_{n},Y_{n})}\right]=\mathcal{L}\left[\psi_{(X,c)}\right].

Hence we must show that, for all
f\in\mathcal{C}_{b}(\Psi)
,

(B.29)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[f(\psi_{(X_{n},Y_{n})})]=\mathbb{E}[f(\psi_{(X,c)})].

We can write

(B.30)

\displaystyle|\mathbb{E}[f(\psi_{(X_{n},Y_{n})})-f(\psi_{(X,c)})]|\leq|\mathbb{E}[f(\psi_{(X_{n},Y_{n})})-f(\psi_{(X_{n},c)})]|+|\mathbb{E}[f(\psi_{(X_{n},c)})-f(\psi_{(X,c)})]|.

Since
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[d(Y_{n}(t),c)]=0
implies
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[d((X_{n}(t),Y_{n}(t)),(X_{n}(t),c))]=0
, it follows from Lemma
8.19
that, for all
\delta>0
,

(B.31)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[d_{P}\left(\psi_{(X_{n},Y_{n})},\psi_{(X_{n},c)}\right)\right]=0.

Hence, for all
f\in\mathcal{C}_{b}(\Psi)
,

(B.32)

\lim_{n\to\infty}|\mathbb{E}[f(\psi_{(X_{n},Y_{n})})-f(\psi_{(X_{n},c)})]|=0.

To see that the second term in the right-hand side of (
B.30
) tends to zero, note that we can define

(B.33)

\tilde{f}(\psi_{x})=f(\psi_{x,c}).

We show that
\tilde{f}
is continuous.

Recall that convergence in the Meyer-Zheng topology is simply convergence in Lebesgue measure. Hence, for two paths
(t,x_{n}(t))
and
(t,x(t))\in M_{E}[0\infty)
we have
\psi_{x_{n}}\to\psi_{x}
if and only if, for all
\delta>0
,

(B.34)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}1_{\{d(x_{n}(t),x(t))>\delta\}}\,{\rm e}^{-t}{\rm d}t=0.

Therefore
\psi_{x_{n}}\to\psi_{x}
implies that, for all
\delta>0
,

(B.35)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}1_{\{d((x_{n}(t),c),(x(t),c))>\delta\}}\,{\rm e}^{-t}{\rm d}t=0,

and hence
\psi_{x_{n},c}\to\psi_{x,c}
. Therefore

(B.36)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\tilde{f}(\psi_{x_{n}})=\lim_{n\to\infty}f(\psi_{(x_{n},c)})=f(\psi_{(x,c)})=\tilde{f}(\psi_{x})

and we conclude that
f\in\mathcal{C}_{b}(\Psi)
. Since
\mathcal{L}[X_{n}]=\mathcal{L}[X]
in the Meyer-Zheng topology, we have, for all
f\in\mathcal{C}_{b}(\Psi)
,

(B.37)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}|\mathbb{E}[f(\psi_{(X_{n},c)})-f(\psi_{(X,c)})]|=\lim_{n\to\infty}|\mathbb{E}[\tilde{f}(\psi_{(X_{n})})-\tilde{f}(\psi_{(X)})]|=0.

Therefore also the second term on the right-hand side of (
B.30
) tends to
0
.
∎

\bullet
Proof of Lemma
8.21
.

Proof.

For part (a), suppose that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\psi_{x_{n}}=\psi_{x}
. Then, since convergence in pseudopath space is convergence in measure, we have, for all
\delta>0
,

(B.38)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}1_{\{d(x_{n}(t),x(t))>\delta\}}\,{\rm e}^{-t}{\rm d}t=0.

Since
f
is a continuous function, this implies that, for all
\epsilon>0
,

(B.39)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}1_{\{d(f(x_{n}(t)),f(x(t)))>\epsilon\}}\,{\rm e}^{-t}{\rm d}t=0.

and we conclude that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\psi_{f(x_{n})}=\psi_{f(x)}
. Hence
h
is indeed continuous.

For part (b), recall that

(B.40)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{L}[X_{n}]=\mathcal{L}[X]\text{ in the Meyer-Zheng topology}

implies that, for all
g\in\mathcal{C}_{b}(\Psi)
,

(B.41)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[g(\psi_{X_{n}})]=\mathbb{E}[g(\psi_{X})].

Since
h\colon\,\Psi\to\Psi
is continuous, we have for all
g\in\mathcal{C}_{b}(\Psi)
that
g\circ h\in\mathcal{C}_{b}(\Psi)
. Hence

(B.42)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[g(\psi_{f(X_{n})}]=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[g\circ h(\psi_{X_{n}})]=\mathbb{E}[g\circ h(\psi_{X})]=\mathbb{E}[g(\psi_{f(X)})].

We conclude that

(B.43)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{L}[f(X_{n})]=\mathcal{L}[f(X)]\text{ in the Meyer-Zheng topology.}

∎

\bullet
Proof of Lemma
10.17
.

Supposethat
\lim_{n\to\infty}\psi_{(x_{n},y_{n})}=\psi_{(x,y)}
. Since convergence of pseudopaths is convergence in Lebesgue measure, we have

(B.44)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}1_{\{d[(x_{n},y_{n}),(x,y)]>\delta\}}{\rm e}^{-t}{\rm d}t=0

and, consequently,

(B.45)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}1_{\{d[x_{n},x]>\delta\}}{\rm e}^{-t}{\rm d}t=0.

Therefore
\lim_{n\to\infty}\psi_{x_{n}}=\psi_{x}
. Suppose that
f\in\mathcal{C}_{b}(\Psi(E))
, so
f
is bounded continuous function on the space of pseudopaths on
[0,\infty]\times E
. Define the function
\tilde{f}
on the space of pseudopaths on
[0,\infty]\times E^{2}
, i.e.,
\tilde{f}
is a function on
\Psi(E^{2})
, by

(B.46)

\tilde{f}(\psi_{(x,y)})=f(\psi_{x}).

Then
\tilde{f}\in\mathcal{C}_{b}(\Psi(E^{2}))
and

(B.47)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\tilde{f}(\psi_{(x_{n},y_{n})})=\lim_{n\to\infty}f(\psi_{x_{n}})=f(\psi_{x})=\tilde{f}(\psi_{(x_{n},y_{n})}).

Hence
\tilde{f}
is indeed a continuous function on
\Psi(E^{2})
. Moreover, since
f
is bounded, it follows that
\tilde{f}
is bounded and we conclude that
\tilde{f}\in\mathcal{C}_{b}(\Psi(E^{2}))
.

Therefore, if
X_{n},Y_{n}
are continuous-time stochastic processes on
E
and

(B.48)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{L}\left[(X_{n}(s),Y_{n}(s))_{s>0}\right]=\mathcal{L}\left[(X(s),Y(s))_{s>0}\right]\,\text{ in Meyer Zheng topology},

then for all
f\in\mathcal{C}_{b}(\Psi(E^{2}))
we have

(B.49)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[f(\psi_{(X_{n},Y_{n})})]=\mathbb{E}[f(\psi_{(X,Y)})].

Since for each
f\in\mathcal{C}_{b}(\Psi(E))
we can construct a function
\tilde{f}\in\mathcal{C}_{b}(\Psi(E^{2}))
as in (
B.46
), we obtain for all
f\in\mathcal{C}_{b}(\Psi(E))
that

(B.50)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[f(\psi_{(X_{n})})]=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[\tilde{f}(\psi_{(X_{n},Y_{n})})]=\mathbb{E}[\tilde{f}(\psi_{(X,Y)})]=\mathbb{E}[\tilde{f}(\psi_{X})].

We conclude that

(B.51)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{L}[(X_{n}(s))]=\mathcal{L}[(X(s))_{s>0}]\,\text{ in Meyer-Zheng topology}

and, similarly,

(B.52)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{L}[(Y_{n}(s))]=\mathcal{L}[(Y(s))_{s>0}]\,\text{ in Meyer-Zheng topology.}
